{\mathcal{C}}^{(k+1)}
, and instead add a single cluster
C^{*}
, whose vertex set is
V(C^{\prime})\cup V(C_{1})\cup\cdots\cup V(C_{q})
, and edge set is the union of
E(C^{\prime})\cup E(C_{1})\cup\cdots\cup E(C_{q})
with the set
\bigcup_{a=1}^{q}S(v(C^{\prime}),v(C_{a}))
of edges.
Note that
C^{*}\subseteq\hat{H}^{(k+1)}
holds. We then delete vertices
v(C^{\prime}),v(C_{1}),\ldots,v(C_{q})
from graph
J
.

Observe that, for every vertex
x\in V(C^{*})
, the level of
x
in
{\mathcal{C}}^{(k)}
was either
s
or
s^{\prime}\leq s
. Moreover, if
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{(k)}
is the cluster that contained
x
, then
|V(C^{*})|>n^{\delta}\cdot|V(C)|
. Therefore, the level of
x
in
{\mathcal{C}}^{(k+1)}
is strictly greater than that in
{\mathcal{C}}^{(k)}
. We conclude that every vertex in
V(C^{*})
is promoted in the current phase, and the level of cluster
C^{*}
is at least
(s+1)
. We use the following simple observation, that will allow us to establish Invariant
I2
.

Observation 4.8
.

For every pair
x,y\in V(C^{*})
of vertices,
\mbox{\sf dist}_{C^{*}}(x,y)\leq 4^{s+1}
.

Proof.

If both
x
and
y
belong to a single cluster of
\left\{C^{\prime},C_{1},\ldots,C_{q}\right\}
, then, since Invariant
I2
held for
{\mathcal{C}}^{(k)}
, and since each such cluster is contained in
C^{*}
,
\mbox{\sf dist}_{C^{*}}(x,y)\leq 4^{s}
must hold. Assume now that
x
and
y
belong to different clusters. We assume w.l.o.g. that
x\in V(C_{1})
and
y\in V(C_{2})
; the other cases are treated similarly.

From the definition of cluster
C^{*}
, meta-edges
\hat{e}_{1}=(v(C^{\prime}),v(C_{1}))
,
\hat{e}_{2}=(v(C^{\prime}),v(C_{2}))
lie in
J
. Consider any real edge
e_{1}\in S(\hat{e}_{1})
and
e_{2}\in S(\hat{e}_{2})
. Both edges must lie in
\hat{H}^{(k+1)}
, and in
C^{*}
. We denote
e_{1}=(x_{1},y_{1})
with
x_{1}\in V(C_{1})
, and
e_{2}=(x_{2},y_{2})
with
y_{2}\in V(C_{2})
. In particular,
y_{1},x_{2}\in V(C^{\prime})
must hold. From Invariant
I2
, there is a path
P_{1}
of length at most
4^{s}
connecting
x
to
x_{1}
in
C_{1}
; a path
P^{\prime}
of length at most
4^{s^{\prime}}\leq 4^{s}
connecting
y_{1}
to
x_{2}
in
C^{\prime}
; and a path
P_{3}
of length at most
4^{s}
connecting
y_{2}
to
y
in
C_{2}
. By combining these three paths with edges
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
, we obtain a path in cluster
C^{*}
, connecting
x
to
y
, whose length is at most
3\cdot 4^{s}+1\leq 4^{s+1}
. Therefore,
\mbox{\sf dist}_{C^{*}}(x,y)\leq 4^{s+1}
.

The algorithm terminates once, every supernode
v(C^{\prime})
of
J
with
C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{(k)}_{s^{\prime}}
, the number of meta-edges incident to
v(C^{\prime})
in the current graph
J
is less than
n^{\delta}
.

From the above discussion, once the algorithm terminates, Invariant
I2
holds for the final clustering
{\mathcal{C}}^{(k+1)}
, as every newly added cluster to
{\mathcal{C}}^{(k+1)}
belongs to level
(s+1)
or higher. It is immediate to verify that Invariant
I1
holds for the final set
{\mathcal{C}}^{(k+1)}
of clusters. It now only remains to prove that sufficiently many vertices are promoted in the current iteration.

Consider the graph
J
that is obtained at the end of the algorithm. In this graph, for every cluster
C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{(k)}_{s^{\prime}}
, its corresponding supernode
v(C^{\prime})
has fewer than
n^{\delta}
meta-edges incident to
v(C^{\prime})
. Since, for every meta-edge
\hat{e}\in E(J)
that is incident to a supernode
v(C)
,
w(\hat{e})\leq|V(C)|
must hold, we get that the total weight of all edges remaining in graph
J
at the end of the algorithm is bounded by:

\sum_{C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{(k)}_{s^{\prime}}}|V(C^{\prime})|\cdot n^{\delta}\leq n^{1+\delta}.

Recall that the total weight of all meta-edges of
J
at the beginning of the algorithm was at least:

\frac{n^{1-\delta}\cdot\delta^{2}}{512}\cdot|I^{\prime\prime}_{k}|\geq\frac{n^{1+3\delta}\cdot\delta^{4}}{2^{12}}>2n^{1+\delta},

since
|I^{\prime\prime}_{k}|\geq\frac{\delta^{2}}{6}\cdot\left\lceil n^{4\delta}\right\rceil
and
n^{\delta}\geq 2^{14}/\delta^{2}
.

Therefore, the total weight of the meta-edges that remain at the end of the algorithm in
J
is less than half the original total weight. In other words, the total weight of all meta-edges that were deleted from
J
is at least
\frac{n^{1-\delta}\cdot\delta^{2}}{1024}\cdot|I^{\prime\prime}_{k}|
. Each of the deleted meta-edges is incident to some supernode
v(C)
, with
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{(k)}_{s}
that was deleted from
J
. Recall that every vertex of such a cluster
C
is promoted in the current phase.

Consider now some supernode
v(C)
that was deleted from
J
in the current phase. The total weight of all meta-edges incident to
v(C)
in the original graph
J
was at most
|V(C)|\cdot|I^{\prime\prime}_{k}|
, and each of the vertices of
C
was promoted in phase
k
. Therefore, if we denote by
U
is the set of all vertices of
V
that were promoted in phase
k
, then the total weight of all meta-edges that were deleted from
J
is at most
|U|\cdot|I^{\prime\prime}_{k}|
. Since, as shown above, the total weight of all such edges is at least
\frac{n^{1-\delta}\cdot\delta^{2}}{1024}\cdot|I^{\prime\prime}_{k}|
, and since we have assumed that
n^{\delta}\geq 2^{14}/\delta^{2}
,
we get that
|U|\geq\frac{n^{1-\delta}\cdot\delta^{2}}{1024}\geq n^{1-2\delta}
.

This concludes the proof of
Theorem

4.2
.
We obtain the following immediate corollary of the theorem.

Corollary 4.9
.

Consider a
Distanced Matching Game
with parameters
n>0,0<\delta<1/4
and
d
, such that
d\geq 2^{4/\delta}
,
n^{\delta}\geq\frac{2^{14}\log n}{\delta^{2}}
. Let
G
be the graph that is obtained at the end of the game. Then
|E(G)|\leq n^{1+8\delta}
holds, and every vertex of
G
has degree at most
n^{8\delta}
.

The corollary follows from the fact that the set
E(G)
of edges is partitioned into at most
n^{8\delta}
matchings – the responses of the matching player in the game.

5
Hierarchical Support Structure

A Hierarchical Support Structure uses two parameters, an integer
N>0
, and another parameter
0<\epsilon\leq 1/4
. Throughout, we denote
\epsilon^{\prime}=\epsilon^{4}
. For an integer
j\geq 1
, we also let
\eta_{j}=N^{6+256j\epsilon^{2}}
and
\tilde{d}_{j}=2^{cj/\epsilon^{4}}
, where
c
is a sufficiently large constant.

For all
1\leq j\leq\left\lceil 1/\epsilon\right\rceil
, we define a
level
-
j
Hierarchical Support Structure for a graph containing
N^{j}
vertices. The definition of the support structure is recursive.

Level-
1
Hierarchical Support Structure.

Given a graph
H
with
|V(H)|=N
, level-
1
Hierarchical Support Structure for
H
consists of a subset
S(H)
of vertices of
H
, such that
|V(H)\setminus S(H)|\leq N^{1-\epsilon^{4}}
.

Level-
j
Hierarchical Support Structure.

Consider now some integer
1<j\leq\left\lceil 1/\epsilon\right\rceil
. Let
H
be a graph with
|V(H)|=N^{j}
. A
level-
j
Hierarchical Support Structure
for graph
H
consists of the following:

•

a collection
{\mathcal{H}}=\left\{H_{1},\ldots,H_{r}\right\}
of
r=N-\left\lceil 2N^{1-\epsilon^{4}}\right\rceil
graphs, such that all vertices in sets
V(H_{1}),V(H_{2}),\ldots,V(H_{r})
are mutually disjoint, and additionally, for all
1\leq i\leq r
:

V(H_{i})\subseteq V(H)
;
|V(H_{i})|=N^{j-1}
; and
|E(H_{i})|\leq N^{j-1+32\epsilon^{2}}
hold;

•

an embedding of the graph
H^{\prime}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{r}H_{i}
into graph
H
via paths of length at most
2^{64/\epsilon^{4}}
, that causes congestion at most
N^{128\epsilon^{2}}
;

•

for all
1\leq i\leq r
, a level-
(j-1)
Hierarchical Support Structure for graph
H_{i}
; and

•

a set
S(H)\subseteq V(H)
of vertices, where
S(H)=\bigcup_{H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}}S(H_{i})
, and, for all
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}
,
S(H_{i})
is the set of vertices that is given as part of the level-
(j-1)
Hierarchical Support Structure for
H_{i}
.

Additionally, we require every graph
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}
is
(\eta_{j-1},\tilde{d}_{j-1})
-well-connected with respect to the set
S(H_{i})
of vertices.
We say that
{\mathcal{H}}
is the set of graphs associated with the level-
j
Hierarchical Support Structure for graph
H
.

This completes the definition of a Hierarchical Support Structure. We will need to use the following simple claim.

Claim 5.1
.

Let
1\leq j\leq\left\lceil 1/\epsilon\right\rceil
be an integer, and let
H
be a graph with
|V(H)|=N^{j}
, together with a level-
j
Hierarchical Support Structure. Then
|V(H)\setminus S(H)|\leq|V(H)|\cdot\frac{4j}{N^{\epsilon^{4}}}
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on
j
. When
j=1
, then
|V(H)|=N
, and, from the definition of level-
1
Hierarchical Support Structure,
|V(H)\setminus S(H)|\leq N^{1-\epsilon^{4}}=\frac{|V(H)|}{N^{\epsilon^{4}}}\leq|V(H)|\cdot\frac{4j}{N^{\epsilon^{4}}}
.

Consider now some integer
j>1
, and assume that the claim holds for
j-1
. Let
H
be a graph with
|V(H)|=N^{j}
, for which a level-
j
Hierarchical Support Structure is given, and let
{\mathcal{H}}
be the collection of graphs associated with the structure. Let
V_{1}=\bigcup_{H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}}V(H_{i})
and
V_{2}=V(H)\setminus V_{1}
. From the definition of level-
j
Hierarchical Support Structure,
|{\mathcal{H}}|=N-\left\lceil 2N^{1-\epsilon^{4}}\right\rceil\geq N-4N^{1-\epsilon^{4}}
. Therefore,
|V_{1}|\geq N^{j}-\frac{4N^{j}}{N^{\epsilon^{4}}}
, and
V_{2}\leq\frac{4N^{j}}{N^{\epsilon^{4}}}=\frac{4|V(H)|}{N^{\epsilon^{4}}}
.

Let
V^{\prime}_{1}=\bigcup_{H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}}S(H_{i})
and
V^{\prime\prime}_{1}=V_{1}\setminus V^{\prime}_{1}
. Clearly,
V(H)\setminus S(H)=V^{\prime\prime}_{1}\cup V_{2}
. We now bound
|V^{\prime\prime}_{1}|
.

Since, for every graph
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}
, by the induction hypothesis,
|V(H_{i})\setminus S(H_{i})|\leq|V(H_{i})|\cdot\frac{4(j-1)}{N^{\epsilon^{4}}}=\frac{4(j-1)\cdot N^{j-1}}{N^{\epsilon^{4}}}
, and since
|{\mathcal{H}}|<N
, we get that
|V^{\prime\prime}_{1}|\leq\frac{4(j-1)\cdot N^{j}}{N^{\epsilon^{4}}}=\frac{4(j-1)\cdot|V(H)|}{N^{\epsilon^{4}}}
.

Altogether, we get that:

|V(H)\setminus S(H)|=|V_{1}^{\prime\prime}|+|V_{2}|\leq\frac{4(j-1)\cdot|V(H)|}{N^{\epsilon^{4}}}+\frac{4|V(H)|}{N^{\epsilon^{4}}}=\frac{4j\cdot|V(H)|}{N^{\epsilon^{4}}}

The following theorem provides an algorithm for the Distancing Player in the Distanced Matching game.

Theorem 5.2
.

There is a large enough constant
c
, and a deterministic algorithm, whose input consists of a parameter
0<\epsilon<1/4
, a pair
N
,
1\leq j\leq\left\lceil 1/\epsilon\right\rceil
of integers, and a graph
H
with
|V(H)|=N^{j}
, such that
N
is sufficiently large, so that
\frac{N^{\epsilon^{4}}}{\log N}\geq 2^{128/\epsilon^{5}}
holds. The algorithm computes one of the following:

•

either a
(\delta_{j},d)
-distancing
(A,B,E^{\prime})
in graph
H
, where
\delta_{j}=4j\epsilon^{4}
,
d=2^{32/\epsilon^{4}}
and
|E^{\prime}|\leq\frac{|A|}{N^{j\epsilon^{4}}}
; or

•

a level-
j
Hierarchical Support Structure for
H
, such that graph
H
is
(\eta_{j},\tilde{d}_{j})
-well-connected with respect to the set
S(H)
of vertices defined by the support structure, where
\eta_{j}=N^{6+256j\epsilon^{2}}
and
\tilde{d}_{j}=2^{cj/\epsilon^{4}}
.

The running time of the algorithm is bounded by:

cj\cdot N^{j(1+64\epsilon^{2})+7}+c|E(H)|\cdot N^{6}.

We prove
Theorem

5.2
in
Section

6
. Note that
Theorem

2.2
follows from the theorem directly, by setting
j=1/\epsilon
.

The following immediate corollary of the theorem can be used in order to either embed a large graph
H
into an input graph
G
, and construct a Hierarchical Support Structure for
H
, so that graph
H
is well-connected with respect to the resulting set
S(H)
of vertices given by the Hierarchical Support Structure; or to compute a distancing in graph
G
. The latter can in turn be used in order to compute a sparse cut in
G
, via
Lemma

4.1
. We state the corollary in a slightly more general form that will be helpful for us later: we assume that, together with graph
G
, we are given a subset
T
of its vertices called terminals, and that we are interested in embedding a large graph
H
into
G
with
V(H)\subseteq T
.

Corollary 5.3
.

There is a deterministic algorithm, whose input consists of an
n
-vertex graph
G
, a set
T
of
k
vertices of
G
called terminals, and parameters
\frac{2}{(\log k)^{1/12}}<\epsilon<\frac{1}{400}
,
d>1
and
\eta>1
, such that
1/\epsilon
is an integer. The algorithm computes one of the following:

•

either a pair
T_{1},T_{2}\subseteq T
of disjoint subsets of terminals, and a set
E^{\prime}
of edges of
G
, such that:

–

|T_{1}|=|T_{2}|
and
|T_{1}|\geq\frac{k^{1-4\epsilon^{3}}}{4}
;

–

|E^{\prime}|\leq\frac{d\cdot|T_{1}|}{\eta}
; and

–

for every pair
t\in T_{1},t^{\prime}\in T_{2}
of terminals,
\mbox{\sf dist}_{G\setminus E^{\prime}}(t,t^{\prime})>d
;

•

or a graph
H
with
V(H)\subseteq T
,
|V(H)|=N^{1/\epsilon}\geq k-k^{1-\epsilon/2}
, where
N=\left\lfloor k^{\epsilon}\right\rfloor
, and maximum vertex degree at most
k^{32\epsilon^{3}}
, together with an embedding
{\mathcal{P}}
of
H
into
G
via paths of length at most
d
that cause congestion at most
\eta\cdot k^{32\epsilon^{3}}
, and a level-
(1/\epsilon)
Hierarchical Support Structure for
H
, such that
H
is
(\eta^{\prime},\tilde{d})
-well-connected with respect to the set
S(H)
of vertices defined by the support structure, where
\eta^{\prime}=N^{6+256\epsilon}
, and
\tilde{d}=2^{c/\epsilon^{5}}
, with
c
being the constant used in the definition of the Hierarchical Support Structure.

The running time of the algorithm is
O\left(k^{1+O(\epsilon)}+|E(G)|\cdot k^{O(\epsilon^{3})}\cdot(\eta+d\log n)\right)
.

Proof.

Let
q=1/\epsilon
, let
N=\left\lfloor k^{\epsilon}\right\rfloor
, and let
T^{\prime}\subseteq T
be any subset of
N^{q}
terminals.
Observe that:

N^{q}=\left\lfloor k^{\epsilon}\right\rfloor^{1/\epsilon}\geq\left(k^{\epsilon}-1\right)^{1/\epsilon}=k\cdot\left(1-\frac{1}{k^{\epsilon}}\right)^{1/\epsilon}\geq k\cdot\left(1-\frac{1}{k^{\epsilon}\cdot\epsilon}\right)\geq k-\frac{k^{1-\epsilon}}{\epsilon}\geq k-k^{1-\epsilon/2}

(3)

(we have used the fact that for all
0<\delta<1
and
a>2
,
(1-\delta)^{a}\geq 1-\delta a
).

We will attempt to construct a graph
H
with
V(H)=T^{\prime}
, together with an embedding
{\mathcal{P}}
of
H
into
G
with congestion at most
\eta\cdot k^{32\epsilon^{3}}
and path lengths at most
d
, and a level-
q
Hierarchical Support Structure for
H
, such that
H
is
(\eta^{\prime},\tilde{d})
-well-connected with respect to the set
S(H)
of vertices defined by the support structure. If we fail to construct such a graph
H
, we will compute the sets
T_{1},T_{2}\subseteq T
of terminals and the set
E^{\prime}\subseteq E(G)
of edges as required.

We will employ the distanced-matching game on graph
H
with parameters
n=|T^{\prime}|
,
\delta=4\epsilon^{3}
, and distance parameter
d^{\prime}=2^{32/\epsilon^{4}}
.

We will use the algorithm from

Theorem

5.2
for the distancing player, with parameter
j=q
, and parameters
N
and
\epsilon
remaining unchanged. In order to be able to use the algorithm, we need to verify that

\frac{N^{\epsilon^{4}}}{\log N}\geq 2^{128/\epsilon^{5}}
holds.
Recall that, from the conditions of
Corollary

5.3
,
\frac{2}{(\log k)^{1/12}}<\epsilon<\frac{1}{400}
. Therefore,
\log k>(2/\epsilon)^{12}

and
k>2^{(2/\epsilon)^{12}}
. Moreover, from the above calculations,
\frac{\log k}{\log\log k}>\frac{1}{\epsilon^{8}}
holds, and so
k^{\epsilon^{8}}>\log k
must hold.
Altogether, we get that:

\frac{N^{\epsilon^{4}}}{\log N}\geq\frac{k^{\epsilon^{5}}}{2\cdot\log k}\geq k^{\epsilon^{6}}\geq 2^{128/\epsilon^{5}}.

(4)

We will bound the number of iterations of the Distanced Matching game via
Theorem

4.2
. In order to use
Theorem

4.2
, we need to verify that
\frac{|T^{\prime}|^{\delta}}{\log(|T^{\prime}|)}\geq\frac{2^{14}}{\delta^{2}}
, or, equivalently:
\frac{|T^{\prime}|^{4\epsilon^{3}}}{\log(|T^{\prime}|)}\geq\frac{2^{10}}{\epsilon^{6}}
.
Recall that
|T^{\prime}|=N^{q}=N^{1/\epsilon}
, and so

\frac{|T^{\prime}|^{4\epsilon^{3}}}{\log(|T^{\prime}|)}=\frac{\epsilon\cdot N^{4\epsilon^{2}}}{\log N}\geq\frac{N^{\epsilon^{4}}}{\log N}\geq 2^{128/\epsilon^{5}}\geq\frac{2^{10}}{\epsilon^{6}}
from Equation
4
, and since
\epsilon\leq 1/400
. We also need to verify that
d^{\prime}\geq 2^{4/\delta}
. Since
d^{\prime}=2^{32/\epsilon^{4}}
and
\delta=4\epsilon^{3}
, this is immediate to verify.
From
Theorem

4.2
, we can now conclude that the number of iterations in a Distanced Matching game with parameters
n=|T^{\prime}|
,
\delta=4\epsilon^{3}
, and distance parameter
d^{\prime}=2^{32/\epsilon^{4}}
is bounded by
|T^{\prime}|^{8\delta}\leq k^{32\epsilon^{3}}
.

We start with a graph
H
whose vertex set is
V(H)=T^{\prime}
, and edge set is
E(H)=\emptyset
, and then iterate. In each iteration
i
, we will add some set
E_{i}
of edges to graph
H
, and we will define an embedding
P(e)
for every edge
e\in E_{i}
. We now describe the execution of a single iteration.

Execution of Iteration
i
.

We apply the algorithm from
Theorem

5.2
to the current graph
H
, with parameter
j=q
, and parameters
N,\epsilon
remaining unchanged. Note that
|V(H)|=|T^{\prime}|=N^{q}
, and, as we have established above,
\frac{N^{\epsilon^{4}}}{\log N}\geq 2^{128/\epsilon^{5}}
holds.

We now consider two cases. The first case is when the algorithm from
Theorem

5.2
returns a
(\delta_{q},d^{\prime})
-distancing
(X_{i},Y_{i},E^{\prime}_{i})
in graph
H
, where
\delta_{q}=4j\epsilon^{4}=4q\epsilon^{4}=4\epsilon^{3}=\delta
(since
q=1/\epsilon
), and
d^{\prime}=2^{32/\epsilon^{4}}
. In this case, we say that iteration
i
is
regular
. We view this distancing as the response of the distancing player in iteration
i
of the Distanced Matching game that we play on graph
H
.

We then apply Procedure
\mathsf{ProcPathPeel}
from
Lemma

3.12
to graph
G
and sets
A_{1}=X_{i},B_{1}=Y_{i}
of its vertices, together with parameters
d
and
\eta
from the statement of
Corollary

5.3
. Let
{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}
denote the collection of paths that the algorithm returns.
Recall that every path in
{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}
connects some vertex of
X_{i}
to a vertex of
Y_{i}
, and that every vertex of
X_{i}\cup Y_{i}
may serve as an endpoint of at most one such path. We let
M_{i}\subseteq X_{i}\times Y_{i}
be the matching that is defined by the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}
: a pair
(x,y)
of vertices with
x\in X_{i},y\in Y_{i}
is added to
M_{i}
iff some path
Q(x,y)\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}
has endpoints
x,y
.
We again consider two cases. The first case happens if
|M_{i}|\geq|X_{i}|/2
. In this case, we say that iteration
i
is successful. We obtain a collection
E_{i}\subseteq M_{i}
of edges as follows: we start with
E_{i}=M_{i}
, and we delete from
E_{i}
all pairs
(x,y)
of vertices where edge
(x,y)
lies in
E^{\prime}_{i}
. Since, from the definition of distancing,
|E^{\prime}_{i}|\leq|X_{i}|/16
, we get that
|E_{i}|\geq|X_{i}|/4
. We let
{\mathcal{P}}_{i}=\left\{Q(x,y)\mid(x,y)\in E_{i}\right\}
be the collection of paths that route the pairs of vertices in
E_{i}
. We add the edges of
E_{i}
to graph
H
, and we view
E_{i}
as the response of the matching player in iteration
i
. We also view the set
{\mathcal{P}}_{i}
of paths in graph
H
as an embedding of the set
E_{i}
of edges. Recall that each path in
{\mathcal{P}}_{i}
has length at most
d
, and the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}_{i}
cause congestion at most
\eta
. We then continue to the next iteration.

The second case happens if
|M_{i}|<|X_{i}|/2
. In this case we say that iteration
i
is unsuccessful. Let
E^{\prime}
be the set of all edges
e
in graph
G
that participate in exactly
\eta
paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}
. Let
X^{\prime}\subseteq X_{i}
and
Y^{\prime}\subseteq Y_{i}
be the sets of vertices that do not serve as endpoints of the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}
. Recall that
Lemma

3.12
guarantees (via Property
P3
) that
the length of the shortest path connecting a vertex of
X^{\prime}
to a vertex of
Y^{\prime}
in
G\setminus E^{\prime}
is greater than
d
.

Recall that
|X^{\prime}|=|Y^{\prime}|\geq\frac{|X_{i}|}{2}\geq\frac{|T^{\prime}|^{1-\delta}}{2}\geq\frac{k^{1-\delta}}{4}\geq\frac{k^{1-4\epsilon^{3}}}{4}
. Since every path in
{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}
has length at most
d
, we get that
\sum_{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}}|E(Q)|\leq d\cdot\frac{|X_{i}|}{2}\leq d\cdot|X^{\prime}|
. Since set
E^{\prime}
contains edges that participate in
\eta
paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}
, we get that:
|E^{\prime}|\leq\frac{d\cdot|X^{\prime}|}{\eta}
. We return the set
E^{\prime}
of edges and the sets
T_{1}=X^{\prime}
,
T_{2}=Y^{\prime}
of terminals. From the above discussion,
|T_{1}|=|T_{2}|
,
|T_{1}|\geq\frac{k^{1-4\epsilon^{3}}}{4}
, and
|E^{\prime}|\leq\frac{d\cdot|T_{1}|}{\eta}
hold as required. Moreover, for every pair
t\in T_{1},t^{\prime}\in T_{2}
of terminals,
\mbox{\sf dist}_{G\setminus E^{\prime}}(t,t^{\prime})>d
.

It remains to consider the second case, when the algorithm from
Theorem

5.2
constructs a level-
q
Hierarchical Support Structure for
H
, such that graph
H
is
(\eta_{q},\tilde{d}_{q})
-well-connected with respect to the set
S(H)
of vertices defined by the support structure, where
\tilde{d}_{q}=2^{cq/\epsilon^{4}}=2^{c/\epsilon^{5}}=\tilde{d}
, and:

\eta_{q}=N^{6+256q\epsilon^{2}}=N^{6+256\epsilon}=\eta^{\prime}.

In this case, we say that iteration
i
is irregular.
Recall that
|V(H)|=|T^{\prime}|=N^{q}\geq k-k^{1-\epsilon/2}
from Inequality
3
. As observed already, the number of iterations in the Distanced Matching game is bounded by
k^{32\epsilon^{3}}
, and so the maximum vertex degree in
H
is bounded by
k^{32\epsilon^{3}}
, and the number of edges in graph
H
is bounded by
k^{1+32\epsilon^{3}}
throughout the algorithm. If we denote by
z\leq k^{32\epsilon^{3}}
the number of iterations in the Distanced Matching game, then for all
1\leq i\leq z
, we have constructed a set
{\mathcal{P}}_{i}
of paths in graph
H
embedding the edges of
E_{i}
. The paths in
{\mathcal{P}}_{i}
have length at most
d
each, and they cause congestion at most
\eta
in
G
. By letting
{\mathcal{P}}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{z}{\mathcal{P}}_{i}
, we obtain an embedding of graph
H
into
G
via paths of length at most
d
, that cause congestion at most
\eta\cdot z\leq\eta\cdot k^{32\epsilon^{3}}
. We output graph
H
, its embedding
{\mathcal{P}}
, and the level-
q
Hierarchical Support Structure for
H
.

This completes the description of a single iteration. It now remains to bound the running time of the algorithm.

Running Time Analysis

Recall that, throughout the algorithm,
|E(H)|\leq k^{1+32\epsilon^{3}}
holds, and that the number of regular successful iterations in the algorithm is at most
k^{32\epsilon^{3}}
. Additionally, there could be at most one irregular iteration, and at most one regular but unsuccessful iteration.

We now bound the running time of a single iteration. This running time is dominated by the running times of the algorithms from
Theorem

5.2
and
Lemma

3.12
.

The former is bounded by:

O(q\cdot N^{q(1+64\epsilon^{2})+7}+|E(H)|\cdot N^{6})\leq O\left(k^{1+O(\epsilon)}+k^{1+32\epsilon^{3}}\cdot k^{O(\epsilon)}\right)\leq O\left(k^{1+O(\epsilon)}\right).

The latter is bounded by:

O(|E(G)|(\eta+d\log n))
.

The total running time of the algorithm is then bounded by:

O\left(k^{1+O(\epsilon)}+|E(G)|\cdot k^{O(\epsilon^{3})}\cdot(\eta+d\log n)\right).

6
Algorithm for the Distancing Player – Proof of
Theorem

5.2

This section is dedicated to proving
Theorem

5.2
.
Throughout the proof we use the following three parameters:
\epsilon^{\prime}=\epsilon^{4}
;
\Delta=64/\epsilon^{\prime}
; and
d^{\prime}=2d\cdot\Delta
. Note that:

d^{\prime}=2\Delta d=\frac{128d}{\epsilon^{\prime}}=\frac{128\cdot 2^{32/\epsilon^{4}}}{\epsilon^{4}}\leq 2^{64/\epsilon^{4}}<\frac{1}{4}\cdot 2^{c/\epsilon^{4}},

(5)

since
c
is large enough.

The proof is by induction on
j
.
We start with the base case, where
j=1
.

Base Case:
j=1

We assume that we are given a graph
H
on
N
vertices. Our goal is to either compute a
(\delta_{1},d)
-distancing in graph
H
, or to construct a set
S(H)\subseteq V(H)
of vertices, such that
|V(H)\setminus S(H)|\leq N^{1-\epsilon^{4}}
, and graph
H
is
(\eta_{1},\tilde{d}_{1})
-well-connected with respect to
S(H)
.

We apply Algorithm
\mathsf{ProcSeparate}
from
Lemma

3.10
to graph
H
, with the set
T=V(H)
of terminal vertices, with distance parameters
d
and
\Delta
remaining the same, and parameter
\alpha=1-\frac{1}{N^{\epsilon^{\prime}}}
.

Assume first that the outcome of the algorithm is a pair
T_{1},T_{2}\subseteq V(H)
of subsets of vertices, with
|T_{1}|=|T_{2}|
, such that for every pair
t\in T_{1}
,
t^{\prime}\in T_{2}
of vertices,
\mbox{\sf dist}_{H}(t,t^{\prime})\geq d
, and additionally:

|T_{1}|\geq N^{1-64/\Delta}\cdot\min\left\{(1-\alpha),\frac{1}{3}\right\}\geq N^{1-2\epsilon^{\prime}}.

(recall that
\Delta=64/\epsilon^{\prime}
and
N^{\epsilon^{\prime}}>3
).
In this case, since
\delta_{1}=4\epsilon^{\prime}
, we obtain a
(\delta_{1},d)
-distancing
(T_{1},T_{2},\emptyset)
in graph
H
. We return this distancing as the outcome of the algorithm.

Otherwise, Procedure
\mathsf{ProcSeparate}
must return a vertex
v\in V(H)
, with
|B_{H}(v,\Delta\cdot d)|>\alpha\cdot N=N\cdot\left(1-\frac{1}{N^{\epsilon^{\prime}}}\right)
.
In this case, we set
S(H)=B_{H}(v,\Delta\cdot d)
, and we report that graph
H
is
(\eta_{1},\tilde{d}_{1})
-well-connected with respect to
S(H)
. We also return
S(H)
as level-
1
Hierarchical Support Structure for
H
. Note that
|V(H)\setminus S(H)|\leq N^{1-\epsilon^{\prime}}=N^{1-\epsilon^{4}}
as required. It remains to show that graph
H
is indeed
(\eta_{1},\tilde{d}_{1})
-well-connected with respect to
S(H)
. Recall that
\eta_{1}>N
and
d^{\prime}<\tilde{d}_{1}
from Inequality
5
, since
\tilde{d}_{1}=2^{c/\epsilon^{4}}
. Let
A,B\subseteq S(H)
be any pair of equal-cardinality subsets of vertices. We define an arbitrary perfect matching
M\subseteq A\times B
between vertices of
A
and vertices of
B
. Consider now any pair
(a,b)\in M
of matched vertices. Since
a,b\in B_{H}(v,\Delta\cdot d)
, there is a path
P(a,b)
connecting
a
to
b
in
H
of length at most
2\Delta\cdot d=d^{\prime}\leq\tilde{d}_{1}
. We then let
{\mathcal{P}}(A,B)=\left\{P(a,b)\mid(a,b)\in M\right\}
be the resulting collection of paths, that routes every vertex of
A
to a distinct vertex of
B
. The length of every path in
{\mathcal{P}}(A,B)
is at most
\tilde{d}_{1}
, and the congestion caused by the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}(A,B)
is at most
|{\mathcal{P}}(A,B)|\leq|V(H)|\leq N<\eta_{1}
.

The running time of the algorithm is dominated by the running time of Procedure
\mathsf{ProcSeparate}
, which is bounded by
O(|E(H)|\cdot N^{64/\Delta})\leq O(|E(H)|\cdot N^{\epsilon^{\prime}})<c|E(H)|\cdot N^{6}
, if
c
is large enough, since
\Delta=64/\epsilon^{\prime}
.

Step:
1<j\leq h

We now assume that we are given some integer
1<j\leq\left\lceil 1/\epsilon\right\rceil
, such that the statement of
Theorem

5.2
holds for
j-1
, and we prove the statement of the theorem for
j
. Recall that we are given as input a graph
H
with
|V(H)|=N^{j}
.

We partition the set
V(H)
of vertices into
N
subsets
V_{1},\ldots,V_{N}
, each of which contains exactly
N^{j-1}
vertices. The algorithm consists of two phases. In the first phase, we run the
Distanced Matching Game
in parallel on
N
graphs
H_{1},\ldots,H_{N}
, where for all
1\leq i\leq N
,
V(H_{i})=V_{i}
. We will add edges to graphs
H_{1},\ldots,H_{N}
gradually via the
Distanced Matching Game
, while computing an embedding of all resulting edges into graph
H
, so that the resulting embedding paths have sufficiently low length and cause sufficiently low congestion. In this phase, we will either compute the required
(\delta_{j},d)
-distancing in
H
, or we will be able to successfully complete the
Distanced Matching Game
on a sufficiently large collection
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}\subseteq\left\{H_{1},\ldots,H_{N}\right\}
of the graphs. In the latter case, for each such graph
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
, we will also compute a level-
(j-1)
Hierarchical Support Structure for
H_{i}
. If the outcome of the first phase is a
(\delta_{j},d)
-distancing for
H
, then we terminate the algorithm and return this distancing. Otherwise, we continue to the second phase. In the second phase we will exploit the graphs in
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
to either compute a
(\delta_{j},d)
-distancing in graph
H
, or to compute a
subset
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
of
r
graphs, so that, if we let
S(H)=\bigcup_{H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime\prime}}S(H_{i})
, then graph
H
is
(\delta_{j},\tilde{d}_{j})
-well-connected with respect to the set
S(H)
of vertices.
We now describe each of the two phases in turn.

6.1
Phase 1: Construction of Smaller Well-Connected Graphs

In this phase, we gradually construct a collection
{\mathcal{H}}=\left\{H_{1},\ldots,H_{N}\right\}
of graphs, over the course of at most
N^{64\epsilon^{2}}
iterations, by running the
Distanced Matching Game
over these graphs in parallel with parameters
\delta=\delta_{j-1}
and
d=2^{32/\epsilon^{4}}
. Initially, for all
1\leq i\leq N
, we let
V(H_{i})=V_{i}
and
E(H_{i})=\emptyset
. In every iteration
q\geq 1
, we will compute, for all
1\leq i\leq N
, a partial matching
E_{i}^{q}
over the vertices of
V_{i}
. We will ensure that either
E_{i}^{q}=\emptyset
, or
|E_{i}^{q}|\geq N^{(j-1)(1-\delta_{j-1})}/8
. The edges of
E_{i}^{q}
are then added to graph
H_{i}
. Additionally, in the
q
th iteration, we will compute an embedding
{\mathcal{P}}^{q}
of all edges in
\bigcup_{i=1}^{N}E_{i}^{q}
into
H
.
We now describe a single iteration
q
.

6.1.1
Description of Iteration
q

Using the induction hypothesis, we apply the algorithm from
Theorem

5.2
to each of the graphs
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}
, with parameter
(j-1)
. We denote by
{\mathcal{H}}^{1}\subseteq{\mathcal{H}}
the collection of all graphs
H_{i}
, for which the algorithm returned a
(\delta_{j-1},d)
-distancing in
H_{i}
, and we denote by
{\mathcal{H}}^{2}={\mathcal{H}}\setminus{\mathcal{H}}^{1}
all remaining graphs. Recall that, for each graph
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{2}
, the algorithm computes a level-
(j-1)
Hierarchical Support Structure. We now consider two cases, depending on whether
|{\mathcal{H}}^{1}|>N^{1-\epsilon^{\prime}}
.

Case 1:
|{\mathcal{H}}^{1}|>N^{1-\epsilon^{\prime}}
.

If this case happens, then we say that iteration
q
is
regular
. Recall that, for every graph
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}
, the algorithm from
Theorem

5.2
returned a
(\delta_{j-1},d)
- distancing
(A_{i},B_{i},E^{\prime}_{i})
, where
|A_{i}|=|B_{i}|\geq|V(H_{i})|^{1-\delta_{j-1}}=N^{(j-1)(1-\delta_{j-1})}
, and
|E^{\prime}_{i}|\leq\frac{|A_{i}|}{N^{(j-1)\epsilon^{\prime}}}
.

Let
z^{\prime}=\left\lceil\frac{2\log N}{\epsilon}\right\rceil
.
We further partition the collection
{\mathcal{H}}^{1}
of graphs into subsets
{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{0},\ldots,{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{z^{\prime}}
, where for all
0\leq z\leq z^{\prime}
, class
{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{z}
contains all graphs
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}
, for which
\frac{N^{j-1}}{2^{z+1}}\leq|A_{i}|<\frac{N^{j-1}}{2^{z}}
holds. Clearly, there must be an index
0\leq z\leq z^{\prime}
, such that:

|{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{z}|\geq\frac{|{\mathcal{H}}^{1}|\cdot\epsilon}{4\log N}\geq\frac{N^{1-\epsilon^{\prime}}\cdot\epsilon}{4\log N}\geq N^{1-2\epsilon^{\prime}}.

(6)

(since
\frac{N^{\epsilon^{\prime}}}{\log N}\geq 2^{128/\epsilon^{5}}
from the statement of
Theorem

5.2
).

From now on we fix this index
z
.
Since, for all
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}
,
|A_{i}|\geq N^{(j-1)(1-\delta_{j-1})}\geq N^{j-j\delta_{j-1}-1}
, we get that:

2^{z}\leq N^{j\delta_{j-1}}.

(7)

For convenience, we denote by
I\subseteq\left\{1,\ldots,N\right\}
the set of all indices
i
with
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{z}
.
Next, we apply Algorithm
\mathsf{ProcPathPeel}
from
Lemma

3.12
, to graph
H
, collections of subsets
\left\{(A_{i},B_{i})\right\}_{i\in I}
of its vertices, length parameter
d^{\prime}
, and congestion parameter
\eta=N^{8\epsilon^{3}}
. Consider the resulting collections
\left\{{\mathcal{P}}^{q}_{i}\right\}_{i\in I}
of paths. Recall that, from
Lemma

3.12
, for all
i\in I
, every path in the corresponding set
{\mathcal{P}}^{q}_{i}
has length at most
d^{\prime}
, and it connects a vertex of
A_{i}
to a vertex of
B_{i}
, so that the endpoints of all paths in
{\mathcal{P}}_{i}
are distinct. Therefore, we can use the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}^{q}_{i}
in order to define a partial matching
M_{i}^{q}
between vertices of
A_{i}
and vertices of
B_{i}
: a pair
(a,b)\in A_{i}\times B_{i}
of vertices is added to
M_{i}^{q}
iff some path of
{\mathcal{P}}^{q}_{i}
has endpoints
a,b
. We denote by
A^{\prime}_{i}\subseteq A_{i}
and
B^{\prime}_{i}\subseteq B_{i}
the subsets of vertices of
A_{i}
and
B_{i}
respectively, that do not serve as endpoints of the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}_{i}^{q}
. Let
E^{*}\subseteq E(H)
be the set of all edges
e
that participate in exactly
\eta
paths of
\bigcup_{i\in I}{\mathcal{P}}^{q}_{i}
. Recall that
Lemma

3.12
further guarantees that the paths of
\bigcup_{i\in I}{\mathcal{P}}^{q}_{i}
cause congestion at most
\eta
in
H
. Moreover, if we consider graph
H\setminus E^{*}
, then for all
i\in I
, the length of a shortest path connecting a vertex of
A^{\prime}_{i}
to a vertex of
B^{\prime}_{i}
is greater than
d^{\prime}
.

Running time of Algorithm
\mathsf{ProcPathPeel}
is
O(|E(H)|\cdot(\eta+jNd^{\prime}\log N))\leq O(|E(H)|\cdot(N^{8\epsilon^{3}}+jNd^{\prime}\log N))\leq O(|E(H)|\cdot jNd^{\prime}\log N)
, since
|{\mathcal{H}}|=N
.

We say that a graph
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{z}
is
successful
if
|{\mathcal{P}}_{i}^{q}|\geq|A_{i}|/4
, and it is unsuccessful otherwise. We let
{\mathcal{G}}^{s}\subseteq{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{z}
be the collection of all successful graphs, and
{\mathcal{G}}^{u}={\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{z}\setminus{\mathcal{G}}^{s}
the collection of all unsuccessful graphs. For convenience, we also partition the collection
I
of indices into a set
I^{s}
containing all indices
i\in I
where
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{G}}^{s}
and
I^{u}=I\setminus I^{s}
.
We consider again two cases, depending on whether
|{\mathcal{G}}^{s}|\geq|{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{z}|/2
.

Case 1a:
|{\mathcal{G}}^{s}|\geq|{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{z}|/2
.

If Case 1a happens then we say that iteration
q
is
successful
. Consider some index
i\in I^{s}
.
Recall that the matching
M_{i}^{q}
that we have defined contains at least
|A_{i}|/4
pairs of vertices (that we refer to as edges), while
|E^{\prime}_{i}|\leq|A_{i}|/16
by the definition of distancing. We discard from
M_{i}^{q}
all edges that lie in
E^{\prime}_{i}
; note that
|M_{i}^{q}|\geq|A_{i}|/8
continues to hold. We add the edges of the resulting matching
M_{i}^{q}
to graph
H_{i}
, and we say that graph
H_{i}

received a matching
in iteration
q
. We view this matching as the response of the matching player in the
Distanced Matching Game
. We let
{\mathcal{P}}^{q}=\bigcup_{i\in I^{s}}{\mathcal{P}}_{i}^{q}
. Notice that the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}^{q}
can be viewed as an embedding of the set
\bigcup_{i\in I^{s}}M_{i}^{q}
of edges into graph
H
. The length of each path is at most
d^{\prime}
, and the congestion of the embedding is at most
\eta
. Observe that, if an iteration is successful, then the number of graphs in
{\mathcal{H}}
that receive matchings is at least
|{\mathcal{G}}^{s}|\geq|{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{z}|/2\geq N^{1-2\epsilon^{\prime}}/2
(from Equation
6
). We terminate the current iteration, and proceed to the next iteration.

Case 1b:
|{\mathcal{G}}^{s}|<|{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{z}|/2
.

If Case 1b happens, then we say that the current iteration is
unsuccessful
.
In this case, we will construct a
(\delta_{j},d)
-distancing
(A,B,E^{*})
in graph
H
, where
E^{*}
is the set of edges that we have defined above. We will ensure that
|E^{*}|\leq|A|/N^{j\epsilon^{4}}
. The current iteration then becomes the last iteration of the algorithm, and we return
(A,B,E^{*})
as its output.
We need the following simple observation bounding
|E^{*}|
:

Observation 6.1
.

|E^{*}|\leq\frac{N^{j}\cdot d^{\prime}}{\eta\cdot 2^{z}}
.

Proof.

Note that for every graph
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{z}
,
|{\mathcal{P}}^{q}_{i}|\leq|A_{i}|\leq\frac{N^{j-1}}{2^{z}}
. Since the length of each such path is at most
d^{\prime}
, the total number of edges on all paths in
\bigcup_{i\in I}{\mathcal{P}}^{q}_{i}
is
\sum_{i\in I}\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}_{i}^{q}}|E(P)|\leq d^{\prime}\cdot|{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{z}|\cdot\frac{N^{j-1}}{2^{z}}\leq\frac{N^{j}\cdot d^{\prime}}{2^{z}}
(as
|{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{z}\leq N
). Since set
E^{*}
only contains edges that lie on
\eta
path of
{\mathcal{P}}^{q}
, the observation follows.

Recall that, if
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{G}}^{u}
, then
|A^{\prime}_{i}|\geq\frac{|A_{i}|}{2}\geq\frac{N^{j-1}}{2^{z+2}}
. Recall also that
|B^{\prime}_{i}|=|A^{\prime}_{i}|
, and
\mbox{\sf dist}_{H\setminus E^{*}}(A^{\prime}_{i},B^{\prime}_{i})>d^{\prime}
.

We denote
T=\bigcup_{i\in I^{u}}(A^{\prime}_{i}\cup B^{\prime}_{i})
, and we call the vertices of
T

terminals
.
Since we have assumed that
|{\mathcal{G}}^{s}|<|{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{z}|/2
, we get that:

|T|\geq|{\mathcal{G}}^{u}|\cdot\frac{N^{j-1}}{2^{z+1}}\geq|{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{z}|\cdot\frac{N^{j-1}}{2^{z+2}}\geq\frac{N^{j-2\epsilon^{\prime}}}{2^{z+2}}.

(8)

(we have used Equation
6
.)

We need the following simple observation.

Observation 6.2
.

For every terminal
t\in T
, at most
|T|/2
terminals may lie in
B_{H\setminus E^{*}}(t,d^{\prime}/2)
.

Proof.

Consider any terminal
t\in T
, and denote
B^{t}=B_{H\setminus E^{*}}(t,d^{\prime}/2)
. Recall that for all
H_{i^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{G}}^{u}
,
\mbox{\sf dist}_{H\setminus E^{*}}(A^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}},B^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})>d^{\prime}
. Therefore,
B^{t}
may not contain a vertex of
A^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}
and a vertex of
B^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}
. We conclude that, for every terminal
t\in T
,
|B^{t}\cap T|\leq|T|/2
.

We apply Algorithm
\mathsf{ProcSeparate}
from
Lemma

3.10
to graph
G=H\setminus E^{*}
, the set
T
of terminals, distance parameters
d
and
\Delta
that remain unchanged, and
\alpha=1/2
.
The running time of the algorithm is
O(|E(H)|\cdot|V(H)|^{64/\Delta})\leq O(|E(H)|\cdot N^{j\epsilon^{\prime}})
.

From
6.2
, the algorithm may not return a terminal
t\in T
with
|B_{H\setminus E^{*}}(t,\Delta\cdot d)\cap T|=|B_{H\setminus E^{*}}(t,d^{\prime}/2)\cap T|>\alpha\cdot|T|
. Therefore, it must compute two subsets
T_{1},T_{2}
of terminals, with
|T_{1}|=|T_{2}|
, such that for every pair
t\in T_{1},t^{\prime}\in T_{2}
of terminals,
\mbox{\sf dist}_{H\setminus E^{*}}(t,t^{\prime})\geq d
. Moreover:

\begin{split}|T_{1}|\geq\frac{|T|^{1-64/\Delta}}{3}\geq\frac{|T|^{1-\epsilon^{\prime}}}{3}\geq\frac{N^{(j-2\epsilon^{\prime})(1-\epsilon^{\prime})}}{3\cdot 2^{(z+2)(1-\epsilon^{\prime})}}\geq\frac{N^{j-2j\epsilon^{\prime}}}{2^{z+4}}\end{split}

(we have used the fact that
\Delta=64/\epsilon^{\prime}
, and Equation
8
).

Recall that, from
6.1
,

|E^{*}|\leq\frac{N^{j}\cdot d^{\prime}}{\eta\cdot 2^{z}}
.
Recall also that
\eta=N^{8\epsilon^{3}}
,
j\leq\left\lceil 1/\epsilon\right\rceil
, and
d^{\prime}\leq 2^{64/\epsilon^{4}\leq N^{\epsilon^{\prime}}}
from Inequality
5
, and since
\frac{N^{\epsilon^{\prime}}}{\log N}\geq 2^{128/\epsilon^{5}}
from the statement of
Theorem

5.2
. Therefore, we get that:

\begin{split}|E^{*}|\leq\frac{N^{j}}{2^{z}}\cdot\frac{N^{\epsilon^{4}}}{N^{8\epsilon^{3}}}\leq\frac{N^{j}}{2^{z+4}}\cdot\frac{1}{N^{6\epsilon^{3}}}\leq\frac{N^{j}}{2^{z+4}}\cdot\frac{1}{N^{3\epsilon^{4}\cdot\left\lceil 1/\epsilon\right\rceil}}\leq\frac{N^{j}}{2^{z+4}}\cdot\frac{1}{N^{3\epsilon^{\prime}j}}\leq\frac{|T_{1}|}{N^{j\epsilon^{\prime}}}.\end{split}

Lastly, recall that we have shown in Inequality
7
, that
2^{z}\leq N^{j\delta_{j-1}}
. Therefore, we get that:

|T_{1}|\geq\frac{N^{j}}{16N^{j(2\epsilon^{\prime}+\delta_{j-1})}}\geq\frac{N^{j}}{16N^{j(4(j-1)\epsilon^{\prime}+2\epsilon^{\prime})}}\geq\frac{N^{j}}{N^{j(4j\epsilon^{\prime})}}=N^{j(1-\delta_{j})}.

We conclude that
(T_{1},T_{2},E^{*})
is a
(\delta_{j},d)
-distancing, with
|E^{*}|\leq|T_{1}|/N^{j\epsilon^{\prime}}
as required. We return this distancing and terminate the algorithm.

Case 2:
|{\mathcal{H}}^{1}|\leq N^{1-\epsilon^{\prime}}
.

If this case happens, then we say that the current iteration is
irregular
. In this case, we terminate Phase 1. The outcome of the phase is the collection
{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\subseteq{\mathcal{H}}
of at least
N-N^{1-\epsilon^{\prime}}
graphs.
Recall that, for each graph
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{2}
, we computed a level-
(j-1)

Hierarchical Support Structure, that includes a subset
S(H_{i})\subseteq V(H_{i})
of vertices, such that
H_{i}
is
(\eta_{j-1},\tilde{d}_{j-1})
-well-connected with respect to
S(H_{i})
.
Let
H^{\prime}=\bigcup_{H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{2}}H_{i}
. Notice that the sets
{\mathcal{P}}^{q}
of paths that are computed in each iteration provide an embedding of graph
H^{\prime}
into
H
. The length of each resulting path is bounded by
d^{\prime}
. We use the following observation in order to both bound the congestion of this embedding, and the running time of the algorithm.

Observation 6.3
.

At the end of Phase 1, for each graph
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}
,
|E(H_{i})|\leq N^{j-1+32\epsilon^{2}}
. The number of iterations in Phase 1 is at most
N^{64\epsilon^{2}}
.

Proof.

Recall first that
\delta_{j-1}=4(j-1)\epsilon^{\prime}\geq 4\epsilon^{4}
, while
d=2^{32/\epsilon^{4}}
. Therefore,
d\geq 2^{4/\delta_{j-1}}
holds. We can then view our algorithm from Phase 1 as running the
Distanced Matching Game
simultaneously over the graphs in
{\mathcal{H}}
.
Note that for every graph
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}
:

|V(H_{i})|^{\delta_{j-1}}=N^{(j-1)\delta_{j-1}}\geq N^{4\epsilon^{\prime}}\geq\frac{2^{128}\log N}{\epsilon^{11}}\geq\frac{2^{124}\log N}{16(\epsilon^{\prime})^{2}\epsilon^{3}}\geq\frac{2^{14}(j-1)\log N}{\delta_{j-1}^{2}}=\frac{2^{14}\log(|V(H_{i})|)}{\delta_{j-1}^{2}},

since

\delta_{j-1}=4(j-1)\epsilon^{\prime}
,
2\leq j\leq\left\lceil 1/\epsilon\right\rceil
, and
\frac{N^{\epsilon^{4}}}{\log N}\geq 2^{128/\epsilon^{5}}
from the statement of
Theorem

5.2
.
We conclude that
|V(H_{i})|^{\delta_{j-1}}\geq\frac{2^{14}\log(|V(H_{i})|)}{\delta_{j-1}^{2}}
holds, satisfying the conditions of
Theorem

4.2
.
From
Theorem

4.2
, the number of iterations in a
Distanced Matching Game
in a single graph
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}
is bounded by:

|V(H_{i})|^{8\delta_{j-1}}\leq N^{(j-1)\cdot 8\delta_{j-1}}\leq N^{32(j-1)^{2}\epsilon^{\prime}}\leq N^{32\epsilon^{2}},

since
\epsilon^{\prime}=\epsilon^{4}
,
\delta_{j-1}=4(j-1)\epsilon^{\prime}
, and
j\leq\left\lceil 1/\epsilon\right\rceil
.
Therefore, a graph
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}
may receive a matching in at most
N^{32\epsilon^{2}}
iterations, and the cardinality of each such matching is at most
|V(H_{i})|/2\leq N^{j-1}/2
. We conclude that at the end of Phase 1, for each graph
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}
,
|E(H_{i})|\leq N^{j-1}\cdot N^{32\epsilon^{2}}
.

Next, we bound the number of iterations in Phase 1.
Note that at most one iteration of Phase 1 may be irregular, and at most one iteration may be regular and unsuccessful. It is now enough to bound the number of regular and successful iterations. In every regular and successful iteration, at least
N^{1-2\epsilon^{\prime}}/2
graphs in
{\mathcal{H}}
receive matchings. Therefore, every regular and successful iteration of Phase 1 results in the completion of a single iteration of the
Distanced Matching Game
in at least
N^{1-2\epsilon^{\prime}}/2
graphs of
{\mathcal{H}}
.
At the same time, the number of pairs
(i,q)
, where graph
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}
receives a matching in iteration
q
must be bounded by
|{\mathcal{H}}|\cdot N^{32\epsilon^{2}}\leq N^{1+32\epsilon^{2}}
. Since in every regular and successful iteration at least
N^{1-2\epsilon^{\prime}}/2
graphs in
{\mathcal{H}}
receive matchings, we get that the number of iterations is bounded by
2N^{32\epsilon^{2}+2\epsilon^{\prime}}\leq N^{64\epsilon^{2}}
.

Since every set
{\mathcal{P}}^{q}
of paths causes congestion at most
\eta=N^{8\epsilon^{3}}
, and the number of iterations is bounded by
N^{64\epsilon^{2}}
, we obtain an embedding of graph
H^{\prime}
into
H
via paths of length at most
d^{\prime}\leq 2^{64/\epsilon^{4}}
(from Equation
5
), that cause total congestion at most
N^{64\epsilon^{2}}\cdot N^{8\epsilon^{3}}\leq N^{128\epsilon^{2}}
. We denote this embedding by
{\mathcal{P}}
.

Note that the Phase 1 can either terminate with a regular unsuccessful iteration, in which case we terminate the algorithm and return the resulting distancing for graph
H
, or with an irregular iteration, in which case we proceed to Phase 2 of the algorithm. Before we describe Phase 2 of the algorithm, we analyze the running time of Phase 1.

Running Time Analysis of Phase 1.

We start by bounding the running time of a single iteration. Over the course of the iteration, we apply the algorithm from
Theorem

5.2
to each of the graphs
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}
, with parameter
(j-1)
. Recall that, from
6.3
, for each graph
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}
,
|E(H_{i})|\leq N^{j-1+32\epsilon^{2}}
.
From the induction hypothesis, the running time of the algorithm from
Theorem

5.2
on a single graph
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}
is bounded by:

\begin{split}c(j-1)\cdot N^{(j-1)(1+64\epsilon^{2})+7}&+c|E(H_{i})|\cdot N^{6}\\
&\leq c(j-1)\cdot N^{j+64(j-1)\epsilon^{2}+6}+cN^{j-1+32\epsilon^{2}}\cdot N^{6}\\
&=c(j-1)\cdot N^{j+64(j-1)\epsilon^{2}+6}+cN^{j+5+32\epsilon^{2}}.\\
&\leq cj\cdot N^{j+64(j-1)\epsilon^{2}+6}.\end{split}

Since
|{\mathcal{H}}|=N
, the running time of this part of the algorithm is bounded by:

cj\cdot N^{j+64(j-1)\epsilon^{2}+7}

If the iteration is regular, then we apply Algorithm
\mathsf{ProcPathPeel}
from
Lemma

3.12
, to graph
H
, collections of subsets
\left\{(A_{i},B_{i})\right\}_{i\in I}
of its vertices, length parameter
d^{\prime}
, and congestion parameter
\eta=N^{8\epsilon^{3}}
. As observed above, the running time of Algorithm
\mathsf{ProcPathPeel}
is
O(|E(H)|\cdot jNd^{\prime}\log N)=O(|E(H)|\cdot N\cdot 2^{64/\epsilon^{4}}\cdot\log N/\epsilon)\leq O(|E(H)|\cdot N^{2})
, since
d^{\prime}\leq 2^{64/\epsilon^{4}}
from Equation
5
and
N^{\epsilon^{\prime}}>2^{64/\epsilon^{5}}\cdot\log N
from the statement of
Theorem

5.2
.

If the iteration is regular and unsuccessful, then
we apply Algorithm
\mathsf{ProcSeparate}
from
Lemma

3.10
, whose running time, as observed above, is bounded by
O(|E(H)|\cdot N^{j\epsilon^{\prime}})\leq O(|E(H)|\cdot N^{2\epsilon^{3}})
, as
j\leq\left\lceil 1/\epsilon\right\rceil
and
\epsilon^{\prime}=\epsilon^{4}
.

Overall, the running time of a single iteration is bounded by:

cj\cdot N^{j+64(j-1)\epsilon^{2}+7}+O\left(|E(H)|\cdot N^{2}\right).

Since, from
6.3
, the number of iterations in Phase 1 is at most
N^{64\epsilon^{2}}
,
we get that the total running time of Phase 1 is bounded by:

cj\cdot N^{j+64j\epsilon^{2}+7}+O\left(|E(H)|\cdot N^{3}\right).

6.2
Phase 2: Distancing or Well-Connectedness

The starting point of Phase 2 is the collection
{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\subseteq{\mathcal{H}}
of at least
N-N^{1-\epsilon^{\prime}}
graphs that was computed in Phase 1.
Recall that, for each graph
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{2}
, we computed a level-
(j-1)

Hierarchical Support Structure, that includes a subset
S(H_{i})\subseteq V(H_{i})
of vertices, such that
H_{i}
is
(\eta_{j-1},\tilde{d}_{j-1})
-well-connected with respect to
S(H_{i})
. Additionally, we have computed an embedding
{\mathcal{P}}
of graph
H^{\prime}=\bigcup_{H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{2}}H_{i}
, so that the length of each path in
{\mathcal{P}}
is at most
d^{\prime}\leq 2^{64/\epsilon^{4}}
, and the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}
cause congestion at most
N^{128\epsilon^{2}}
.

In this phase we will either compute a subset
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathcal{H}}^{2}
of
r
graphs, such that graph
H
is
(\eta_{j},\tilde{d}_{j})
-well-connected with respect to the set
S(H)=\bigcup_{H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}}S(H_{i})
of vertices; or we compute a
(\delta_{j},d)
-distancing
(A,B,E^{\prime})
in graph
H
, with
|E^{\prime}|\leq\frac{|A|}{N^{j\epsilon^{4}}}
.

We consider every pair
H_{i},H_{i^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{2}
of graphs, with
i<i^{\prime}
one by one. When the pair
(H_{i},H_{i^{\prime}})
of graphs is considered, we apply Procedure
\mathsf{ProcPathPeel}
from
Lemma

3.12
to graph
H
, and two sets
A_{1}=S(H_{i}),B_{1}=S(H_{i^{\prime}})
of its vertices, with distance parameter
d^{\prime}
and congestion parameter
\eta=N^{4}
. Recall that the running time of the procedure is
O(|E(H)|(N^{4}+jd^{\prime}\log N))\leq O(|E(H)|\cdot N^{4})
. We denote by
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i,i^{\prime}}
the set of paths that the algorithm returns, and by
E^{\prime}_{i,i^{\prime}}
the set of all edges of
H
that participate in
N^{4}
paths of
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i,i^{\prime}}
. We also denote by
A^{\prime}_{i,i^{\prime}}\subseteq S(H_{i})
and
B^{\prime}_{i,i^{\prime}}\subseteq S(H_{i^{\prime}})
the sets of vertices that do not serve as endpoints of paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i,i^{\prime}}
. Recall that the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i,i^{\prime}}
have length at most
d^{\prime}
each, and they cause congestion at most
N^{4}
. Every path in
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i,i^{\prime}}
connects a vertex of
S(H_{i})
to a vertex of
S(H_{i^{\prime}})
, and every vertex of
S(H_{i})\cup S(H_{i^{\prime}})
may serve as an endpoint of at most one path in
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i,i^{\prime}}
. Moreover, the length of the shortest path in
H\setminus E^{\prime}_{i,i^{\prime}}
connecting a vertex of
A^{\prime}_{i,i^{\prime}}
to a vertex of
B^{\prime}_{i,i^{\prime}}
is greater than
d^{\prime}
. Observe also that, since
|{\mathcal{Q}}_{i,i^{\prime}}|\leq|S(H_{i})|\leq|V(H_{i})|=N^{j-1}
, and since the length of every path in
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i,i^{\prime}}
is at most
d^{\prime}
, we get that
\sum_{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{i,i^{\prime}}}|E(Q)|\leq d^{\prime}\cdot N^{j-1}
. Since
E^{\prime}_{i,i^{\prime}}
only contains edges that participate in
N^{4}
paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}
, we get that
|E^{\prime}_{i,i^{\prime}}|\leq d^{\prime}\cdot N^{j-5}
.

Let
E^{\prime}
be the union of all sets
E^{\prime}_{i,i^{\prime}}
of edges, over all pairs
H_{i},H_{i^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{2}
of graphs with
i<i^{\prime}
. Clearly,
|E^{\prime}|\leq d^{\prime}\cdot N^{j-3}
. Moreover, for every pair
H_{i},H_{i^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{2}
of graphs with
i<i^{\prime}
, the length of the shortest path in
H\setminus E^{\prime}
connecting a vertex of
A^{\prime}_{i,i^{\prime}}
to a vertex of
B^{\prime}_{i,i^{\prime}}
is greater than
d^{\prime}
.

Next, we apply procedure
\mathsf{ProcSeparate}
from
Lemma

3.10
to graph
\tilde{H}=H\setminus E^{\prime}
, with the set
T=V(H)
of terminal vertices, distance parameters
d
and
\Delta
that remain unchanged, and parameter
\alpha=1-\frac{1}{8N^{\epsilon^{\prime}}}
. Recall that the running time of the algorithm is

O(|E(H)|\cdot N^{64j/\Delta})\leq O(|E(H)|\cdot N^{2\epsilon^{3}})
(since
\Delta=64/\epsilon^{\prime}
,
j\leq\left\lceil 1/\epsilon\right\rceil
, and
\epsilon^{\prime}=\epsilon^{4}
).

We now consider two cases. The first case is that Procedure
\mathsf{ProcSeparate}
returns two subsets
A,B\subseteq V(H)
of vertices, such that
|A|=|B|
, and for every pair
v\in A,u\in B
of vertices,
\mbox{\sf dist}_{\tilde{H}}(u,v)\geq d
. Recall that in this case, the algorithm also ensures that:

\begin{split}|A|&\geq|V(H)|^{1-64/\Delta}\cdot\min\left\{(1-\alpha),\frac{1}{3}\right\}\\
&\geq N^{j(1-\epsilon^{\prime})}\cdot\frac{1}{8N^{\epsilon^{\prime}}}\\
&\geq N^{j(1-\epsilon^{\prime})-2\epsilon^{\prime}}\\
&\geq N^{j(1-4j\epsilon^{\prime})}\\
&=|V(H)|^{1-\delta_{j}}.\end{split}

(9)

(We have used the fact that
\Delta=64/\epsilon^{\prime}
and
\delta_{j}=4j\epsilon^{\prime}
).

Recall that:

|E^{\prime}|\leq d^{\prime}\cdot N^{j-3}\leq N^{j-3+\epsilon^{\prime}}.

(since
d^{\prime}\leq 2^{64/\epsilon^{3}}
from Equation
5
and
N^{\epsilon^{4}}\geq 2^{128/\epsilon^{5}}
from the statement of
Theorem

5.2
.)
Since, from Equation
9
,
|A|\geq N^{j-j\epsilon^{\prime}-2\epsilon^{\prime}}\geq N^{j-3\epsilon^{3}}
(as
\epsilon^{\prime}=\epsilon^{4}
), we get that
|E^{\prime}|\leq|A|/N^{j\epsilon^{4}}
.

We conclude that
(A,B,E^{\prime})
is a valid
(\delta_{j},d)
-distancing in graph
H
, with
|E^{\prime}|\leq|A|/N^{j\epsilon^{4}}
. We return this distancing and terminate the algorithm.

From now on we assume that Procedure
\mathsf{ProcSeparate}
computed a vertex
v\in V(H)
, such that
|B_{\tilde{H}}(v,\Delta\cdot d)|>\alpha\cdot|V(H)|=N^{j}\cdot\left(1-\frac{1}{8N^{\epsilon^{\prime}}}\right)
.
For convenience, we denote
B^{*}=B_{\tilde{H}}(v,\Delta\cdot d)
.

In this case, we construct a set
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathcal{H}}^{2}
of graphs as follows: we add graph
H_{i}
to
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
iff
|B^{*}\cap V(H_{i})|\geq\frac{7|V(H_{i})|}{8}
.
We need the following observation.

Observation 6.4
.

|{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}|\geq N-2N^{1-\epsilon^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

Recall that
|{\mathcal{H}}^{2}|\geq N-N^{1-\epsilon^{\prime}}
.
Notice that, if
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\setminus{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
, then
|V(H_{i})\setminus B^{*}|\geq|V(H_{i})|/8=N^{j-1}/8
.
Since
|V(H)\setminus B^{*}|\leq N^{j-\epsilon^{\prime}}/8
, we get that
|{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\setminus{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}|\leq\frac{N^{j-\epsilon^{\prime}}/8}{N^{j-1}/8}=N^{1-\epsilon^{\prime}}
.
Therefore,
|{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}|\geq|{\mathcal{H}}^{2}|-N^{1-\epsilon^{\prime}}\geq N-2N^{1-\epsilon^{\prime}}
.

We discard arbitrary graphs from
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
, until
|{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}|=N-\left\lceil 2N^{1-\epsilon^{\prime}}\right\rceil
holds. Let
S(H)=\bigcup_{H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}}S(H_{i})
. Note that we have now obtained a level-
j
Hierarchical Support Structure for graph
H
, whose associated collection of graphs is
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
, with
|{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}|=N-\left\lceil 2N^{1-\epsilon^{\prime}}\right\rceil=r
. We use the embedding
{\mathcal{P}}
of the graph
\bigcup_{H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{2}}H_{i}
that we have computed in Phase 1. By discarding paths that are no longer needed from
{\mathcal{P}}
, we obtain an embedding
{\mathcal{P}}
of graph
\bigcup_{H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}}H_{i}
into
H
, such that every path in the embedding has length at most
d^{\prime}\leq 2^{64/\epsilon^{4}}
, and the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}
cause congestion at most
N^{128/\epsilon^{3}}
.
We prove the following lemma in
Section

6.3
.

Lemma 6.5
.

Graph
H
is
(\eta_{j},\tilde{d}_{j})
-well-connected with respect to
S(H)
.

In order to complete the proof of
Theorem

5.2
, it is now enough to show that the running time of the algorithm is suitably bounded.

Running Time Analysis

The algorithm performs at most
N^{2}
calls to Procedure
\mathsf{ProcPathPeel}
. As observed above, the running time of each such call is at most
O(|E(H)|\cdot N^{4})
. The running time of Procedure
\mathsf{ProcSeparate}
, as shown above, is at most
O(|E(H)|\cdot N^{2\epsilon^{3}})
. Overall, the running time of Phase 2 is
O(|E(H)|\cdot N^{6})
.

Altogether, the running time of the whole algorithm is bounded by:

cj\cdot N^{j+64j\epsilon^{2}+7}+O\left(|E(H)|\cdot N^{6}\right)\leq cj\cdot N^{j(1+64\epsilon^{2})+7}+c|E(H)|\cdot N^{6},

if
c
is a large enough constant.

In order to complete the proof of
Theorem

5.2
, it is now enough to prove
Lemma

6.5
, which we do next.

6.3
Proof of
Lemma

6.5

We will use the following simple observation.

Observation 6.6
.

For every pair
H_{i},H_{i^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
with
i<i^{\prime}
,
|{\mathcal{Q}}_{i,i^{\prime}}|\geq N^{j-1}/4
. (Here,
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i,i^{\prime}}
is the set of paths that was computed in Phase 2 of the algorithm.)

Proof.

Recall that, from
5.1
,
|V(H_{i})\setminus S(H_{i})|\leq|V(H_{i})|\cdot\frac{4(j-1)}{N^{\epsilon^{4}}}<\frac{|V(H_{i})|}{64}
(since
j\leq\left\lceil\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right\rceil
). Therefore,
|S(H_{i})|\geq\frac{63|V(H_{i})|}{64}=\frac{63\cdot N^{j-1}}{64}
, and similarly,
|S(H_{i^{\prime}})|\geq\frac{63\cdot N^{j-1}}{64}
.

Assume now for contradiction that
|{\mathcal{Q}}_{i,i^{\prime}}|<\frac{N^{j-1}}{4}
. Recall that we have defined sets
A^{\prime}_{i,i^{\prime}}\subseteq S(H_{i})
and
B^{\prime}_{i,i^{\prime}}\subseteq S(H_{i^{\prime}})
of vertices that do not serve as endpoints of paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i,i^{\prime}}
. If
|{\mathcal{Q}}_{i,i^{\prime}}|<\frac{N^{j-1}}{4}
, then:

|A^{\prime}_{i,i^{\prime}}|\geq|S(H_{i})|-|{\mathcal{Q}}_{i,i^{\prime}}|\geq\frac{63\cdot N^{j-1}}{64}-\frac{N^{j-1}}{4}>\frac{2\cdot N^{j-1}}{3}.

Similarly,
|B^{\prime}_{i,i^{\prime}}|>\frac{2\cdot N^{j-1}}{3}
. Since
H_{i}
was added to
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
,
|B^{*}\cap V(H_{i})|\geq\frac{7\cdot N^{j-1}}{8}
. So at least one vertex
u\in A^{\prime}_{i,i^{\prime}}
must lie in
B^{*}
. For similar reasons, at least one vertex
u^{\prime}\in B^{\prime}_{i,i^{\prime}}
must lie in
B^{*}
. From the definition of
B^{*}
,
\mbox{\sf dist}_{\tilde{H}}(u,u^{\prime})\leq 2\Delta\cdot d=d^{\prime}
. Recall however that
\tilde{H}=H\setminus E^{\prime}
, and
E^{\prime}_{i,i^{\prime}}\subseteq E^{\prime}
. Procedure
\mathsf{ProcPathPeel}
guarantees that the shortest path connecting
u
to
u^{\prime}
in graph
H\setminus E^{\prime}_{i,i^{\prime}}
has length greater than
d^{\prime}
. So
\mbox{\sf dist}_{\tilde{H}}(u,u^{\prime})>d^{\prime}
must hold, contradicting our previous claim that
\mbox{\sf dist}_{\tilde{H}}(u,u^{\prime})\leq d^{\prime}
.

We now turn to the proof of
Lemma

6.5
.
We assume that we are given two disjoint equal-cardinality subsets
A,B
of vertices of
S(H)
. Our goal is to prove that there exists a set
{\mathcal{P}}^{*}
of paths in graph
H
, routing every vertex of
A
to a distinct vertex of
B
, such that the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}^{*}
cause congestion at most
\eta_{j}
in
H
, and the length of every path is at most
\tilde{d}_{j}
. We will prove that such a set of paths exists by exploiting the fact that, every graph
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
is
(\eta_{j-1},\tilde{d}_{j-1})
-well-connected with respect to the set
S(H_{i})
of its vertices, together with the embedding of these graphs into
H
, and the sets
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i,i^{\prime}}
of paths that we have computed in Phase 2 of the algorithm for every pair
H_{i},H_{i^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
of graphs with
i<i^{\prime}
.

The remainder of the proof of
Lemma

6.5
consists of three steps. In the first step, we route some pairs in
A\times B
within the graphs
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
. After the completion of this step, for every graph
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
, either all vertices of
S(H_{i})
that remain to be routed lie in
A
, or all such vertices lie in
B
. In the remaining two steps we complete the routing of these remaining vertices. Specifically, in Step 2 we define a “meta-graph”
\hat{G}
, whose vertices represent the graphs
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
, with weights on its edges. Intuitively, if an edge connecting two vertices that represent graphs
H_{i}
and
H_{i^{\prime}}
has weight
w(e)
, then we intend to construct
w(e)
paths that connect vertices of
S(H_{i})\cap A
to vertices of
S(H_{i^{\prime}})\cap B
. In this step, we also perform a “global routing”: for every pair
H_{i},H_{i^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
of graphs, whose corresponding edge in
\hat{G}
has weight
w
, we connect vertices of
S(H_{i})
to vertices of
S(H_{i^{\prime}})
with
w
paths. In the third and the final step, we complete the construction of the set
{\mathcal{P}}^{*}
of paths by using “local routing”, in which some pairs of vertex subsets are routed within each graph
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
. We now describe each of the three steps in turn.

Step 1: Initial Routing within the Graphs of
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}

We process every graph
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
one by one. When graph
H_{i}
is processed, we denote
N^{A}_{i}=|A\cap S(H_{i})|
and
N^{B}_{i}=|B\cap S(H_{i})|
. Denote
\beta_{i}=\min\left\{N^{A}_{i},N^{B}_{i}\right\}
. Next, we select two arbitrary subsets
X_{i}\subseteq A\cap S(H_{i})
and
Y_{i}\subseteq B\cap S(H_{i})
, each of which contains exactly
\beta_{i}
vertices.
Since our algorithm ensures that graph
H_{i}
is
(\eta_{j-1},\tilde{d}_{j-1})
-well-connected with respect to
S(H_{i})
, there exists a set
{\mathcal{R}}_{i}
of paths in graph
H_{i}
, which is a one-to-one routing of vertices of
X_{i}
to vertices of
Y_{i}
. Every path in
{\mathcal{R}}_{i}
has length at most
\tilde{d}_{j-1}
, and the paths in
{\mathcal{R}}_{i}
cause congestion at most
\eta_{j-1}
in
H_{i}
.

Let
H^{\prime}=\bigcup_{H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}}H_{i}
. Recall that we have computed, in Phase 1 of the algorithm, an embedding
{\mathcal{P}}
of
H^{\prime}
into
H
, where every path in the embedding has length at most
d^{\prime}
, and the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}
cause congestion at most
N^{128\epsilon^{2}}
in
H
.

Consider now the set
\bigcup_{H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}}{\mathcal{R}}_{i}
of paths in graph
H^{\prime}
. This set of paths defines a one-to-one routing of vertex set
X=\bigcup_{H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}}X_{i}
to vertex set
Y=\bigcup_{H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}}Y_{i}
, where the length of every path is at most
\tilde{d}_{j-1}
, and the paths in
{\mathcal{R}}
cause congestion at most
\eta_{j-1}
in
H^{\prime}
.
We now use the embedding
{\mathcal{P}}
of
H^{\prime}
into
H
in order to compute a set
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
of paths in graph
H
, that route every vertex of
X
to a distinct vertex of
Y
, via the algorithm from
3.1
.
We are then guaranteed that the length of every path in
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
is at most
\tilde{d}_{j-1}\cdot d^{\prime}
. Recall that
\tilde{d}_{j-1}=2^{c(j-1)/\epsilon^{4}}
, while
d^{\prime}<\frac{1}{4}\cdot 2^{c/\epsilon^{4}}
from Inequality
5
. Therefore, the length of every path in
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
is bounded by:

\tilde{d}_{j-1}\cdot d^{\prime}\leq 2^{c(j-1)/\epsilon^{4}}\cdot 2^{c/\epsilon^{4}}=2^{cj/\epsilon^{4}}=\tilde{d}_{j}.

The algorithm from
3.1
also guarantees that the congestion caused by the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
in
H
is at most
\eta_{j-1}\cdot N^{128\epsilon^{2}}
. Since
\eta_{j-1}=N^{6+256(j-1)\epsilon^{2}}
, we get that the congestion caused by the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
is bounded by:

\eta_{j-1}\cdot N^{128\epsilon^{2}}\leq N^{6+256(j-1)\epsilon^{2}}\cdot N^{128\epsilon^{2}}\leq\frac{N^{6+256j\epsilon^{2}}}{2}=\frac{\eta_{j}}{2}.

We have now obtained a set
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
of paths in graph
H
, that routes every vertex of
X
to a distinct vertex of
Y
, so that the length of every path is at most
\tilde{d}_{j}
, and the congestion caused by the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
is bounded by
\eta_{j}/2
.

We partition the graphs of
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
into three subsets. Set
{\mathcal{H}}^{N}
contains all graphs
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
, in which
N_{i}^{A}=N_{i}^{B}
. We no longer need to route any vertices in such graphs, as for each such graph
\beta_{i}=N^{A}_{i}=N^{B}_{i}
;
X_{i}=A\cap S(H_{i})
; and
Y_{i}=B\cap S(H_{i})
must hold. Set
{\mathcal{H}}^{A}
contains graphs
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
with
N_{i}^{A}>N_{i}^{B}
. For each such graph
H_{i}
, we denote by
D(H_{i})=N_{i}^{A}-N_{i}^{B}
, and by
X^{\prime}_{i}=(A\cap S(H_{i}))\setminus X_{i}
– the set of vertices of
S(H_{i})
that remain to be routed. Clearly,
|X^{\prime}_{i}|=D(H_{i})
. Similarly, set
{\mathcal{H}}^{B}
contains graphs

H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
with
N_{i}^{B}>N_{i}^{A}
. For each such graph
H_{i}
, we denote by
D(H_{i})=N_{i}^{B}-N_{i}^{A}
, and by
X^{\prime}_{i}=(B\cap S(H_{i}))\setminus Y_{i}
– the set of vertices that remain to be routed. As before,
|X^{\prime}_{i}|=D(H_{i})
holds. Notice also that, since
|A|=|B|
,
\sum_{H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{A}}D(H_{i})=\sum_{H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{B}}D(H_{i})
must hold. Denote
\hat{A}=\bigcup_{H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{A}}X^{\prime}_{i}
and
\hat{B}=\bigcup_{H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{B}}X^{\prime}_{i}
.
It is now enough to prove the following lemma.

Lemma 6.7
.

There is a set
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime\prime}
of paths in graph
H
, routing every vertex of
\hat{A}
to a distinct vertex of
\hat{B}
, so that the length of every path in
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime\prime}
is at most
\tilde{d}_{j}
, and the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime\prime}
cause congestion at most
\eta_{j}/2
in
H
.

Indeed, by letting
{\mathcal{P}}^{*}={\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}\cup{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime\prime}
, we obtain a set of paths in graph
H
that defines a one-to-one routing of the set
A
of vertices to the set
B
of vertices via paths of length at most
\tilde{d}_{j}
, so that the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}^{*}
cause congestion at most
\eta_{j}
. In order to complete the proof of
Lemma

6.5
, it is now enough to prove
Lemma

6.7
. We focus on the proof of
Lemma

6.7
in the remainder of this section.

We will start by constructing a “meta-graph” representing the graphs of
{\mathcal{H}}^{A}\cup{\mathcal{H}}^{B}
, that will guide the construction of global routing.

Step 2: Meta-Graph and Global Routing

Abusing the notation, for simplicity, in the remainder of this proof we denote
{\mathcal{H}}^{A}=\left\{H_{1},H_{2},\ldots,H_{q}\right\}
, and for all
1\leq i\leq q
, we denote
D(H_{i})
by
D_{i}
. We also denote
{\mathcal{H}}^{B}=\left\{H^{\prime}_{1},H^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,H^{\prime}_{q^{\prime}}\right\}
, and for
1\leq i^{\prime}\leq q^{\prime}
, we denote
D(H^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})
by
D^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}
. For all
1\leq i\leq q
, we denote the set
X^{\prime}_{i}\subseteq S(H_{i})
of
D_{i}
vertices that remains to be routed by
Y_{i}
, and for all
1\leq i^{\prime}\leq q^{\prime}
, we denote the corresponding subset of
D^{\prime}_{i}
vertices of
S(H^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})
by
Y^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}
.
We now define a
routing meta-graph
, that will be used in order to guide the construction of the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}^{*}
, and show that such a graph exists.

Routing Meta-Graph

We start by defining a routing meta-graph.

Definition 6.1
(Routing Meta-Graph).

A bipartite graph
\hat{G}=(U,U^{\prime},\hat{E})
with integral weights
w(e)\geq 0
on its edges
e\in\hat{E}
is a
routing meta-graph
if:

•

U=\left\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{q}\right\}
;

•

U^{\prime}=\left\{v^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,v^{\prime}_{q^{\prime}}\right\}
;

•

for every vertex
v_{i}\in U
,
\sum_{e\in\delta_{\hat{G}}(v_{i})}w(e)=D_{i}
; and

•

for every vertex
v_{i^{\prime}}^{\prime}\in U^{\prime}
,
\sum_{e\in\delta_{\hat{G}}(v^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})}w(e)=D^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}
.

We refer to vertices of
\hat{G}
as supernodes and edges of
\hat{G}
as meta-edges.

We use the following claim to show that a routing meta-graph exists.

Claim 6.8
.

There exists a routing meta-graph.

Proof.

We start with the graph
\hat{G}=(U,U^{\prime},\hat{E})
, where
U=\left\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{q}\right\}
,
U^{\prime}=\left\{v^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,v^{\prime}_{q^{\prime}}\right\}
, and
\hat{E}=\emptyset
, and then iterate, as long as there exist indices
1\leq i\leq q
and
1\leq i^{\prime}\leq q^{\prime}
, such that
D_{i}>0
and
D^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}>0
holds.

In order to execute an iteration, we consider any pair of such indices
(i,i^{\prime})
. Let
\hat{\Delta}=\min\left\{D_{i},D^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}\right\}
. We add an edge
(v_{i},v^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})
to
\hat{E}
, whose weight is
\hat{\Delta}
, and we decrease
D_{i}
and
D^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}
by
\hat{\Delta}
. Once the algorithm terminates, since
\sum_{i=1}^{q}D_{i}=\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{q^{\prime}}D^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}
, it is immediate to verify that the resulting graph
\hat{G}
is a valid routing meta-graph.

Global Routing

Consider some pair
v_{i}\in U
,
v^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}\in U^{\prime}
of supernodes in graph
\hat{G}
. From
6.6
, there exists a collection of paths in graph
H
, that we denote, abusing the notation, by
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i,i^{\prime}}
, such that the following hold:

•

every path in
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i,i^{\prime}}
originates at a vertex of
S(H_{i})
and terminates at a vertex of
S(H^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})
;

•

every vertex of
S(H_{i})\cup S(H^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})
is an endpoint of at most one path in
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i,i^{\prime}}
;

•

|{\mathcal{Q}}_{i,i^{\prime}}|=\left\lceil N^{j-1}/4\right\rceil
;

•

each path in
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i,i^{\prime}}
has length at most
d^{\prime}
; and

•

the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i,i^{\prime}}
cause congestion at most
N^{4}
in
H
.

The set
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i,i^{\prime}}
of paths naturally defines a matching
M_{i,i^{\prime}}\subseteq S(H_{i})\times S(H^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})
: we include a pair
(x,y)
of vertices in
M_{i,i^{\prime}}
if
x\in S(H_{i})
,
y\in S(H^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})
, and some path in
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i,i^{\prime}}
has
x
and
y
as its endpoints. Clearly,
|M_{i,i^{\prime}}|=\left\lceil N^{j-1}/4\right\rceil
. Notice that for every meta-edge
e=(v_{i},v^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})
in graph
\hat{G}
,
w(e)\leq D_{i}\leq|S(H_{i})|\leq N^{j-1}
must hold. We will select, for every edge
e=(v_{i},v^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})\in\hat{G}
, a multi-set
M^{\prime}_{i,i^{\prime}}
of pairs
(x,y)\in M_{i,i^{\prime}}
of vertices, of cardinality
w(e)
. (We note that a pair
(x,y)\in M_{i,i^{\prime}}
of vertices may be added to
M^{\prime}_{i,i^{\prime}}
multiple times). We will ensure that, for every supernode
v_{i}\in U
, a vertex
x\in S(H_{i})
may participate in at most four pairs in
\bigcup_{e=(v_{i},v^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})\in\delta_{\hat{G}}(v_{i})}M^{\prime}_{i,i^{\prime}}
, and the same holds for supernodes of
U^{\prime}
. For every meta-edge
e=(v_{i},v^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})\in\hat{E}
, we will then use the paths of
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i,i^{\prime}}
whose endpoints lie in
M^{\prime}_{i,i^{\prime}}
to define a
global routing
. Let
{\mathcal{Q}}^{0}
denote the resulting collection (multiset) of all such paths. So for every meta-edge
(v_{i},v^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})\in\hat{E}
, for every pair
(x,y)\in M^{\prime}_{i,i^{\prime}}
of vertices,
{\mathcal{Q}}^{0}
contains the path of
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i,i^{\prime}}
whose endpoints are
x
and
y
. If
(x,y)
appears multiple times in
M^{\prime}_{i,i^{\prime}}
, then
{\mathcal{Q}}^{0}
contains multiple copies of this path. For every graph
\tilde{H}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{A}\cap{\mathcal{H}}^{B}
, for every vertex
x\in S(\tilde{H})
, we denote by
k(x)
the number of paths of
{\mathcal{Q}}^{0}
, for which
x
serves as an endpoint. Our construction will guarantee that, for all
1\leq i\leq q
,
\sum_{x\in S(H_{i})}k(x)=D_{i}
, and similarly, for all
1\leq i^{\prime}\leq q^{\prime}
,
\sum_{x\in S(H^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})}k(x)=D^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}
. As mentioned already, we will ensure that, for every graph
\tilde{H}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
, for every vertex
x\in S(\tilde{H})
,
k(x)\in\left\{0,\ldots,4\right\}
. In our last step, we will perform
local routing
, in which, for all
1\leq i\leq q
, we connect every vertex of
Y_{i}
to some vertex of
S(H_{i})
by a path, such that every vertex
x\in S(H_{i})
is the last endpoint of exactly
k(x)
such paths. We perform a similar routing in graphs of
{\mathcal{H}}^{B}
. This local routing exploits the fact that every graph
\tilde{H}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
is
(\eta_{j-1},\tilde{d}_{j-1})
-well-connected, together with the embedding
{\mathcal{P}}
of the graph
H^{\prime}=\bigcup_{\tilde{H}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}}\tilde{H}
into
H
that we have computed.

In order to simplify the notation, for all
1\leq i\leq q
, we denote by
\hat{E}_{i}\subseteq\hat{E}
the set of all meta-edges of
\hat{G}
that are incident to supernode
v_{i}
in
\hat{G}
. Similarly, for all
1\leq i^{\prime}\leq q^{\prime}
, we denote by
\hat{E}_{i^{\prime}}^{\prime}\subseteq\hat{E}
the set of all meta-edges of
\hat{G}
that are incident to supernode
v^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}
in
\hat{G}
.
We prove the following lemma, that allows us to perform global routing.

Lemma 6.9
.

For every meta-edge
e=(v_{i},v^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})\in\hat{E}
, there is a multiset
M^{\prime}_{i,i^{\prime}}
of pairs of vertices of
S(H_{i})\times S(H^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})
, for which the following hold. For all
1\leq i\leq q
, for every vertex
x\in S(H_{i})
, let
k(x)
be the total number of pairs in
\bigcup_{(v_{i},v^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})\in\hat{E}_{i}}M^{\prime}_{i,i^{\prime}}
, in which vertex
x
participates. Similarly, for all
1\leq i^{\prime}\leq q^{\prime}
, for every vertex
x\in S(H^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})
, let
k(x)
be the total number of pairs in
\bigcup_{(v_{i},v^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})\in\hat{E}^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}}M^{\prime}_{i,i^{\prime}}
, in which vertex
x
participates. Then:

•

for every meta-edge
e=(v_{i},v^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})\in E(\hat{G})
, a pair
(x,y)
of vertices may only belong to
M^{\prime}_{i,i^{\prime}}
if
(x,y)\in M_{i,i^{\prime}}
(but it may be added to
M^{\prime}_{i,i^{\prime}}
multiple times);

•

for every vertex
x\in\left(\bigcup_{i=1}^{q}S(H_{i})\right)\cup\left(\bigcup_{i^{\prime}=1}^{q^{\prime}}S(H^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})\right)
,
k(x)\in\left\{0,\ldots,4\right\}
;

•

for all
1\leq i\leq q
,
\sum_{x\in S(H_{i})}k(x)=D_{i}
; and

•

for all
1\leq i^{\prime}\leq q^{\prime}
,
\sum_{x\in S(H^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})}k(x)=D^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

We construct the following directed flow network. We start with a bipartite graph
\tilde{G}=(\tilde{X},\tilde{Y},\tilde{E})
, where
\tilde{X}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{q}S(H_{i})
,
\tilde{Y}=\bigcup_{i^{\prime}=1}^{q^{\prime}}S(H^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})
, and
\tilde{E}=\bigcup_{(v_{i},v^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})\in E(\hat{G})}M_{i,i^{\prime}}
. All edges are directed from vertices of
\tilde{X}
towards vertices of
\tilde{Y}
, and they have capacity
4
each. For all
1\leq i\leq q
, we add a vertex
s_{i}
, that connects to every vertex in
S(H_{i})
with an edge of capacity
4
. For all
1\leq i^{\prime}\leq q^{\prime}
, we add a vertex
t_{i^{\prime}}
, to which every vertex of
S(H^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})
connects with an edge of capacity
4
. Lastly, we add a source vertex
s
, and a destination vertex
t
. For all
1\leq i\leq q
, we add an edge
(s,s_{i})
of capacity
D_{i}
, and for all
1\leq i^{\prime}\leq q^{\prime}
, we add an edge
(t_{i},t)
of capacity
D^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}
.

We claim that this network as a valid
s
-
t
flow
f
of value
D=\sum_{i=1}^{q}D_{i}=\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{q^{\prime}}D^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}
. We obtain this flow as follows. Consider a meta-edge
e=(v_{i},v^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})\in E(\hat{G})
. Recall that we are given an integral weight
w(e)\leq N^{j-1}
, and a matching
M_{i,i^{\prime}}\subseteq S(H_{i})\times S(H^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})
of cardinality
\left\lceil N^{j-1}/4\right\rceil
. For every edge
e^{\prime}=(x,y)\in M_{i,i^{\prime}}
, we set the flow
f(e^{\prime})=\frac{w(e)}{|M_{i,i^{\prime}}|}=\frac{w(e)}{\left\lceil N^{j-1}/4\right\rceil}
. Notice that this ensures that the total flow on all edges of
M_{i,i^{\prime}}
is precisely
w(e)
, and for every edge
e^{\prime}\in M_{i,i^{\prime}}
,
f(e^{\prime})\leq 4
. Once we process every meta-edge of
\hat{G}
, we finalize the flow values
f(e^{\prime})
for all edges
e^{\prime}\in\tilde{E}
.

Consider now some index
1\leq i\leq q
, and some vertex
x\in S(H_{i})
. We claim that the total flow on all edges of
\tilde{E}
that are incident to
x
in the flow network is at most
4
. Indeed, recall that
\hat{E}_{i}
is the set of all meta-edges of
\hat{G}
that are incident to supernode
v_{i}
. From the definition of a routing meta-graph, we are guaranteed that
\sum_{e\in\hat{E}_{i}}w(e)=D_{i}
. For every meta-edge
e=(v_{i},v_{i^{\prime}}^{\prime})\in\hat{E}_{i}
, if some edge of
M_{i,i^{\prime}}
is incident to
x
, then the flow on this edge is
\frac{w(e)}{\left\lceil N^{j-1}/4\right\rceil}
. Therefore, the total flow on all edges of
\tilde{E}
that are incident to
x
is bounded by:

\sum_{e\in\hat{E}_{i}}\frac{w(e)}{\left\lceil N^{j-1}/4\right\rceil}=\frac{D_{i}}{\left\lceil N^{j-1}/4\right\rceil}\leq 4,

since
D_{i}\leq|S(H_{i})|\leq N^{j-1}
must hold.

We set the flow on edge
(s_{i},x)
to be equal to the total amount of flow on all edges of
\tilde{E}
that are incident to
x
in the flow network, which, from the above discussion, is bounded by
4
.

From similar arguments, for every index
1\leq i^{\prime}\leq q^{\prime}
, and every vertex
y\in S(H^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})
, the total flow on all edges of
\tilde{E}
that are incident to
y
in the flow network is at most
4
. We set the flow on the edge
(y,t_{i})
to be the total flow on all edges of
\tilde{E}
that are incident to
y
in the flow network.

Next, we consider an index
1\leq i\leq q
. We set the flow on edge
(s,s_{i})
to be
D_{i}
. We claim that
\sum_{x\in S(H_{i})}f(s_{i},x)=D_{i}
. Indeed, from our construction:

\sum_{x\in S(H_{i})}f(s_{i},x)=\sum_{(v_{i},v^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})\in\hat{E}_{i}}\sum_{(x,y)\in M_{i,i^{\prime}}}f(x,y)=\sum_{e=(v_{i},v^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})\in\hat{E}_{i}}\sum_{(x,y)\in M_{i,i^{\prime}}}\frac{w(e)}{\left\lceil N^{j-1}/4\right\rceil}=\sum_{e=(v_{i},v^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})\in\hat{E}_{i}}w(e)=D_{i}.

(we have used the fact that
|M_{i,i^{\prime}}|=\left\lceil N^{j-1}/4\right\rceil
for every meta-edge
(v_{i},v^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})\in E(\hat{G})
).

Similarly, we consider an index
1\leq i^{\prime}\leq q^{\prime}
. We set the flow on edge
(t_{i^{\prime}},t)
to be
D^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}
. Using the same reasoning as above,
\sum_{y\in S(H^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})}f(y,t_{i})=D^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}
.
We conclude that we have obtained a valid
s
-
t
flow in the above flow network, whose value is
D
. Since all edge capacities in the flow network are integral, from the integrality of flow, there is an integral
s
-
t
flow
f^{\prime}
of value
D
in this flow network.

We are now ready to define the multisets
M^{\prime}_{i,i^{\prime}}
of pairs of vertices from
S(H_{i})\times S(H^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})
, for all
(v_{i},v^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})\in E(\hat{G})
. Consider any meta-edge
(v_{i},v^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})\in E(\hat{G})
, and some pair
(x,y)\in M_{i,i^{\prime}}
of vertices. If
f^{\prime}(x,y)>0
, then we include
f^{\prime}(x,y)
copies of the pair
(x,y)
to
M^{\prime}_{i,i^{\prime}}
.

We now verify that all requirements hold for this definition of the multisets
M^{\prime}_{i,i^{\prime}}
for all
(v_{i},v^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})\in E(\hat{G})
. Clearly, a pair
(x,y)
of vertices may only be added to
M^{\prime}_{i,i^{\prime}}
if
(x,y)\in M_{i,i^{\prime}}
.

Consider now some vertex
x\in\bigcup_{i=1}^{q}S(H_{i})
. Recall that
k(x)
is the total number of pairs in
\bigcup_{(v_{i},v^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})\in\hat{E}}M^{\prime}_{i,i^{\prime}}
in which
x
participates. This is equal to the total flow leaving vertex
x
in
f^{\prime}
, which, in turn, is equal to the flow on edge
(s_{i},x)
. From our definition, the capacity of this edge is
4
, so
k(x)\in\left\{0,\ldots,4\right\}
. If
x\in\bigcup_{i^{\prime}=1}^{q^{\prime}}S(H^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})
, then
k(x)\in\left\{0,\ldots,4\right\}
for similar reasons.

Consider now some index
1\leq i\leq q
. From the above discussion,
\sum_{x\in S(H_{i})}k(x)=\sum_{x\in S(H_{i})}f^{\prime}(s_{i},x)
. In other words,
\sum_{x\in S(H_{i})}k(x)
is the total amount of flow leaving vertex
s_{i}
in
f^{\prime}
. From conservation of flow this must be equal to the total amount of flow entering
s_{i}
. Since we send
D=\sum_{i=1}^{q}D_{i}
flow units from
s
to
t
, and since, for all
1\leq i\leq q
, the capacity of the edge
(s,s_{i})
is
D_{i}
, we must send
D_{i}
flow units on edge
(s,s_{i})
. In other words, for all
1\leq i\leq q
,
\sum_{x\in S(H_{i})}k(x)=D_{i}
must hold. From similar arguments, for all
1\leq i^{\prime}\leq q^{\prime}
,
\sum_{x\in S(H^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})}k(x)=D^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}
must hold.

We are now ready to define the global routing.
For every meta-edge
e=(v_{i},v^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})\in E(\hat{G})
, we consider the resulting collection
M^{\prime}_{i,i^{\prime}}\subseteq S(H_{i})\times S(H_{i^{\prime}})
of pairs of vertices. We define a (multi)-set
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}_{i,i^{\prime}}
of paths, as follows. Consider any pair
(x,y)\in M^{\prime}_{i,i^{\prime}}
, and assume that the number of times that it appears in
M^{\prime}_{i,i^{\prime}}
is
N(x,y)
. Recall that
(x,y)\in M_{i,i^{\prime}}
must hold, so there must be a path
Q(x,y)\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{i,i^{\prime}}
connecting
x
to
y
. We add
N(x,y)
copies of this path to
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}_{i,i^{\prime}}
. Note that, from
Lemma

6.9
,
N(x,y)\leq k(x)\leq 4
must hold.

We then let
{\mathcal{Q}}^{0}=\bigcup_{(v_{i},v^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})\in E(\hat{G})}{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}_{i,i^{\prime}}
