(again, set
{\mathcal{Q}}^{0}
is a multiset, so if some path appears several times in some set
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}_{i,i^{\prime}}
, then it will appear several times in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{0}
).

Recall that, for every pair
H_{i},H^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
of graphs, the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i,i^{\prime}}
have length at most
d^{\prime}
each, and they cause congestion at most
N^{4}
in
H
. Since
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}_{i,i^{\prime}}
contains at most four copies of each path in
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i,i^{\prime}}
, and since
|E(\hat{G})|\leq|{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}|^{2}\leq N^{2}
, the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{0}
cause congestion at most
4N^{6}
in
H
, and every path has length at most
d^{\prime}
as before.
For every vertex
x\in\left(\bigcup_{i=1}^{q}S(H_{i})\right)\cup\left(\bigcup_{i^{\prime}=1}^{q^{\prime}}S(H^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})\right)
, we use the definition of the value
k(x)
from
Lemma

6.9
. The number of paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{0}
, in which
x
serves as an endpoint is then precisely
k(x)
, and, from
Lemma

6.9
,
k(x)\in\left\{0,\ldots,4\right\}
. Recall also that,
for all
1\leq i\leq q
,
\sum_{x\in S(H_{i})}k(x)=D_{i}
, and
for all
1\leq i^{\prime}\leq q^{\prime}
,
\sum_{x\in S(H^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})}k(x)=D^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}
.

Step 3: Local Routing

Consider some graph
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{A}
. We have defined a set
Y_{i}\subseteq S(H_{i})
of
D_{i}
vertices of
H_{i}
that need to be routed. For every vertex
x\in S(H_{i})
, we are also now given a value
k(x)\in\left\{0,\ldots,4\right\}
, which is exactly the number of paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{0}
for which vertex
x
serves as an endpoint. We are also guaranteed that
\sum_{x\in S(H_{i})}k(x)=D_{i}
. We can then construct four sets
Z_{i}^{1},Z_{i}^{2},Z_{i}^{3},Z_{i}^{4}
of vertices of
S(X)
, such that for every vertex
x\in S(X)
, each of the four sets contains at most one copy of
x
; and the number of sets in
\left\{Z_{i}^{1},\ldots,Z_{i}^{4}\right\}
containing
x
is exactly
k(x)
. Clearly,
\sum_{a=1}^{4}|Z_{i}^{a}|=D_{i}
. We also partition the set
Y_{i}
of vertices into four subsets
Y_{i}^{1},\ldots,Y_{i}^{4}
arbitrarily, so that, for all
1\leq a\leq 4
,
|Y_{i}^{a}|=|Z_{i}^{a}|
. Since
Y_{i}\subseteq S(H_{i})
, from the fact that graph
H_{i}
is
(\eta_{j-1},\tilde{d}_{j-1})
-well-connected with respect to
S(H_{i})
, for all
1\leq a\leq 4
, there is a set
\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{i}^{a}
of
|Z_{i}^{a}|
paths in graph
H_{i}
, routing every vertex of
Y_{i}^{a}
to a distinct vertex of
Z_{i}^{a}
, such that the length of every path is at most
\tilde{d}_{j-1}
, and the paths cause congestion at most
\eta_{j-1}
in graph
H_{i}
.
Let
\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{i}=\bigcup_{a=1}^{4}\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{i}^{a}
. We think of the paths in
\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{i}
as being directed away from vertices of
Y_{i}
. Notice that the paths in
\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{i}
route every vertex of
Y_{i}
to some vertex of
S(H_{i})
, such that, for every vertex
x\in S(H_{i})
, exactly
k(x)
paths of
\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{i}
terminate at
x
. The paths in
\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{i}
cause congestion at most
4\eta_{j-1}
in graph
H_{i}
, and have length at most
\tilde{d}_{j-1}
each.

Consider the graph
H^{\prime}=\bigcup_{H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}}H_{i}
. Recall that we have computed, in Phase 1 of the algorithm, an embedding
{\mathcal{P}}
of
H^{\prime}
into
H
, where every path in the embedding has length at most
d^{\prime}
, and the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}
cause congestion at most
N^{128\epsilon^{2}}
in
H
.

Consider now the set
\hat{\mathcal{P}}=\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq q}\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{i}
of paths in graph
H^{\prime}
. This set of paths routes every vertex of
\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq q}Y_{i}
to some vertex of
\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq q}S(H_{i})
, such that, for every vertex
x\in\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq q}S(H_{i})
, exactly
k(x)
paths of
\hat{\mathcal{P}}
terminate at
x
. Additionally, the length of every path in
\hat{\mathcal{P}}
is is at most
\tilde{d}_{j-1}
, and the paths in
\hat{\mathcal{P}}
cause congestion at most
4\eta_{j-1}
in
H^{\prime}
.

We now use the algorithm from
3.1

with the collection
\hat{\mathcal{P}}
of paths, and
the embedding
{\mathcal{P}}
of graph
H^{\prime}=\bigcup_{H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}}H_{i}
into
H
, in order to compute a set
{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}
of paths in graph
H
, routing every vertex
\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq q}Y_{i}
to some vertex of
\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq q}S(H_{i})
, such that, for every vertex
x\in\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq q}S(H_{i})
, exactly
k(x)
paths of
\hat{\mathcal{P}}
terminate at
x
.
The algorithm ensures that the length of every path in
\hat{\mathcal{P}}
is at most
\tilde{d}_{j-1}\cdot d^{\prime}
, and the paths in
\hat{\mathcal{P}}
cause congestion at most
4\eta_{j-1}\cdot N^{128\epsilon^{2}}
.

For every index
1\leq i^{\prime}\leq q^{\prime}
, we similarly compute a set
\hat{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}
of paths in graph
H^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}
, that route some vertices of
S(H_{i^{\prime}})
to vertices of
Y^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}
, so that for every vertex
y\in Y^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}
, exactly one path in
\hat{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}
terminates at
y
, and for every vertex
x\in S(H^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})
, exactly
k(x)
paths of
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}
originate at
x
.
We use the embedding
{\mathcal{P}}
of graph
H^{\prime}
into
H
exactly as before, in order to compute a set
{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}
of paths in graph
H
,
routing vertices of
\bigcup_{1\leq i^{\prime}\leq q^{\prime}}S(H^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})
to vertices of
\bigcup_{1\leq i^{\prime}\leq q^{\prime}}Y^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}
, such that, for every vertex
x\in\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq q}S(H_{i})
, exactly
k(x)
paths of
{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}
originate at
x
, and for every vertex
y\in\bigcup_{1\leq i^{\prime}\leq q^{\prime}}Y^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}
, exactly one path of
{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}
terminates at
y
. As before, we can ensure that the length of each every path in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}
is at most
\tilde{d}_{j-1}\cdot d^{\prime}
, and the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}
cause congestion at most
4\eta_{j-1}\cdot N^{128\epsilon^{2}}
in
H
.

By concatenating the paths of
{\mathcal{Q}}^{1},{\mathcal{Q}}^{0}
and
{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}
, we obtain the final set
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime\prime}
of paths, that defines a one-to-one routing between vertices of
\bigcup_{i=1}^{q}Y_{i}
and vertices of
\bigcup_{i^{\prime}=1}^{q^{\prime}}Y^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}
. The length of every path in
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime\prime}
is bounded by
2\tilde{d}_{j-1}\cdot d^{\prime}+d^{\prime}\leq 3\tilde{d}_{j-1}\cdot d^{\prime}
.

Recall that
\tilde{d}_{j-1}=2^{c(j-1)/\epsilon^{4}}
, while
d^{\prime}<\frac{1}{4}\cdot 2^{c/\epsilon^{4}}
from Inequality
5
. Therefore, we get that the length of every path in
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime\prime}
is at most:

3\tilde{d}_{j-1}\cdot d^{\prime}\leq 2^{c(j-1)/\epsilon^{4}}\cdot 2^{c/\epsilon^{4}}=2^{cj/\epsilon^{4}}=\tilde{d}_{j}.

The total congestion caused by the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime\prime}
is bounded by:

4N^{6}+8\eta_{j-1}\cdot N^{128\epsilon^{2}}=4N^{6}+8\cdot N^{6+256(j-1)\epsilon^{2}}\cdot N^{128\epsilon^{2}}\leq\frac{N^{6+256j\epsilon^{2}}}{2}=\frac{\eta_{j}}{2},

since
\eta_{j-1}=N^{6+256(j-1)\epsilon^{2}}
, and
N
is sufficiently large.

This completes the proof of
Lemma

6.7

7
APSP in Well-Connected Graphs – Proof of
Theorem

2.3

The goal of this section is to prove
Theorem

2.3
. We do so using the following theorem.

Theorem 7.1
.

There are large enough constants
c^{\prime},c^{\prime\prime}
, and a deterministic algorithm, whose input consists of:

•

a parameter
0<\epsilon<1/400
;

•

a pair
N
,
1\leq j\leq\left\lceil 1/\epsilon\right\rceil
of integers, such that
N
is sufficiently large, so that
\frac{N^{\epsilon^{4}}}{\log N}\geq 2^{128/\epsilon^{6}}
holds;

•

a graph
H
with
|V(H)|=N^{j}
; and

•

a level-
j
Hierarchical Support Structure for graph
H
, such that graph
H
is
(\eta_{j},\tilde{d}_{j})
-well-connected with respect to the set
S(H)
of vertices defined by the Hierarchical Support Structure.

Further, we assume that graph
H
undergoes an online sequence of less than
\Lambda_{j}=N^{j-8-300j\epsilon^{2}}
edge deletions. The algorithm maintains a set
S^{\prime}(H)\subseteq S(H)
of vertices of
H
, called
supported vertices
, such that, at the beginning of the algorithm,
S^{\prime}(H)=S(H)
, and over the course of the algorithm, vertices can leave
S^{\prime}(H)
but they may not join it. The algorithm ensures that
|S^{\prime}(H)|\geq\frac{N^{j}}{16^{j}}
holds over the course of the algorithm, and it supports short-path queries between supported vertices: given a pair
x,y\in S^{\prime}(H)
of vertices, return a path
P
connecting
x
to
y
in the current graph
H
, whose length is at most
d^{*}_{j}=2^{c^{\prime}j/\epsilon^{5}}
, in time
O(|E(P)|)
. The total update time of the algorithm is bounded by
2c^{\prime\prime}jN^{j+3}\cdot 2^{4c^{\prime}/\epsilon^{6}}+c^{\prime\prime}m\cdot N^{2}\cdot 2^{4c^{\prime}/\epsilon^{6}}
, where
m
is the number of edges in graph
H
at the beginning of the algorithm. (If
\Lambda_{j}\leq 1
, then the algorithm only needs to support short-path queries until the first edge deletion).

It is immediate to verify that
Theorem

2.3
follows from
Theorem

7.1
, by substituting
j=1/\epsilon
.
In the remainder of this section we prove
Theorem

7.1
.
The proof is by induction on
j
.

7.1
Base Case:
j\leq 8

We first consider the base case, where
j\leq 8
. In this case,
\Lambda_{j}\leq 1
holds, and so we only need to support short-path queries until the first edge deletion.

In this case, the level-
j
Hierarchical Support Structure for graph
H
defines a set
S(H)
of vertices, and, from
5.1
,
|V(H)\setminus S(H)|\leq|V(H)|\cdot\frac{4j}{N^{\epsilon^{4}}}
.
Since
j\leq\left\lceil 1/\epsilon\right\rceil
, and
N^{\epsilon^{4}}\geq 2^{128/\epsilon^{5}}
from the statement of
Theorem

7.1
, we get that
|S(H)|\geq|V(H)|/2=N^{j}/2
. We set
S^{\prime}(H)=S(H)
, and this set remains unchanged throughout the algorithm.

Recall that are guaranteed that graph
H
is
(\eta_{j},\tilde{d}_{j})
-well-connected with respect to
S(H)
, where
\tilde{d}_{j}=2^{cj/\epsilon^{4}}\leq\frac{2^{c^{\prime}j/\epsilon^{5}}}{2}=\frac{d^{*}_{j}}{2}
(if
c^{\prime}>c
), and
\eta_{j}=N^{6+256j\epsilon^{2}}
. In particular, for every pair
x,y\in S^{\prime}(H)
of supported vertices, there is a path of length at most
d^{*}_{j}/2
connecting
x
to
y
in
H
.
We let
s\in S(H)
be an arbitrary vertex, and we construct a BFS tree
\tau
rooted at vertex
s
, whose depth is bounded by
d^{*}_{j}/2
. Computing the tree takes time
O(|E(H)|)
. In order to respond to a short-path query between a pair
x,y
of vertices of
H
, we simply compute the unique simple path
P
connecting
x
to
y
in the tree
\tau
, which can be done in time
O(|E(P)|)
. Since the depth of the tree is bounded by
d^{*}_{j}/2
, the length of the path is at most
d^{*}_{j}
.

7.2
Step:
j>8

We assume that we are given a graph
H
with
|V(H)|=N^{j}
, together
level-
j
hierarchical support structure for graph
H
, whose associated collection of graphs is
{\mathcal{H}}=\left\{H_{1},\ldots,H_{r}\right\}
. Recall that
r=N-\left\lceil 2N^{1-\epsilon^{4}}\right\rceil
, and we are guaranteed that graph
H
is
(\eta_{j},\tilde{d}_{j})
-well-connected with respect to the set
S(H)=\bigcup_{H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}}S(H_{i})
of vertices. Additionally, the hierarchical support structure contains an embedding
{\mathcal{P}}
of the graph
H^{\prime}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{r}H_{i}
into graph
H
via paths of length at most
2^{64/\epsilon^{4}}
, that causes congestion at most
N^{128\epsilon^{2}}
. For every edge
e\in E(H^{\prime})
, we denote by
P(e)\in{\mathcal{P}}
the unique path that serves as the embedding of
e
in
G
.

Our algorithm will maintain a set
S^{\prime}(H)\subseteq S(H)
of supported vertices, where initially
S^{\prime}(H)=S(H)
. While vertices may leave set
S^{\prime}(H)
over the course of the algorithm, set
S(H)
remains unchanged.

Our algorithm recursively applies the algorithm from
Theorem

7.1
to each of the graphs in
{\mathcal{H}}
. When an edge
e\in E(H)
is deleted, then for all
1\leq i\leq r
, for every edge
e^{\prime}\in E(H_{i})
whose corresponding embedding path
P(e^{\prime})
contains
e
, we delete edge
e^{\prime}
from graph
H_{i}
. Since the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}
cause congestion at most
N^{128\epsilon^{2}}
, the deletion of a single edge from graph
H
may trigger the deletion of up to
N^{128\epsilon^{2}}
edges from graphs
H_{1},\ldots,H_{r}
overall. As the result of these edge deletions, the supported sets of vertices
S^{\prime}(H_{i})
that the algorithm from
Theorem

7.1
maintains recursively for each of the graphs
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}
may need to be updated. Once a graph
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}
undergoes
\left\lceil\Lambda_{j-1}\right\rceil
edge deletions, we say that it is
destroyed
. Once graph
H_{i}
is destroyed, the corresponding set
S^{\prime}(H_{i})
of vertices is set to
\emptyset
.

Our algorithm maintains a partition of the set
{\mathcal{H}}
of graphs into three subsets: set
{\mathcal{H}}^{D}
of
destroyed graphs
, set
{\mathcal{H}}^{I}
of
inactive graphs
, and set
{\mathcal{H}}^{A}
of active graphs.
Set
{\mathcal{H}}^{D}
contains all graphs that have been destroyed so far. Once a graph joins set
{\mathcal{H}}^{D}
, it remains in
{\mathcal{H}}^{D}
for the remainder of the algorithm.
We define the sets
{\mathcal{H}}^{I}
and
{\mathcal{H}}^{A}
of graphs later. We will ensure that the set
{\mathcal{H}}^{A}
of active graphs is decremental, and we will set
S^{\prime}(H)=\bigcup_{H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{A}}S^{\prime}(H_{i})
throughout the algorithm. Intuitively, we will maintain, for every active graph
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{A}
, an
ES-Tree
data structure that is rooted at the vertices of
S^{\prime}(H_{i})
.
We need the following simple observation bounding the number of graphs in
{\mathcal{H}}^{D}
.

Observation 7.2
.

Over the course of the algorithm,
|{\mathcal{H}}^{D}|<N/32
always holds.

Proof.

Assume otherwise, and consider the first time
t
during the algorithm when
|{\mathcal{H}}^{D}|\geq N/32
held.
Recall that a graph
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}
is destroyed once it undergoes
\left\lceil\Lambda_{j-1}\right\rceil
edge deletions. Therefore, at least
\frac{N}{32}\cdot\left\lceil\Lambda_{j-1}\right\rceil
edges have been deleted from
\bigcup_{H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}}E(H_{i})
by time
t
.
On the other hand, the deletion of a single edge from
H
may trigger the deletion of at most
N^{128\epsilon^{2}}
edges from graphs
H_{1},\ldots,H_{r}
. Therefore, the number of edges that have been deleted from
H
by time
t
is at least:

\frac{N}{32N^{128\epsilon^{2}}}\cdot\left\lceil\Lambda_{j-1}\right\rceil=\frac{N^{1-128\epsilon^{2}}}{32}\cdot\left\lceil N^{j-9-300(j-1)\epsilon^{2}}\right\rceil>N^{j-8-300j\epsilon^{2}}=\Lambda_{j},

a contradiction.

Consider now a graph
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}\setminus{\mathcal{H}}^{D}
at some time during the algorithm’s execution. For every vertex
s\in S^{\prime}(H_{i})
, we denote by
{\mathcal{G}}(s)
the set of all graphs
H_{i^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{H}}\setminus{\mathcal{H}}^{D}
, such that
S^{\prime}(H_{i^{\prime}})\cap B_{H}(s,d^{*}_{j}/32)\neq\emptyset
.
In other words, set
{\mathcal{G}}(s)
contains all graphs
H_{i^{\prime}}
that have not been destroyed yet, such that some vertex in the current set
S^{\prime}(H_{i^{\prime}})
of supported vertices is sufficiently close to
s
in the current graph
H
. Recall that the set
S^{\prime}(H_{i})
of vertices is decremental. The following observation will be useful for us.

Observation 7.3
.

Consider any time
t
during the algorithm’s execution. Let
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}\setminus{\mathcal{H}}^{D}
be a graph that has not been destroyed by time
t
, and let
s\in S^{\prime}(H_{i})
be any vertex in the current set of supported vertices for
H_{i}
. Then, since the beginning of the algorithm and until time
t
, the collection
{\mathcal{G}}(s)
of graphs has been decremental: that is, graphs may have left it, but no graph may have joined it since the beginning of the algorithm.

Proof.

Assume for contradiction that
H_{i^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{H}}
is some graph that did not belong to set
{\mathcal{G}}(s)
at time
t^{\prime}
, but belongs to set
{\mathcal{G}}(s)
at time
t^{\prime\prime}
, where
t^{\prime}<t^{\prime\prime}\leq t
.

From the definition, at time
t^{\prime\prime}
,
H_{i^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{H}}\setminus{\mathcal{H}}^{D}
held. Since graphs may leave set
{\mathcal{H}}\setminus{\mathcal{H}}^{D}
(when they are destroyed) but they may never join
{\mathcal{H}}\setminus{\mathcal{H}}^{D}
over the course of the algorithm, we get that at time
t^{\prime}
,
H_{i^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{H}}\setminus{\mathcal{H}}^{D}
held. Furthermore, from the definition, at time
t^{\prime\prime}
, some vertex
x\in S^{\prime}(H_{i^{\prime}})
belonged to
B_{H}(s,d^{*}_{j}/32)
. Since set
S^{\prime}(H_{i^{\prime}})
of vertices is decremental,
x\in S^{\prime}(H_{i^{\prime}})
held at time
t^{\prime}
. Since distances in graph
H
may only grow over time,
x\in B_{H}(s,d^{*}_{j}/32)
held at time
t^{\prime}
. Therefore,
S^{\prime}(H_{i^{\prime}})\cap B_{H}(s,d^{*}_{j}/32)\neq\emptyset
must have held at time
t^{\prime}
, and graph
H_{i^{\prime}}
must have belonged to
{\mathcal{G}}(s)
at time
t^{\prime}
, a contradiction.

Throughout the algorithm, the set
{\mathcal{H}}^{I}
of inactive graphs will only contain graphs
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}
that have not been destroyed yet, for which the following property holds:

P1.

For every vertex
s\in S^{\prime}(H_{i})
,
|{\mathcal{G}}(s)|\leq 7N/8
.

We note that it is possible that some graph
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}\setminus{\mathcal{H}}^{D}
has Property
P1
but is not added to
{\mathcal{H}}^{I}
.

The following observation shows that once property
P1
holds for some graph
H_{i}
, it will continue to hold until the algorithm terminates or the graph is destroyed.

Observation 7.4
.

Let
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}
be any graph, and assume that Property
P1
holds for
H_{i}
at some time
t
during the algorithm’s execution. Then Property
P1
holds for
H_{i}
from time
t
and until the algorithm terminates or until
H_{i}
is destroyed.

Proof.

Assume that Property
P1
holds for graph
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}
at some time
t
during the algorithm’s execution, and consider some time
t^{\prime}>t
during the algorithm’s execution. We assume that graph
H_{i}
is not destroyed at time
t^{\prime}
, and prove that Property
P1
continues to hold for
H_{i}
at time
t^{\prime}
. Indeed, consider any vertex
s
that lies in set
S^{\prime}(H_{i})
at time
t^{\prime}
. Since set
S^{\prime}(H_{i})
is decremental, vertex
s
lied in
S^{\prime}(H_{i})
at time
t
. Since Property
P1
held for graph
H_{i}
at time
t
,
|{\mathcal{G}}(s)|\leq 7N/8
held at time
t
. From
7.3
, set
{\mathcal{G}}(s)
is decremental, so
|{\mathcal{G}}(s)|
may not grow between time
t
and time
t^{\prime}
. Therefore,
|{\mathcal{G}}(s)|\leq 7N/8
holds at time
t^{\prime}
. We conclude that Property
P1
continues to hold for
H_{i}
at time
t^{\prime}
.

To summarize, over the course of the algorithm, we maintain a partition of the collection
{\mathcal{H}}
of graphs into three subsets: the set
{\mathcal{H}}^{D}
of destroyed graphs; the set
{\mathcal{H}}^{I}
of inactive graphs; and the set
{\mathcal{H}}^{A}
of all remaining graphs, that are called
active graphs
. We ensure that every graph in
{\mathcal{H}}^{I}
has Property
P1
. We also ensure that the set
{\mathcal{H}}^{A}
of active graphs is decremental – graphs may leave it but they may not join it over time.
The following claim bounds the cardinality of the collection
{\mathcal{H}}^{I}
of graphs.

Claim 7.5
.

Over the course of the algorithm,
|{\mathcal{H}}^{I}|\leq N/32
always holds.

Proof.

Assume otherwise, and consider the first time
t
during the algorithm when
|{\mathcal{H}}^{I}|>N/32
held.
We will construct two large sets
T_{1},T_{2}
of vertices, so that the distance between the vertices of
T_{1}
and the vertices of
T_{2}
is large in the current graph
H
. We will then reach a contradiction by using the facts that, at the beginning of the algorithm, graph
H
was well-connected with respect to
S(H)
, and that the number of edges that were deleted from
H
is relatively small.

Recall that
|{\mathcal{H}}|=r=N-\left\lceil 2N^{1-\epsilon^{4}}\right\rceil\geq 63N/64
, since
N^{\epsilon^{4}}\geq 2^{128/\epsilon^{5}}
from the statement of
Theorem

7.1
. Additionally, from
7.2
,
|{\mathcal{H}}^{D}|<N/32
holds at time
t
. Therefore, at time
t
,
|{\mathcal{H}}\setminus{\mathcal{H}}^{D}|\geq\frac{63N}{64}-\frac{N}{32}\geq\frac{61N}{64}
.
Let
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}\subseteq{\mathcal{H}}\setminus{\mathcal{H}}^{D}
be a collection of graphs that is obtained as follows. We start with
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{H}}\setminus{\mathcal{H}}^{D}
. We then consider the graphs of
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}
one by one, starting with the graphs of
{\mathcal{H}}^{A}
. If, when graph
H_{i}
is considered,
|\tilde{\mathcal{H}}|>\left\lceil\frac{61N}{64}\right\rceil
holds, then we discard
H_{i}
from set
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}
. Otherwise, we terminate the algorithm with the final collection
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}
of graphs, whose cardinality must be
\left\lceil\frac{61N}{64}\right\rceil
. Notice that, if any graph of
{\mathcal{H}}^{A}
lies in
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}
, then
{\mathcal{H}}^{I}\subseteq\tilde{\mathcal{H}}
.
Recall that, from the induction hypothesis, for every graph
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}\setminus{\mathcal{H}}^{D}
,
|S^{\prime}(H_{i})|\geq\frac{N^{j-1}}{16^{j-1}}
holds throughout the algorithm.
We construct a set
T
of vertices as follows. For every graph
H_{i}\in\tilde{\mathcal{H}}
, we let
S^{\prime\prime}(H_{i})
be an arbitrary collection of
\left\lceil\frac{N^{j-1}}{16^{j-1}}\right\rceil
vertices that lie in set
S^{\prime}(H_{i})
at time
t
. We then let
T=\bigcup_{H_{i}\in\tilde{\mathcal{H}}}S^{\prime\prime}(H_{i})
. Clearly:

|T|=\left\lceil\frac{61N}{64}\right\rceil\cdot\left\lceil\frac{N^{j-1}}{16^{j-1}}\right\rceil.

Intuitively, we would like to apply Procedure
\mathsf{ProcSeparate}
from
Lemma

3.10
to graph
H
, set
T
of terminals, and distance parameters
\Delta=64/\epsilon^{2}
and
d=\frac{d^{*}_{j}}{64\Delta}
, in order to compute two large subsets
T_{1},T_{2}\subseteq T
of vertices with
\mbox{\sf dist}_{H}(T_{1},T_{2})\geq d
. However, the procedure may instead return a single vertex
s\in T
, for which the ball
B_{H}(s,d^{*}_{j}/64)
contains many vertices of
T
. In the next observation we show that this is impossible, that is, for every vertex
s\in T
,
|B_{H}(s,d^{*}_{j}/64)\cap T|
is sufficiently small.

Observation 7.6
.

At time
t
, for every vertex
s\in T
,
|B_{H}(s,d^{*}_{j}/64)\cap T|<|T|\cdot\left(1-\frac{1}{256}\right)
holds.

Proof.

Consider some vertex
s\in T
, and denote
B=B_{H}(s,d^{*}_{j}/64)
.
Let
T^{\prime}=\bigcup_{H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{I}\cap\tilde{\mathcal{H}}}S^{\prime\prime}(H_{i})
. Assume first that
B
does not contain any vertex of
T^{\prime}
. Notice that in this case, at least one graph of
{\mathcal{H}}^{A}
lies in
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}
, and so
{\mathcal{H}}^{I}\subseteq\tilde{\mathcal{H}}
.
Since we have assumed that
|{\mathcal{H}}^{I}|>\frac{N}{32}
, we get that:

|\tilde{\mathcal{H}}\setminus{\mathcal{H}}^{I}|\leq|\tilde{\mathcal{H}}|-|{\mathcal{H}}^{I}|\leq\left\lceil\frac{61N}{64}\right\rceil-\frac{N}{32}\leq\left\lceil\frac{61N}{64}\right\rceil\cdot\left(1-\frac{1}{61}\right)\leq\left\lceil\frac{61N}{64}\right\rceil\cdot\left(1-\frac{1}{256}\right).

Therefore:

|B\cap T|\leq|T|-|T^{\prime}|\leq|\tilde{\mathcal{H}}\setminus{\mathcal{H}}^{I}|\cdot\left\lceil\frac{N^{j-1}}{16^{j-1}}\right\rceil\leq\left\lceil\frac{61N}{64}\right\rceil\cdot\left(1-\frac{1}{256}\right)\cdot\left\lceil\frac{N^{j-1}}{16^{j-1}}\right\rceil\leq|T|\cdot\left(1-\frac{1}{256}\right).

Assume now that
B
contains at least one vertex of
T^{\prime}
, and let
s^{\prime}
be any such vertex. Note that
B=B_{H}(s,d^{*}_{j}/64)\subseteq B_{H}(s^{\prime},d^{*}_{j}/32)
. Observe that
s^{\prime}
must lie in the current set
S^{\prime}(H_{i})
of supported vertices of some graph
H_{i}
that currently lies in
{\mathcal{H}}^{I}
. From Property
P1
,
|{\mathcal{G}}(s^{\prime})|\leq\frac{7N}{8}
. Let
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}(s^{\prime})={\mathcal{H}}\setminus{\mathcal{G}}(s^{\prime})
, so
|{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}(s^{\prime})|\geq\frac{N}{8}
.

From our definitions, for every graph
H_{i^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}(s^{\prime})
,
S^{\prime}(H_{i^{\prime}})\cap B_{H}(s^{\prime},d^{*}_{j}/32)=\emptyset
, and therefore,
S^{\prime}(H_{i^{\prime}})\cap B=\emptyset
.

Let
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}=\tilde{\mathcal{H}}\cap{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}(s^{\prime})
. On the one hand, since
|\tilde{\mathcal{H}}|=\left\lceil\frac{61N}{64}\right\rceil
, and
|{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}(s^{\prime})|\geq\frac{N}{8}
, while
|{\mathcal{H}}|=N
, we get that
|\tilde{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}|\geq\frac{5N}{64}\geq\left\lceil\frac{61N}{64}\right\rceil\cdot\frac{1}{256}
. On the other hand, the vertices of
\bigcup_{H_{i}\in\tilde{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}}S^{\prime\prime}(H_{i})
may not lie in set
B
. Therefore:

|B\cap T|\leq\left(|\tilde{\mathcal{H}}|-|\tilde{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}|\right)\cdot\left\lceil\frac{N^{j-1}}{16^{j-1}}\right\rceil\leq\left\lceil\frac{61N}{64}\right\rceil\cdot\left(1-\frac{1}{256}\right)\cdot\left\lceil\frac{N^{j-1}}{16^{j-1}}\right\rceil\leq|T|\cdot\left(1-\frac{1}{256}\right).

We apply Algorithm
\mathsf{ProcSeparate}
from
Lemma

3.10
to graph
H
, set
T
of terminals, distance parameters
\Delta=64/\epsilon^{2}
, and
d=\frac{d^{*}_{j}}{64\Delta}
, and parameter
\alpha=\left(1-\frac{1}{256}\right)
. From
7.6
, the algorithm may not return a vertex
s\in T
with
|B_{H}(s,\Delta\cdot d)\cap T|=|B_{H}(s,d^{*}_{j}/64)\cap T|>\alpha\cdot|T|
. Therefore, it must compute two subsets
T_{1},T_{2}
of vertices with
|T_{1}|=|T_{2}|
, such that
|T_{1}|\geq\frac{|T|^{1-64/\Delta}}{256}
, and for every pair
s\in T_{1},s^{\prime}\in T_{2}
of terminals,
\mbox{\sf dist}_{H}(s,s^{\prime})\geq d
. We will now exploit the facts that graph
H
was well-connected with respect to set
S(H)
of vertices at the beginning of the algorithm, and that relatively few edges were deleted from
H
, in order to reach a contradiction.

Observe first that:

|T_{1}|\geq\frac{|T|^{1-\epsilon^{2}}}{256}\geq\frac{1}{256}\cdot\left(\frac{61\cdot N^{j}}{4\cdot 16^{j}}\right)^{1-\epsilon^{2}}\geq\frac{N^{j(1-\epsilon^{2})}}{1024\cdot 16^{j}}.

On the other hand:

d=\frac{d^{*}_{j}}{64\Delta}=\frac{2^{c^{\prime}j/\epsilon^{5}}\cdot\epsilon^{2}}{2^{12}}>2^{cj/\epsilon^{4}}=\tilde{d}_{j}.

Let
H^{(0)}
denote the graph
H
at the beginning of the algorithm, and let
H^{(t)}
denote graph
H
at time
t
.
Recall that, at the beginning of the algorithm, graph
H^{(0)}
was
(\eta_{j},\tilde{d}_{j})
-well-connected with respect to the set
S(H)=\bigcup_{H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}}S(H_{i})
of vertices. Since the sets
S^{\prime}(H_{i})
of vertices for graphs
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}
are decremental, and since
S^{\prime}(H_{i})=S(H_{i})
at the beginning of the algorithm for each such graph, we get that
T\subseteq S(H)
held at the beginning of the algorithm. Therefore, there was a collection
{\mathcal{P}}(T_{1},T_{2})
of paths in graph
H^{(0)}
, routing every vertex of
T_{1}
to a distinct vertex of
T_{2}
, such that the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}(T_{1},T_{2})
cause congestion at most
\eta_{j}=N^{6+256j\epsilon^{2}}
, and the length of every path is at most
\tilde{d}_{j}
.

Let
E^{\prime}
be the set of edges that have been deleted from graph
H
by time
t
. Note that, in graph
H^{(t)}
, no path of length at most
\tilde{d}_{j}
connecting a vertex of
T_{1}
to a vertex of
T_{2}
exists. Therefore, set
E^{\prime}
must contain at least one edge from every path in
{\mathcal{P}}(T_{1},T_{2})
. We conclude that:

|E^{\prime}|\geq\frac{|T_{1}|}{\eta_{j}}\geq\frac{N^{j(1-\epsilon^{2})}}{1024\cdot 16^{j}\cdot N^{6+256j\epsilon^{2}}}=\frac{N^{j-257j\epsilon^{2}-6}}{1024\cdot 16^{j}}>N^{j-8-257j\epsilon^{2}}\geq\Lambda_{j},

since
j\leq\left\lceil 1/\epsilon\right\rceil
,
\epsilon\leq 1/400
, and
N^{\epsilon}>2^{8/\epsilon}
from the statement of
Theorem

7.1
. This is a contradiction, since fewer than
\Lambda_{j}
edges may be deleted from
H
.

From
7.2
and
7.5
, throughout the algorithm,
|{\mathcal{H}}^{I}|+|{\mathcal{H}}^{D}|\leq N/16
holds. Since
|{\mathcal{H}}|=r=N-\left\lceil 2N^{1-\epsilon^{4}}\right\rceil\geq 63N/64
, we get that, throughout the algorithm:

|{\mathcal{H}}^{A}|\geq|{\mathcal{H}}|-|{\mathcal{H}}^{I}|-|{\mathcal{H}}^{D}|\geq\frac{63N}{64}-\frac{N}{16}\geq\frac{59N}{64}.

(10)

The set
S^{\prime}(H)
that we maintain throughout the algorithm is defined to be:
S^{\prime}(H)=\bigcup_{H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{A}}S^{\prime}(H_{i})
. Since, for every graph
H_{i}
, set
S^{\prime}(H_{i})
of vertices is decremental, and since the collection
{\mathcal{H}}^{A}
of graphs is decremental, we get that the set
S^{\prime}(H)
of vertices is decremental as well. It is also easy to see that
S^{\prime}(H)=S(H)=\bigcup_{H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}}S(H)
hods at the beginning of the algorithm. Since, from Inequality
10
,
|{\mathcal{H}}^{A}|\geq\frac{59N}{64}
holds throughout the algorithm, and since, from the induction hypothesis, for every graph
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{A}
,
|S^{\prime}(H_{i})|\geq\frac{N^{j-1}}{16^{j-1}}
holds, we get that, throughout the algorithm:

|S^{\prime}(H)|\geq|{\mathcal{H}}^{A}|\cdot\frac{N^{j-1}}{16^{j-1}}\geq\frac{59N}{64}\cdot\frac{N^{j-1}}{16^{j-1}}\geq\frac{N^{j}}{16^{j}},

as required.

7.2.1
Data Structures and Initialization

Consider a graph
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}
. Note that, as part of the level-
j
hierarchical support structure for
H
, we are given a level-
(j-1)
hierarchical support structure for
H_{i}
, and we are guaranteed that
H_{i}
is
(\eta_{j-1},\tilde{d}_{j-1})
-well-connected with respect to
S(H_{i})
.
Therefore, from the induction hypothesis, we can apply the algorithm from
Theorem

7.1
with parameter
(j-1)
to graph
H_{i}
, and the corresponding level-
(j-1)
hierarchical support structure. Parameters
N
and
\epsilon
remain unchanged. We denote the corresponding data structure by
{\mathcal{D}}_{j-1}(H_{i})
.

As part of the initialization procedure, for every graph
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}
, we initialize the corresponding data structure
{\mathcal{D}}_{j-1}(H_{i})
.

Our algorithm also maintains, for every edge
e\in E(H)
, a list
L(e)
of edges
e^{\prime}\in\bigcup_{H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}}E(H_{i})
, such that the path
P(e^{\prime})
, contains
e
. Here,
P(e^{\prime})
is the path in the embedding
{\mathcal{P}}
of
\bigcup_{H\in{\mathcal{H}}}H^{\prime}
into
H
, that serves as the embedding of edge
e^{\prime}
.
Together with this list, we maintain a pointer from
e
to each such edge
e^{\prime}
in its corresponding graph
H_{i}
, and a pointer in the opposite direction. We initialize the lists
L(e)
for edges
e\in E(H)
at the beginning of the algorithm.

We also initialize
{\mathcal{H}}^{A}={\mathcal{H}}
,
{\mathcal{H}}^{I}={\mathcal{H}}^{D}=\emptyset
, and
S^{\prime}(H)=S(H)
.

Lastly, for every graph
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}
, we initialize an
ES-Tree
data structure, whose corresponding tree is denoted by
\tau_{i}
, that, intuitively, is rooted at the set
S^{\prime}(H_{i})
of vertices. Specifically, in order to construct data structure
\tau_{i}
, we let
\tilde{G}_{i}
be a graph that is obtained from
H
, after we add a source vertex
s_{i}
to it, which connects to every vertex in
S^{\prime}(H_{i})
with an edge. We then let
\tau_{i}
be an
ES-Tree
data structure in graph
\tilde{G}_{i}
, rooted at vertex
s_{i}
, with depth bound
d^{*}_{j}/8+1
.
When an edge is deleted from
H
, we will also delete it from
\tilde{G}_{i}
, and update the
ES-Tree
data structure
\tau_{i}
accordingly. When a vertex
s
is deleted from set
S^{\prime}(H_{i})
, we will delete the edge
(s_{i},s)
from graph
\tilde{G}_{i}
, and update
\tau_{i}
accordingly.

We maintain, for every pair of graphs
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{A}
and
H_{i^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{H}}\setminus{\mathcal{H}}^{D}
, a counter
n_{i,i^{\prime}}
, that counts the number of vertices of
S^{\prime}(H_{i^{\prime}})
that lie at distance at most
d^{*}_{j}/32+1
from
s_{i}
in tree
\tau_{i}
. Note that, if
n_{i,i^{\prime}}=0
, then for every vertex
s\in S^{\prime}(H_{i})
,
S^{\prime}(H_{i^{\prime}})\cap B_{H}(s,d^{*}_{j}/32)=\emptyset
. In other words,
H_{i^{\prime}}\not\in{\mathcal{G}}(s)
holds for all
s\in S^{\prime}(H_{i})
. We also maintain a counter
\tilde{n}_{i}
, whose value is the number of graphs
H_{i^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{H}}\setminus{\mathcal{H}}^{D}
, with
n_{i,i^{\prime}}>0
.
We can initialize the values
n_{i,i^{\prime}}
for every pair
H_{i},H_{i^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{H}}
of graphs, and the counters in
\left\{\tilde{n}_{i}\right\}_{H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}}
at the beginning of the algorithm. The time that is required in order to do so is subsumed by the time required to initialize the
ES-Tree
data structures.
We need the following simple observation.

Observation 7.7
.

Let
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}
be a graph. Assume that at some time
t
in the algorithm’s execution,
H_{i}\not\in{\mathcal{H}}^{D}
holds, and
\tilde{n}_{i}\leq 7N/8
. Then graph
H_{i}
has Property
P1
at time
t
.

Proof.

Assume otherwise. Then at time
t
, there is some vertex
s\in S^{\prime}(H_{i})
with
|{\mathcal{G}}(s)|>7N/8
. If
H_{i^{\prime}}
is a graph that lies in
{\mathcal{G}}(s)
at time
t
, then at least one vertex of
S^{\prime}(H_{i^{\prime}})
must lie in
B_{H}(s,d^{*}_{j}/32)
at time
t
, and so
n_{i,i^{\prime}}>0
must hold. But then
\tilde{n}_{i}>7N/8
must hold at time
t
, a contradiction.

Throughout the algorithm’s execution, whenever, for some graph
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{A}
, the value of counter
\tilde{n}_{i}
becomes at most
7N/8
, we move graph
H_{i}
from
{\mathcal{H}}^{A}
to
{\mathcal{H}}^{I}
. Lastly, we need the following simple observation.

Observation 7.8
.

Let
H_{i},H_{i^{\prime}}
be any pair of graphs that lie in set
{\mathcal{H}}^{A}
at some time
t
during the algorithm’s execution. Let
s
be any vertex that lies in
S^{\prime}(H_{i})
at time
t
, and let
s^{\prime}
be any vertex that lies in
S^{\prime}(H_{i^{\prime}})
at time
t
. Then
\mbox{\sf dist}_{H}(s,s^{\prime})\leq d^{*}_{j}/8
at time
t
.

Proof.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}_{i}
be the set of all graphs
H_{i^{\prime\prime}}\in{\mathcal{H}}\setminus{\mathcal{H}}^{D}
with
n_{i,i^{\prime\prime}}>0
at time
t
. Since
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{A}
at time
t
,
|{\mathcal{G}}_{i}|\geq 7N/8
must hold. Similarly, let
{\mathcal{G}}_{i^{\prime}}
be the set of all graphs
H_{i^{\prime\prime}}\in{\mathcal{H}}\setminus{\mathcal{H}}^{D}
with
n_{i^{\prime},i^{\prime\prime}}>0
at time
t
. As before,
|{\mathcal{G}}_{i^{\prime}}|\geq 7N/8
. Therefore, there is some graph
H_{i^{\prime\prime}}\in{\mathcal{G}}_{i}\cap{\mathcal{G}}_{i^{\prime}}
.

In the remainder of this proof, whenever we refer to vertex sets
S^{\prime}(H_{i}),S^{\prime}(H_{i^{\prime}}),S^{\prime}(H_{i^{\prime\prime}})
, or to graphs
H_{i},H_{i^{\prime}},H_{i^{\prime\prime}},H
, we mean the corresponding sets of vertices or the corresponding graphs at time
t
.

Since, at time
t
,
n_{i,i^{\prime\prime}}>0
, there is some vertex
x\in S^{\prime}(H_{i^{\prime\prime}})
, such that the distance from
s_{i}
to
x
in tree
\tau_{i}
is at most
d^{*}_{j}/32+1
. Therefore, there is some vertex
x^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}(H_{i})
, such that
\mbox{\sf dist}_{H}(x,x^{\prime})\leq d^{*}_{j}/32
. Let
Q
be a path of length at most
d^{*}_{j}/32
connecting
x
to
x^{\prime}
in
H
. From a similar reasoning, there is a pair of vertices
y\in S^{\prime}(H_{i^{\prime\prime}})
and
y^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}(H_{i^{\prime}})
, such that
\mbox{\sf dist}_{H}(y,y^{\prime})\leq d^{*}_{j}/32
. Let
Q^{\prime}
be a path of length at most
d^{*}_{j}/32
connecting
y
to
y^{\prime}
in
H
.

From the induction hypothesis, there is a path
P_{1}
of length at most
d^{*}_{j-1}
connecting
s
to
x^{\prime}
in graph
H_{i}
. Recall that we are given an embedding
{\mathcal{P}}
of the edges of
\bigcup_{H_{a}\in{\mathcal{H}}}E(H_{a})
into
H
, where the length of every path in
{\mathcal{P}}
is at most
2^{64/\epsilon^{4}}
. From
3.1
, there is a path
P_{1}^{\prime}
in graph
H
, connecting
s
to
x^{\prime}
, whose length is at most
2^{64/\epsilon^{4}}\cdot d^{*}_{j-1}
.

Using similar reasonings, there is a path
P_{2}^{\prime}
in graph
H
connecting
x
to
y
of length at most
2^{64/\epsilon^{4}}\cdot d^{*}_{j-1}
, and a path
P_{3}^{\prime}
in graph
H
connecting
y^{\prime}
to
s^{\prime}
, of length at most
2^{64/\epsilon^{4}}\cdot d^{*}_{j-1}
. By concatenating the paths
P_{1}^{\prime},Q,P_{2}^{\prime},Q^{\prime},P_{3}^{\prime}
, we obtain a path in graph
H
, connecting
s
to
s^{\prime}
, whose length is bounded by:

\frac{d^{*}_{j}}{16}+3\cdot 2^{64/\epsilon^{4}}\cdot d^{*}_{j-1}=\frac{d^{*}_{j}}{16}+3\cdot 2^{64/\epsilon^{4}}\cdot 2^{c^{\prime}(j-1)/\epsilon^{5}}\leq\frac{d^{*}_{j}}{16}+\frac{2^{c^{\prime}j/\epsilon^{5}}}{16}\leq\frac{d^{*}_{j}}{8}

(since
d^{*}_{j}=2^{c^{\prime}j/\epsilon^{5}}
).

Next, we describe an algorithm for updating the data structures after each edge deletion, followed by the analysis of the total update time of the algorithm. We then conclude with an algorithm for responding to queries.

7.2.2
Maintaining the Data Structures

For each graph
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}
, our algorithm will only maintain
ES-Tree
data structure
\tau_{i}
, together with the corresponding counters
\tilde{n}_{i}
and
n_{i,i^{\prime}}
for all
H_{i^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{H}}\setminus{\mathcal{H}}^{D}
, as long as graph
H_{i}
lies in
{\mathcal{H}}^{A}
. Once graph
H_{i}
is removed from
{\mathcal{H}}^{A}
, we no longer maintain these data structures.

We now describe an algorithm for updating the data structures following the deletion of an edge
e
from graph
H
. The algorithm, called
\operatorname{DeleteEdge}(e)
, is straightforward and it is summarized in
Figure

1
.

When an edge
e
is deleted from graph
H
, we consider every edge
e^{\prime}\in L(e)
(that is, edges
e^{\prime}\in\bigcup_{H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}}E(H_{i})
, whose embedding path
P(e^{\prime})
contains edge
e
). We assume that
e^{\prime}\in E(H_{i})
, and that
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}\setminus{\mathcal{H}}^{D}
(if
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{D}
, no further action is required for processing edge
e^{\prime}
). We then delete edge
e^{\prime}
from graph
H_{i}
and update the corresponding data structure
{\mathcal{D}}_{j-1}(H_{i})
. As a result, we obtain a set
X_{i}
(that may be empty) of vertices that have been deleted from
S^{\prime}(H_{i})
. We also update lists
L(e^{\prime\prime})
of edges
e^{\prime\prime}\in E(H)
that contain
e^{\prime}
, to remove
e^{\prime}
from these lists.

If the number of edges deleted so far from
H_{i}
reaches at least
\left\lceil\Lambda_{j-1}\right\rceil
, then graph
H_{i}
is destroyed. We add the graph to set
{\mathcal{H}}^{D}
, and we update all counters
n_{i^{\prime},i}
and
\tilde{n}_{i^{\prime}}
for graphs
H_{i^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{A}
as needed. Otherwise, we process every vertex
s\in X_{i}
one by one. If
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{A}
, then we delete edge
(s_{i},s)
from graph
\tilde{G}_{i}
, and the corresponding
ES-Tree
data structure
\tau_{i}
. As the result, some distances in tree
\tau_{i}
may have increased, and we need to update all counters in
\left\{n_{i,i^{\prime}}\right\}_{H_{i^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{H}}\setminus{\mathcal{H}}^{D}}
, as well as counter
\tilde{n}_{i}
accordingly.
Additionally, for every graph
H_{i^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{A}
, if the distance from
s_{i}
to
s
in tree
\tau_{i}
was bounded by
d^{*}_{j}/32+1
, then we need to decrease
n_{i^{\prime},i}
by
1
(as vertex
s
no longer lies in
S^{\prime}(H_{i})
), and if needed, we need to update counter
\tilde{n}_{i^{\prime}}
.

Once we finish processing every edge
e^{\prime}\in L(e)
, we also need to delete edge
e
from every graph
\tilde{G}_{i^{\prime}}
, where
H_{i^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{A}
, and the corresponding
ES-Tree
data structure
\tau_{i^{\prime}}
. As before, this may increase some distances in tree
\tau_{i^{\prime}}
, and we may need to update counters
\left\{n_{i^{\prime},i^{\prime\prime}}\right\}_{H_{i^{\prime\prime}}\in{\mathcal{H}}\setminus{\mathcal{H}}^{D}},\tilde{n}_{i^{\prime}}
accordingly. Lastly, we consider every graph
H_{i^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{A}
in turn. If
\tilde{n}_{i^{\prime}}\leq 7N/8
holds for any such graph, then we move it from
{\mathcal{H}}^{A}
to
{\mathcal{H}}^{I}
.

Algorithm
\operatorname{DeleteEdge}(e)

1.

For every edge
e^{\prime}\in L(e)
, such that the graph
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}
containing
e^{\prime}
lies in
{\mathcal{H}}\setminus{\mathcal{H}}^{D}
do:

(a)

Delete
e^{\prime}
from
H_{i}
and update the corresponding data structure
{\mathcal{D}}_{j-1}(H_{i})
. Let
X_{i}
be the set of vertices that were deleted from
S^{\prime}(H_{i})
as the result of this update.

(b)

For every edge
e^{\prime\prime}\in E(H)
with
e^{\prime}\in L(e^{\prime\prime})
, delete
e^{\prime}
from
L(e^{\prime\prime})
.

(c)

If the number of edges deleted so far from
H_{i}
becomes at least
\left\lceil\Lambda_{j-1}\right\rceil
:

i.

Add graph
H_{i}
to
{\mathcal{H}}^{D}
and remove it from the set
{\mathcal{H}}^{A}
or
{\mathcal{H}}^{I}
to which it belonged.

ii.

For every graph
H_{i^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{A}
, if
n_{i^{\prime},i}>0
, set
n_{i^{\prime},i}
to
0
and decrease
\tilde{n}_{i^{\prime}}
by
1
.

(d)

Otherwise: for every vertex
s\in X_{i}
do:

i.

If
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{A}
, delete edge
(s_{i},s)
from graph
\tilde{G}_{i}
and update the
ES-Tree

\tau_{i}
, together with counters
\left\{n_{i,i^{\prime}}\right\}_{H_{i^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{H}}\setminus{\mathcal{H}}^{D}},\tilde{n}_{i}
accordingly.

ii.

For every graph
H_{i^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{A}
, if
\mbox{\sf dist}_{\tau_{i^{\prime}}}(s_{i^{\prime}},s)\leq d^{*}_{j}/32+1
, decrease
n_{i^{\prime},i}
by
1
. If
n_{i^{\prime},i}
decreases from
1
to
0
, decrease
\tilde{n}_{i^{\prime}}
by
1
.

2.

For every graph
H_{i^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{A}
, delete edge
e
from graph
\tilde{G}_{i^{\prime}}
, and update the
ES-Tree

\tau_{i^{\prime}}
, together with counters
\left\{n_{i^{\prime},i^{\prime\prime}}\right\}_{H_{i^{\prime\prime}}\in{\mathcal{H}}\setminus{\mathcal{H}}^{D}},\tilde{n}_{i^{\prime}}
accordingly.

3.

For every graph
H_{i^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{A}
, if
\tilde{n}_{i^{\prime}}\leq 7N/8
holds, move
H_{i^{\prime}}
from
{\mathcal{H}}^{A}
to
{\mathcal{H}}^{I}
.

Figure 1:
Algorithm
\operatorname{DeleteEdge}(e)

It is easy to verify that the algoritm maintains all data structures correctly, and, from
7.7
, when graph
H_{i}
is added to
{\mathcal{H}}^{I}
, Property
P1
holds for it. From
7.4
, once
H_{i}
is added to
{\mathcal{H}}^{I}
, Property
P1
continues to hold for it until the end of the algorithm, or until
H_{i}
is added to
{\mathcal{H}}^{D}
. From the above discussion, we are guaranteed that, throughout the algorithm,
|{\mathcal{H}}^{A}|\neq\emptyset
.

7.2.3
Analysis of Total Update Time

Let
m
denote the number of edges in graph
H
at the beginning of the algorithm.
Recall that every edge
e\in E(H)
participates in at most
N^{128\epsilon^{2}}
paths in
{\mathcal{P}}
. Therefore, the length of the list
L(e)
is bounded by
N^{128\epsilon^{2}}
. Every edge of
\bigcup_{H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}}E(H_{i})
may be added at most once to list
L(e)
when the data structure is initialized, and subsequently it may be deleted at most once from
L(e)
. Therefore, the total time required to maintain the lists
L(e)
for all edges
e\in E(H)
is at most
O(m\cdot N^{128\epsilon^{2}})
.

Consider now some graph
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}
, and denote by
m_{i}
the number of edges in
H_{i}
at the beginning of the algorithm.
Recall that, from the definition of the hierarchical support structure,

|E(H_{i})|\leq N^{j-1+32\epsilon^{2}}
. From the induction hypothesis, maintaining data structure
{\mathcal{D}}_{j-1}(H_{i})
recursively takes time at most:

\begin{split}2c^{\prime\prime}(j-1)N^{j+2}\cdot 2^{4c^{\prime}/\epsilon^{6}}+c^{\prime\prime}|E(H_{i})|\cdot N^{2}\cdot 2^{4c^{\prime}/\epsilon^{6}}&\leq 2c^{\prime\prime}(j-1)N^{j+2}\cdot 2^{4c^{\prime}/\epsilon^{6}}+c^{\prime\prime}N^{j+1+32\epsilon^{2}}\cdot 2^{4c^{\prime}/\epsilon^{6}}\\
&\leq c^{\prime\prime}(2j-1)N^{j+2}\cdot 2^{4c^{\prime}/\epsilon^{6}}.\end{split}

Since
|{\mathcal{H}}|=N
, the total update time needed in order to maintain these data structures for all graphs
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}
is bounded by
c^{\prime\prime}(2j-1)N^{j+3}\cdot 2^{4c^{\prime}/\epsilon^{5}}
.

Consider now some graph
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}
. The total update time that is needed in order to maintain
ES-Tree

\tau_{i}
is bounded by:

O(jm\cdot d^{*}_{j}\cdot\log N)\leq O(jm\cdot 2^{c^{\prime}j/\epsilon^{5}}\log N)\leq O(m\cdot 2^{4c^{\prime}/\epsilon^{6}}\log N),

since
d^{*}_{j}=2^{c^{\prime}j/\epsilon^{4}}
and
j\leq\left\lceil 1/\epsilon\right\rceil
.
We can initialize the counters
n_{i,i^{\prime}}
for all graphs
H_{i^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{H}}
and
\tilde{n}_{i}
without increasing this asymptotic running time. We can also perform updates to these counters in Step
1(d)i
within the same asymptotic running time. Since
|{\mathcal{H}}|=N
, this part of the algorithm takes total update time at most
O(N\cdot m\cdot 2^{4c^{\prime}/\epsilon^{6}}\log N)
.

Whenever a vertex
s
is deleted from a set
S^{\prime}(H_{i})
for any graph
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}
, we may need to update the counters
n_{i^{\prime},i}
and
\tilde{n}_{i^{\prime}}
for some graphs
H_{i^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{A}
. This can be done in time
O(N)
per vertex. Since a vertex may be deleted at most once from
\bigcup_{H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}}S^{\prime}(H_{i})
, these updates can be done in time
O(N^{j+1})
.

Lastly, every graph
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}
may be moved to set
{\mathcal{H}}^{D}
at most once over the course of the algorithm, at which time we may need to update counters
n_{i^{\prime},i}
and
\tilde{n}_{i^{\prime}}
for graphs
H_{i^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{A}
. This takes time
O(N)
per graph
H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}
, and
O(N^{2})
overall.

From the above discussion, the total update time of the algorithm is bounded by:

c^{\prime\prime}(2j-1)N^{j+3}\cdot 2^{4c^{\prime}/\epsilon^{6}}+O(N\cdot m\cdot 2^{4c^{\prime}/\epsilon^{6}}\log N)+O(N^{j+1})\leq 2c^{\prime\prime}jN^{j+3}\cdot 2^{4c^{\prime}/\epsilon^{6}}+c^{\prime\prime}m\cdot N^{2}\cdot 2^{4c^{\prime}/\epsilon^{6}}.

7.2.4
Response to Queries

In this subsection we describe an algorithm for responding to a short-path query between a pair
x,y\in S^{\prime}(H)
of vertices. Recall that the goal is to return a path
P
of length at most
d^{*}_{j}
connecting
x
to
y
in
H
, in time
O(|E(P)|)
.

Recall that
S^{\prime}(H)=\bigcup_{H_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{A}}S^{\prime}(H_{i})
. Therefore, there is a pair of graphs
H_{i},H_{i^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{A}
(where possibly
H_{i}=H_{i^{\prime}}
), with
x\in S^{\prime}(H_{i})
and
y\in S^{\prime}(H_{i^{\prime}})
. From
7.8
,
\mbox{\sf dist}_{H}(x,y)\leq d^{*}_{j}/8
must hold. In particular, if we consider the
ES-Tree
data structure
\tau_{i}
, then
y\in V(\tau_{i})
must hold, and the distance from
y
to
s_{i}
in the tree must be at most
d^{*}_{j}/8+1
. Let
Q_{1}
be the path connecting
y
to
s_{i}
in tree
\tau_{i}
. We delete the last vertex on the path, and let
x^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}(H_{i})
be the new last vertex of
Q_{1}
. Using the induction hypothesis, we can compute a path
P^{\prime}
in graph
H_{i}
connecting
x
to
x^{\prime}
, whose length is at most
d^{*}_{j-1}
. Assume that the sequence of edges on path
Q^{\prime}
is
(e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{z})
. For all
1\leq z^{\prime}\leq z
, consider the path
P(e_{z^{\prime}})\in{\mathcal{P}}
that serves as the embedding of edge
z^{\prime}
into
H
. Recall that the length of the path is bounded by
2^{64/\epsilon^{4}}
. By concatenating the paths
P(e_{1}),\ldots,P(e_{z})
, we obtain a path
Q_{2}
in graph
H
, connecting
x
to
x^{\prime}
, whose length is at most
d^{*}_{j-1}\cdot 2^{64/\epsilon^{4}}
. Lastly, by concatenating paths
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
, we obtain a path
P
in graph
H
connecting
x
to
y
. The length of the path is bounded by:

d^{*}_{j-1}\cdot 2^{64/\epsilon^{4}}+\frac{d^{*}_{j}}{8}\leq 2^{c^{\prime}(j-1)/\epsilon^{5}}\cdot 2^{64/\epsilon^{4}}+\frac{2^{c^{\prime}j/\epsilon^{5}}}{8}\leq 2^{c^{\prime}j/\epsilon^{5}}=d^{*}_{j}.

since
d^{*}_{j}=2^{c^{\prime}j/\epsilon^{5}}
. It is easy to see that the algorithm can be implemented in time
O(|E(P)|)
.

8
APSP in Expanders – Proof of
Theorem

2.4

This section is dedicated to the proof of
Theorem

2.4
. We first prove the following lemma, that can be viewed as a weaker variation of
Theorem

2.4
, in the sense that it can only withstand a significantly shorter sequence of edge deletions.

Lemma 8.1
.

There is a deterministic algorithm whose input consists of
an
n
-vertex graph
G
with
|E(G)|=m
that is a
\varphi^{*}
-expander, for some
0<\varphi^{*}<1
, with maximum vertex degree at most
\Delta
, and a parameter
\frac{2}{(\log n)^{1/12}}<\epsilon<\frac{1}{400}
, such that
1/\epsilon
is an integer. We assume that graph
G
undergoes an online sequence of at most
\frac{n^{1-20\epsilon}(\varphi^{*})^{2}}{\Delta^{2}}
edge deletions. The algorithm
maintains a set
U\subseteq V(G)
of vertices, such that, for every integer
t>0
, after
t
edges are deleted from
G
,
|U|\leq\frac{4\Delta t}{\varphi^{*}}
holds.
Vertex set
U
is incremental, so vertices may join it but they may not leave it.
The algorithm also supports short-path queries: given a pair of vertices
x,y\in V(G)\setminus U
, return an
x
-
y
path
P
in the current graph
G
, of length at most
\frac{2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\cdot\Delta\cdot\log n}{\varphi^{*}}
, with query time
O(|E(P)|)
.
The total update time of the algorithm is
O\left(\frac{n^{1+O(\epsilon)}\cdot\Delta^{3}}{(\varphi^{*})^{2}}\right)
.

The proof of
Lemma

8.1
is deferred to
Section

8.1
. We now complete the proof of
Theorem

2.4
using it.
We partition the execution of our algorithm into phases.
Let
k^{\prime}=\left\lfloor\frac{n^{1-20\epsilon}\varphi^{2}}{2^{11}\cdot\Delta^{4}}\right\rfloor
.
The first phase lasts as long as the number of edges deleted from
G
via the input update sequence is at most
k^{\prime}
. Once
k^{\prime}
edges are deleted from graph
G
, the second phase begins. Each subsequent phase similarly lasts as long as at most
k^{\prime}
edges are deleted since the beginning of the phase, except for the last phase which may be shorter, if the input update sequence terminates before
k^{\prime}
edges are deleted from
G
since the beginning of the phase.
For all
i\geq 1
, we denote by
\Sigma_{i}
the sequence of edge deletions that graph
G
undergoes as part of the online input sequence of edge deletions in phase
i
, and we denote by
E_{i}
the set of edges that belong to
\Sigma_{i}
.
Since the total number of edges in the input sequence of edge deletions is bounded by
\frac{n\cdot\varphi^{2}}{2^{13}\Delta^{4}}
, we get that the number of phases is bounded by
\frac{n\cdot\varphi^{2}}{2^{13}\cdot\Delta^{4}\cdot k^{\prime}}\leq\frac{n^{20\epsilon}}{4}
.

We define another dynamic graph
G^{\prime}
. At the beginning of the algorithm, we set
G^{\prime}=G
. As the algorithm progresses, we will delete some edges from
G^{\prime}
. Specifically, at the end of every phase of the algorithm, we will define a set of edges to be deleted from graph
G^{\prime}
; we do not delete any edges from
G^{\prime}
as long as a phase progresses. We will run the algorithm from
Theorem

3.4
(expander pruning) on graph
G^{\prime}
, and we let
\tilde{U}
be the set of vertices of
G^{\prime}
that it maintains. We will ensure that the number of edges that are deleted from
G^{\prime}
over the course of the entire algorithm is bounded by
k=\frac{\varphi n}{16\Delta}
. Clearly,
k\leq\frac{\varphi\cdot|E(G)|}{10\Delta}
. From
Theorem

3.4
, we are guaranteed that, throughout the algorithm,
|\tilde{U}|\leq\frac{8k\Delta}{\varphi}\leq\frac{n}{2}
holds.

In order to execute the first phase, we let
H_{1}=G^{\prime}=G
, and we apply the algorithm from
Lemma

8.1
to graph
H_{1}
, and the online sequence
\Sigma_{1}
of edge deletions.
Recall that graph
G
is a
\varphi
-expander, and that
|\Sigma_{1}|\leq k^{\prime}=\left\lfloor\frac{n^{1-20\epsilon}\varphi^{2}}{2^{11}\cdot\Delta^{4}}\right\rfloor
. Recall that the algorithm maintains an incremental set
U\subseteq V(G)
of vertices, that we denote by
U_{1}
, such that, for every integer
t>0
, after
t
edges are deleted from
G
,
|U_{1}|\leq\frac{4\Delta t}{\varphi}
holds.
Throughout the first phase, we let the set
U
of vertices that our algorithm maintains be
U_{1}
. Recall that the algorithm from
Lemma

8.1
supports queries short-path queries: given a pair of vertices
x,y\in V(G)\setminus U_{1}
, return an
x
-
y
path
P
in the current graph
H_{1}=G
, of length at most
\frac{2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\cdot\Delta\cdot\log n}{\varphi}
, with query time
O(|E(P)|)
.

From the above discussion, at the end of the first phase,
|U_{1}|\leq\frac{4\Delta k^{\prime}}{\varphi}\leq\frac{n^{1-20\epsilon}\cdot\varphi}{2^{9}\cdot\Delta^{3}}
holds. We denote by
\tilde{E}_{1}
the set of all edges that are incident to the vertices of
U_{1}
in the current graph
G
. We then update graph
G^{\prime}
, by deleting the edges of
E_{1}\cup\tilde{E}_{1}
from it. Therefore, at the end of the first phase, the number of edges that are deleted from
G^{\prime}
is bounded by:

|E_{1}|+|\tilde{E}_{1}|\leq\frac{n^{1-20\epsilon}\cdot\varphi}{2^{8}\cdot\Delta^{2}}<k.

We denote by
\tilde{U}_{1}
the set of vertices that the algorithm from
Theorem

3.4
produces at the end of Phase 1, and the deletion of the edges of
E_{1}\cup\tilde{E}_{1}
from
G^{\prime}
. We also denote by
H_{2}=G^{\prime}\setminus\tilde{U}_{1}
. From
Theorem

3.4
, graph
H_{2}
is a
\varphi/(6\Delta)
-expander.
Notice also that, if
U_{1}
is the set of vertices that the algorithm from
Lemma

8.1
maintains at the end of the phase, then
U_{1}\subseteq\tilde{U}_{1}
must hold.

The remaining phases are executed similarly, with several minor differences. We let
\tilde{U}_{i}
be the set
\tilde{U}
that the algorithm from
Theorem

3.4
produces at the end of Phase
(i-1)
, and we denote
H_{i}=G^{\prime}\setminus\tilde{U}_{i}
. We will ensure that the total number of edges that are deleted from graph
G^{\prime}
over the course of the first
(i-1)
phases is bounded by:

(i-1)\cdot\frac{n^{1-20\epsilon}\cdot\varphi}{32\cdot\Delta}.

Since the total number of phases is bounded by
\frac{n^{20\epsilon}}{4}
, this ensures that the number of edges deleted so far from
G^{\prime}
is less than
\frac{n\cdot\varphi}{16\Delta}=k
. Therefore, from
Theorem

3.4
, graph
H_{i}
is a
\varphi/(6\Delta)
-expander, and furthermore,
|\tilde{U}_{i}|\leq n/2
, so
|V(H_{i})|\geq n/2
. Denote
\varphi^{*}=\frac{\varphi}{6\Delta}
, and note that:

k^{\prime}=\left\lfloor\frac{n^{1-20\epsilon}\varphi^{2}}{2^{11}\cdot\Delta^{4}}\right\rfloor\leq\frac{|V(H_{i})|^{1-20\epsilon}(\varphi^{*})^{2}}{\Delta^{2}}

Therefore, we can apply the algorithm from
Lemma

8.1
to graph
H_{i}
with the sequence
\Sigma_{i}
of edge deletions. We denote by
U_{i}
the incremental set of vertices of
H_{i}
that the algorithm maintains. The set
U
of vertices of
G
that our algorithm maintains over the course of the
i
th phase is defined to be
\tilde{U}_{i}\cup U_{i}
.

Recall that the total number of edges that are deleted over the course of the first
(i-1)
phases of the algorithm from graph
G
is
(i-1)\cdot k^{\prime}\geq(i-1)\cdot\left\lfloor\frac{n^{1-20\epsilon}\varphi^{2}}{2^{11}\cdot\Delta^{4}}\right\rfloor
.

Since the total number of edges deleted from graph
G^{\prime}
over the course of the first
(i-1)
phases is bounded by
(i-1)\cdot\frac{n^{1-20\epsilon}\cdot\varphi}{32\cdot\Delta}
, from
Theorem

3.4
, we get that:

|\tilde{U}_{i}|\leq\frac{n^{1-20\epsilon}\cdot(i-1)}{4}.

Consider some integer
0\leq t\leq k^{\prime}
, and the time during the execution of phase
i
, immediately after the
t
-th edge of
\Sigma_{i}
is deleted from graph
G
in phase
i
. Then at this time, the total number of edges deleted from graph
G
since the beginning of the algorithm is at least:

m^{i}_{t}=(i-1)\cdot\left\lfloor\frac{n^{1-20\epsilon}\varphi^{2}}{2^{11}\cdot\Delta^{4}}\right\rfloor+t.

At the same time:

|U|=|\tilde{U}_{i}|+|U_{i}|\leq(i-1)\cdot\frac{n^{1-20\epsilon}}{4}+\frac{4\Delta t}{\varphi^{*}}=(i-1)\cdot\frac{n^{1-20\epsilon}}{4}+\frac{24\Delta^{2}t}{\varphi}\leq\frac{2^{11}\Delta^{4}}{\varphi^{2}}\cdot m^{i}_{t}.

Notice that, over the course of the
i
th phase,
V(H_{i})\setminus U_{i}=V(G)\setminus(U_{i}\cup\tilde{U}_{i})=V(G)\setminus U
holds. Therefore, when short-path query arrives for a pair
x,y\in V(G)\setminus U
of vertices, it must be the case that
x,y\in V(H_{i})\setminus U_{i}
. We can then perform
short-path query in the data structure maintained by the algorithm from
Lemma

8.1
, to obtain a path
P
in the current graph
H_{i}\subseteq G
, of length at most
\frac{2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\cdot\Delta\cdot\log n}{\varphi^{*}}\leq\frac{2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\cdot\Delta^{2}\cdot\log n}{\varphi}
, in time
O(|E(P)|)
. We return this path as the response to the query.

From the above discussion, at every time
t
during the execution of phase
i
, if
m_{t}^{i}
is the number of edges deleted so far by the algorithm, and
U^{(t)}
is the current set
U
, then:

|U^{(t)}|\leq\frac{2^{11}\Delta^{2}}{\varphi^{2}}\cdot m^{i}_{t}.

Once all edges of
\Sigma_{i}
edges are deleted from graph
G
, we let
\tilde{E}_{i}
be the set of all edges that are incident to the vertices in the current set
U_{i}
. Observe that:

|\tilde{E}_{i}|\leq\Delta\cdot|U_{i}|\leq\frac{4\Delta^{2}}{\varphi^{*}}\cdot k^{\prime}\leq\frac{24\Delta^{3}}{\varphi}\cdot\frac{n^{1-20\epsilon}\varphi^{2}}{2^{11}\cdot\Delta^{4}}\leq\frac{n^{1-20\epsilon}\cdot\varphi}{64\cdot\Delta}.

since
\varphi^{*}=\frac{\varphi}{6\Delta}
and
k^{\prime}=\left\lfloor\frac{n^{1-20\epsilon}\varphi^{2}}{2^{11}\cdot\Delta^{4}}\right\rfloor
.

We delete the edges of
E_{i}\cup\tilde{E}_{i}
from graph
G^{\prime}
, and update the data structure maintained by the algorithm from
Theorem

3.4
. Since
|E_{i}|\leq k^{\prime}=\left\lfloor\frac{n^{1-20\epsilon}\varphi^{2}}{2^{11}\cdot\Delta^{4}}\right\rfloor
, we get that
|E_{i}\cup\tilde{E}_{i}|\leq\frac{n^{1-20\epsilon}\cdot\varphi}{32\cdot\Delta}
. Recall that we have assumed that, over the course of the first
(i-1)
phases, the total number of edges deleted from graph
G^{\prime}
is bounded by

(i-1)\cdot\frac{n^{1-20\epsilon}\cdot\varphi}{32\cdot\Delta}
. We then get that, over the course of the first
i
phases, the total number of edges deleted from graph
G^{\prime}
is bounded by
i\cdot\frac{n^{1-20\epsilon}\cdot\varphi}{32\cdot\Delta}
. We let
\tilde{U}_{i+1}
the set
\tilde{U}
that the algorithm from
Theorem

3.4
maintains after the edges of
E_{i}\cup\tilde{E}_{i}
are deleted from
G^{\prime}
. Since the vertices of
U_{i}
are isolated in graph
G^{\prime}\setminus\tilde{E}_{i}
, while graph
G^{\prime}\setminus\tilde{U}_{i+1}
must be a
\varphi/(6\Delta)
-expander, we get that
U_{i}\subseteq\tilde{U}_{i+1}
. We then let the set
U
that the algorithm maintains be
\tilde{U}_{i+1}
, and we continue to the next phase, with the graph
H_{i+1}=G^{\prime}\setminus\tilde{U}_{i+1}
.

It is easy to verify that the set
U
of vertices that the algorithm maintains is incremental. Indeed, consider some phase
i
of the algorithm. Throughout the phase, we let
U=\tilde{U}_{i}\cup U_{i}
, where
\tilde{U}_{i}
is fixed over the course of the phase, and
U_{i}
is incremental. If we denote by
U_{i}^{\prime}
the set
U_{i}
at the end of Phase
i
, then we are guaranteed that
U^{\prime}_{i}\subseteq\tilde{U}_{i+1}
, and, from
Theorem

3.4
,
\tilde{U}_{i}\subseteq\tilde{U}_{i+1}
. At the beginning of Phase
(i+1)
, we set
U=\tilde{U}_{i+1}
, and so
\tilde{U}_{i}\cup U_{i}^{\prime}\subseteq U
at this point. Therefore, set
U
is incremental
throughout the algorithm.

It now only remains to bound the total update time of the algorithm. The algorithm consists of at most
O(n^{20\epsilon})
phases. In every phase, we run the algorithm from
Lemma

8.1
, whose total update time is
O\left(\frac{m^{1+O(\epsilon)}\cdot\Delta^{3}}{(\varphi^{*})^{2}}\right)\leq O\left(\frac{m^{1+O(\epsilon)}\cdot\Delta^{5}}{\varphi^{2}}\right)
.

Additionally, the total update time of the algorithm from

Theorem

3.4
, over the course of at most
m
deletions of edges from
G^{\prime}
, is bounded by
\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{m\Delta^{2}}{\varphi^{2}}\right)
.

Altogether, the total update time of the algorithm is bounded by:

O(n^{20\epsilon})\cdot O\left(\frac{m^{1+O(\epsilon)}\cdot\Delta^{5}}{\varphi^{2}}\right)+\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{m\Delta^{2}}{\varphi^{2}}\right)\leq O\left(\frac{m^{1+O(\epsilon)}\cdot\Delta^{5}}{\varphi^{2}}\right).

In order to complete the proof of
Theorem

2.4
, it now remains to prove
Lemma

8.1
.

8.1
Proof of
Lemma

8.1

We start by describing the data structures that the algorithm maintains, together with their initialization. We then describe an algorithm for maintaining the data structures under the deletion of edges from
G
. Finally, we describe an algorithm for responding to short-path query.

Before we do so, we establish some bounds on the parameters that will be useful for us later. All of these bounds follow from the fact that
\frac{2}{(\log n)^{1/12}}<\epsilon<\frac{1}{400}
holds, from the statement of
Lemma

8.1
.

First, since
\epsilon>\frac{2}{(\log n)^{1/12}}
, we get that
\log n\geq(2/\epsilon)^{12}
, and:

n\geq 2^{(2/\epsilon)^{12}}\geq 2^{800^{12}}.

(11)

Additionally:

n^{\epsilon^{8}}>n^{(2/(\log n)^{1/12})^{8}}=n^{256/(\log n)^{2/3}}>2^{(\log n)^{1/3}}>\log n.

(12)

8.1.1
Data Structures and Initialization

We start by
applying the algorithm from
Corollary

5.3
to graph
G
, with the set
T=V(G)
of terminals, parameter
\epsilon
given by the statement of
Lemma

8.1
, and parameters
d=\frac{64\Delta\log m}{\varphi^{*}}
and
\eta=\frac{512\Delta^{2}\log m}{(\varphi^{*})^{2}}
.

We claim that the algorithm may not return a pair

T_{1},T_{2}\subseteq T
of disjoint subsets of terminals, and a set
E^{\prime}
of edges of
G
, with
|T_{1}|=|T_{2}|
,
|T_{1}|\geq\frac{n^{1-4\epsilon^{3}}}{4}
and
|E^{\prime}|\leq\frac{d\cdot|T_{1}|}{\eta}
, such that for every pair
t\in T_{1},t^{\prime}\in T_{2}
of terminals,
\mbox{\sf dist}_{G\setminus E^{\prime}}(t,t^{\prime})>d
. Indeed, assume for contradiction that the algorithm returns a pair
T_{1},T_{2}
of disjoint subsets of vertices of
G
with the above properties. Denote
\varphi=\frac{\varphi^{*}}{\Delta}
, and note that
|E^{\prime}|\leq\frac{d\cdot|T_{1}|}{\eta}=\frac{\varphi^{*}\cdot|T_{1}|}{8\Delta}\leq\frac{\varphi\cdot|T_{1}|}{4}
.
Note also that
d=\frac{64\Delta\log m}{\varphi^{*}}\geq\frac{32\log m}{\varphi}
.
Clearly, we can view
(T_{1},T_{2},E^{\prime})
as a
(\delta,d)
-distancing in graph
G
, for some parameter
0<\delta<1
. From
Lemma

4.1
, there is a cut
(X,Y)
in graph
G
, with
T_{1}\subseteq X
and
T_{2}\subseteq Y
, such that
|E_{G}(X,Y)|\leq\varphi\cdot\min\left\{|E(X)|,|E(Y)|\right\}
. Since the maximum vertex degree in
G
is bounded by
\Delta
,
|E(X)|<\Delta\cdot|X|
, and similarly
|E(Y)|<\Delta\cdot|Y|
. Therefore, we are guaranteed that
|E(X,Y)|<\varphi\cdot\Delta\cdot\min\left\{|X|,|Y|\right\}=\varphi^{*}\cdot\min\left\{|X|,|Y|\right\}
, contradicting the fact that
G
is a
\varphi^{*}
-expander.

We conclude that the algorithm from
Corollary

5.3
must return a graph
H
with
V(H)\subseteq V(G)
,
|V(H)|=N^{1/\epsilon}\geq n-n^{1-\epsilon/2}\geq n/2
, where
N=\left\lfloor n^{\epsilon}\right\rfloor
, so that the maximum vertex degree in
H
is at most
n^{32\epsilon^{3}}
. The algorithm also must return an embedding
{\mathcal{P}}
of
H
into
G
via paths of length at most
d
that cause congestion at most
\eta\cdot n^{32\epsilon^{3}}
, and a level-
(1/\epsilon)
hierarchical support structure for
H
, such that
H
is
(\eta^{\prime},\tilde{d})
-well-connected with respect to the set
S(H)
of vertices defined by the support structure, where
\eta^{\prime}=N^{6+256\epsilon}
, and
\tilde{d}=2^{c/\epsilon^{5}}
, with
c
being the constant used in the definition of the Hierarchical Support Structure.
Recall that the running time of the algorithm is:

O\left(n^{1+O(\epsilon)}+|E(G)|\cdot n^{O(\epsilon^{3})}\cdot(\eta+d\log n)\right)\leq O\left(n^{1+O(\epsilon)}+n^{1+O(\epsilon^{3})}\cdot\frac{\Delta^{3}\log^{2}n}{(\varphi^{*})^{2}}\right)\leq O\left(\frac{n^{1+O(\epsilon)}\cdot\Delta^{3}}{(\varphi^{*})^{2}}\right).

(We have used the fact that
d=\frac{64\Delta\log m}{\varphi^{*}}
,
\eta=\frac{512\Delta^{2}\log m}{(\varphi^{*})^{2}}
, and Inequality
12
). Let
q=1/\epsilon
.

For every edge
e^{\prime}\in E(H)
, let
P(e^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{P}}
be the path embedding edge
e^{\prime}
into
G
. For every edge
e\in E(G)
, we will maintain a list
L(e)
of all edges
e^{\prime}\in E(H)
with
e\in E(P(e^{\prime}))
. The list also contains, for each edge
e^{\prime}\in L(e)
, a pointer to edge
e^{\prime}
in graph
H
, and each edge
e^{\prime}\in E(H)
maintains a pointer to every edge in
E(P(e^{\prime}))
. Whenever an edge
e\in E(G)
is deleted from graph
G
, we will delete every edge
e^{\prime}\in L(e)
from graph
H
. Since the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}
cause congestion at most
\eta\cdot n^{32\epsilon^{3}}=\frac{512\Delta^{2}\cdot n^{32\epsilon^{3}}\log m}{(\varphi^{*})^{2}}\leq\frac{\Delta^{2}\cdot n^{33\epsilon^{3}}}{(\varphi^{*})^{2}}
in
H
, every deletion of an edge in
G
may trigger the deletion of at most
\tilde{\eta}=\frac{\Delta^{2}\cdot n^{33\epsilon^{3}}}{(\varphi^{*})^{2}}
edges from
H
. Let
q=1/\epsilon
. Then the total number of edge deletions from graph
H
over the course of the algorithm is bounded by:

\frac{n^{1-20\epsilon}(\varphi^{*})^{2}}{\Delta^{2}}\cdot\tilde{\eta}=n^{1-20\epsilon+33\epsilon^{2}}\leq\frac{n^{1-16\epsilon}}{2}\leq N^{q-16q\epsilon}<N^{q-8-300q\epsilon^{2}},

as
q=1/\epsilon
and
\frac{n}{2}\leq N^{q}
.

Note that
\eta^{\prime}=N^{6+256\epsilon}=N^{6+256q\epsilon^{2}}=\eta_{q}
and
\tilde{d}=2^{c/\epsilon^{5}}=\tilde{d}_{q}
, where
\eta_{q}
and
\tilde{d}_{q}
are the parameters from the definition of Hierarchical Support Structure. We will use the algorithm from
Theorem

7.1
in graph
H
, with parameter
j=q
, and parameters
\epsilon,N
remaining unchanged.
In order to be able to use the theorem, we need to verify that
\frac{N^{\epsilon^{4}}}{\log N}\geq 2^{128/\epsilon^{6}}
holds.
Since
N=\left\lfloor n^{\epsilon}\right\rfloor
, we get that:

\frac{N^{\epsilon^{4}}}{\log N}\geq\frac{n^{\epsilon^{6}}}{\epsilon\cdot\log n}\geq n^{\epsilon^{6}/2}\geq 2^{128/\epsilon^{6}}.

(we have used the fact that, from Inequality
12
,
\log n<n^{\epsilon^{8}}
, from Inequality
11
,
n\geq 2^{(2/\epsilon)^{12}}
, and
\epsilon<1/400
).

As observed already, the number of edge deletions that graph
H
undergoes over the course of the algorithm is bounded less than
N^{q-8-300q\epsilon^{2}}=\Lambda_{q}
. We will maintain a data structure from
Theorem

7.1
in graph
H
, with parameters
\epsilon,N
and
q
as defined above. We denote this data structure by
{\mathcal{D}}(H)
. At the beginning of the algorithm, we initialize this data structure.
Recall that data structure
{\mathcal{D}}(H)
maintains
a decremental set
S^{\prime}(H)\subseteq S(H)
of vertices of
H
, called supported vertices, such that, at the beginning of the algorithm,
S^{\prime}(H)=S(H)
. The algorithm ensures that
|S^{\prime}(H)|\geq\frac{N^{q}}{16^{q}}
holds over the course of the algorithm, and it supports short-path queries between supported vertices: given a pair
x,y\in S^{\prime}(H)
of vertices, return a path
P
connecting
x
to
y
in the current graph
H
, whose length is at most
d^{*}_{q}=2^{O(q/\epsilon^{5})}=2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}
, in time
O(|E(P)|)
.
If
m^{\prime}\leq n^{1+32\epsilon^{3}}
is the number of edges in
H
at the beginning of the algorithm, then the total update time needed to maintain data structure
{\mathcal{D}}(H)
is bounded by:

O\left(qN^{q+3}\cdot 2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}+m^{\prime}\cdot N^{2}\cdot 2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\right)\leq O\left(n^{1+O(\epsilon)}\cdot 2^{O(1/\epsilon^{5})}\right)\leq O\left(n^{1+O(\epsilon)}\right)

(We have used the fact that, from Inequality
11
,
n^{\epsilon}\geq 2^{(2/\epsilon)^{12}\cdot\epsilon}\geq 2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}
).

Lastly, we maintain an
ES-Tree
data structure
\tau
in graph
G
, rooted at the set
S^{\prime}(H)
of vertices, with depth parameter
d=\frac{64\Delta\log m}{\varphi^{*}}
. Specifically, we maintain a graph
G^{\prime}
, that is obtained from graph
G
by adding a source vertex
s
, that connects to every vertex that lies in the current set
S^{\prime}(H)
of supported vertices with an edge. We then let
\tau
be an
ES-Tree
data structure in graph
G^{\prime}
, rooted at
s
, with depth
d+1
. Whenever a vertex
x
is deleted from set
S^{\prime}(H)
, we will delete edge
(s,x)
from
G^{\prime}
, and update the data structure
\tau
accordingly. Also, whenever an edge
e
is deleted from graph
G
, we will also delete
e
from graph
G^{\prime}
, and update the data structure
\tau
accordingly. Throughout the algorithm, we let
U
be the set of all vertices
v\in V(G)
, such that
v\not\in V(\tau)
. In other words,
\mbox{\sf dist}_{G}(S^{\prime}(H),v)>d
. Clearly, the set
U
of vertices is incremental: vertices can joint it but they cannot leave it. In the next claim we bound the cardinality of
U
.

Claim 8.2
.

Let
t
be any time during the algorithm’s execution, let
E^{\prime}_{t}
be the set of edges that were deleted so far from
G
, and let
U_{t}
be the current set
U
of vertices. Then
|U_{t}|\leq\frac{4\Delta|E^{\prime}_{t}|}{\varphi^{*}}
.

Proof.

Assume otherwise, and let
t
be some time during the algorithm’s execution, when
|U_{t}|>\frac{4\Delta|E^{\prime}_{t}|}{\varphi^{*}}
holds. Denote
\varphi=\frac{\varphi^{*}}{\Delta}
, so that
|E^{\prime}_{t}|<\varphi\cdot|U_{t}|/4
.

Let
S^{\prime}_{t}
be the set
S^{\prime}(H)
of vertices at time
t
. Recall that we are guaranteed that
|S^{\prime}(H)|\geq\frac{N^{q}}{16^{q}}\geq\frac{n}{2^{4/\epsilon+1}}
holds throughout the algorithm. Since the total number of the edges deleted from
G
over the course of the algorithm is at most
\frac{n^{1-20\epsilon}(\varphi^{*})^{2}}{\Delta^{2}}=n^{1-20\epsilon}\varphi^{2}\leq n^{1-20\epsilon}\cdot\varphi
, we get that
|E^{\prime}_{t}|\leq n^{1-20\epsilon}\cdot\varphi
. On the other hand, from Inequality
11
,
n^{20\epsilon}\geq 2^{4/\epsilon+4}
, and so:

|S^{\prime}_{t}|\geq\frac{n}{2^{4/\epsilon+1}}\geq 4n^{1-20\epsilon}\geq\frac{4|E^{\prime}_{t}|}{\varphi}.

We conclude that
|E^{\prime}_{t}|\leq\frac{\varphi}{4}\cdot\min\left\{|S^{\prime}_{t}|,|U_{t}|\right\}
.
Denote
M=\min\left\{|S^{\prime}_{t}|,|U_{t}|\right\}
. Let
X\subseteq S^{\prime}_{t}
,
Y\subseteq U_{t}
be arbitrary subsets of vertices of cardinality
M
each. Let
G^{0}
be the graph
G
at the beginning of the algorithm. Clearly,
\mbox{\sf dist}_{G^{0}\setminus E^{\prime}_{t}}(X,Y)>d
. Recall that
d=\frac{64\Delta\log m}{\varphi^{*}}=\frac{64\log m}{\varphi}
. From
Lemma

4.1
, there is a cut
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
in graph
G^{0}
, with
with
X\subseteq X^{\prime}
and
Y\subseteq Y^{\prime}
, such that
|E_{G^{0}}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})|\leq\varphi\cdot\min\left\{|E_{G^{0}}(X^{\prime})|,|E_{G^{0}}(Y^{\prime})|\right\}
. Since
|E_{G^{0}}(X^{\prime})|<\Delta\cdot|X^{\prime}|
and
|E_{G^{0}}(Y^{\prime})|<\Delta\cdot|Y^{\prime}|
, we get that:

|E_{G^{0}}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})|<\varphi\cdot\Delta\cdot\min\left\{|X^{\prime}|,|Y^{\prime}|\right\}=\varphi^{*}\cdot\min\left\{|X^{\prime}|,|Y^{\prime}|\right\},

contradicting the fact that graph
G^{0}
is a
\varphi^{*}
-expander.

At the beginning of the algorithm, we initialize the
ES-Tree
data structure
\tau
, and set
U=\emptyset
. The total update time needed in order to maintain
\tau
is bounded by
O(md\log m)\leq O\left(\frac{m\Delta\log^{2}m}{\varphi^{*}}\right)
.

We now bound the running time that is needed to initialize all data structures, and to maintain data structures
{\mathcal{D}}(H)
,
\tau
, and
\left\{L(e)\right\}_{e\in E(G)}
over the course of the algorithm.
The running time of the algorithm from
Corollary

5.3
, from the above discussion, is bounded by
O\left(\frac{n^{1+O(\epsilon)}\cdot\Delta^{3}}{(\varphi^{*})^{2}}\right)
. The time that is needed in order to initialize and maintain the lists
L(e)
for edges
e\in E(G)
is subsumed by this running time. Additionally, from the above discussion, the total update time of data structure
{\mathcal{D}}(H)
is bounded by
O\left(n^{1+O(\epsilon)}\right)
, and the total update time of data structure
\tau
is bounded by
O\left(\frac{m\Delta\log^{2}m}{\varphi^{*}}\right)
. The set
U
of vertices can be maintained within this asymptotic running time. Overall, the total time needed to initialize all data structures, and to maintain data structures
{\mathcal{D}}(H)
,
\tau
, and
\left\{L(e)\right\}_{e\in E(G)}
over the course of the algorithm is bounded by:

O\left(\frac{m\Delta\log^{2}m}{\varphi^{*}}\right)+O\left(\frac{n^{1+O(\epsilon)}\cdot\Delta^{3}}{(\varphi^{*})^{2}}\right)\leq O\left(\frac{n^{1+O(\epsilon)}\cdot\Delta^{3}}{(\varphi^{*})^{2}}\right).

8.1.2
Maintaining the Data Structures

Maintaining the data structures under the deletion of edges from
G
is now straightforward. In
Figure

2
we describe algorithm
\operatorname{DeleteExpanderEdge}(e)
that is invoked whenever an edge
e
is deleted from graph
G
. We start by considering every edge
e^{\prime}\in E(H)
whose embedding path
P(e^{\prime})
contains edge
e
– in other words, all edges of
L(e)
. We delete each such edge
e^{\prime}
from graph
H
, and update data structure
{\mathcal{D}}(H)
with this deletion. As a result, it is possible that some vertices are removed from set
S^{\prime}(H)
. For each such vertex
x
, we delete edge
(s,x)
from graph
G^{\prime}
, and update the data structure
\tau
accordingly. Finally, we delete edge
e
from data structure
\tau
.
Whenever a vertex leaves the tree
\tau
, we add it to
U
.

Algorithm
\operatorname{DeleteExpanderEdge}(e)

1.

For every edge
e^{\prime}\in L(e)
do:

(a)

Delete
e^{\prime}
from
H
and update data structure
{\mathcal{D}}(H)
. Let
X
be the set of vertices that were deleted from
S^{\prime}(H)
as the result of this update.

(b)

For every edge
e^{\prime\prime}\in E(G)
with
e^{\prime}\in L(e^{\prime\prime})
, delete
e^{\prime}
from
L(e^{\prime\prime})
.

(c)

for every vertex
x\in X
, delete edge
(s,x)
from graph
G^{\prime}
and update the
ES-Tree

\tau
with this deletion. Add every vertex that is removed from
\tau
to
U
.

2.

Delete edge
e
from graph
G^{\prime}
and update data structure
\tau
accordingly. Add every vertex that is removed from
\tau
to
U
.

Figure 2:
Algorithm
\operatorname{DeleteExpanderEdge}(e)

It is easy to verify that the total update time of the algorithm is dominated by the time needed to initialize the data structures, and to maintain
data structures
{\mathcal{D}}(H)
,
\tau
, and
\left\{L(e)\right\}_{e\in E(G)}
. From the above discussion, the total update time of the algorithm is bounded by
O\left(\frac{n^{1+O(\epsilon)}\cdot\Delta^{3}}{(\varphi^{*})^{2}}\right)
.

8.1.3
Responding to Short-Path Queries

We assume that we are given a pair of vertices
x,y\in V(G)\setminus U
, and describe an algorithm for responding to short-path query between
x
and
y
. Recall that our goal is to return an
x
-
y
path
P
in the current graph
G
, of length at most
\frac{2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\cdot\Delta\cdot\log n}{\varphi^{*}}
, with query time
O(|E(P)|)
.

Using the
ES-Tree

\tau
, we compute a path
Q
connecting
x
to some vertex
x^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}(H)
, and a path
Q^{\prime}
connecting vertex
y
to some vertex
y^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}(H)
, so that the length of each path is bounded by
d
. Next, we query data structure
{\mathcal{D}}(H)
with the pair
x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}(H)
of vertices. The data structure must return a path
\tilde{Q}
connecting
x^{\prime}
to
y^{\prime}
in
H
, whose length is at most
2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}
, in time
O(|E(\tilde{Q})|)
. Using the embedding
{\mathcal{P}}
of
H
into
G
, in which the length of every path is bounded by
d
, we can compute a path
Q^{\prime\prime}
in graph
G
, connecting
x^{\prime}
to
y^{\prime}
, whose length is bounded by
|E(\tilde{Q})|\cdot d\leq 2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\cdot d
. Lastly, by concatenating the paths
Q,Q^{\prime\prime}
and
Q^{\prime}
, we obtain a path
P
connecting
x
to
y
in graph
G
, whose length is at most:

2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\cdot d\leq\frac{2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\cdot\Delta\cdot\log m}{\varphi^{*}}\leq\frac{2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\cdot\Delta\cdot\log n}{\varphi^{*}}.

It is easy to see that the running time of the algorithm is
O(|E(P)|)
.

9
Advanced Path Peeling – Proof of
Theorem

2.5

In this section we prove
Theorem

2.5
.
The main tool in the proof is the following theorem.

Theorem 9.1
.

There is a large enough constant
c^{*}
, and a deterministic algorithm, whose input consists of a connected
n
-vertex
m
-edge graph
G
, a collection
{M}=\left\{(s_{1},t_{1}),\ldots,(s_{k},t_{k})\right\}
of pairs of vertices of
G
, such that
{M}
is a matching, and parameters
d,\eta>0
,
0<\alpha\leq 1/2
and
\frac{2}{(\log n)^{1/24}}<\epsilon<\frac{1}{400}
, such that
1/\epsilon
is an integer
and
256d<\eta\leq\frac{d^{2}}{2^{c^{*}/\epsilon^{6}}\cdot\log m}
holds. The algorithm computes one of the following:

•

either a cut
(A,B)
with
|E_{G}(A,B)|\leq\frac{1024d}{\eta}\cdot\min\left\{|E_{G}(A)|,|E_{G}(B)|\right\}
, and each of
A
,
B
contains at least
\frac{\alpha k}{8}
vertices of set
T=\left\{s_{1},t_{1},\ldots,s_{k},t_{k}\right\}
; or

•

a routing
{\mathcal{P}}
in
G
of a subset
{M}^{\prime}\subseteq{M}
containing at least
(1-\alpha)k
pairs of vertices, such that every path in
{\mathcal{P}}
has length at most
d
, and the total congestion caused by the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}
is at most
\frac{4\eta}{\alpha}
.

The running time of the algorithm is bounded by
O\left(m^{1+O(\epsilon)}(d^{2}+\eta d)\right)
.

The proof of
Theorem

2.5
easily follows from
Theorem

9.1
.
Let
z=\left\lceil\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right\rceil
, and let
\epsilon^{\prime}=\frac{1}{z}
. Clearly,
\frac{1}{\epsilon}\leq z\leq\frac{2}{\epsilon}
, and so
\frac{\epsilon}{2}\leq\epsilon^{\prime}\leq\epsilon
.
Since we have assumed that
\frac{4}{(\log n)^{1/24}}<\epsilon<\frac{1}{400}
, we get that
\frac{2}{(\log n)^{1/24}}<\epsilon^{\prime}<\frac{1}{400}
.
For convenience, in the remainder of the proof we will denote
\epsilon^{\prime}
by
\epsilon
.

Let
c^{*}
be the constant from
Theorem

9.1
.
We set
d=\frac{2^{c^{*}/\epsilon^{6}+10}\cdot\log m}{\varphi}
and
\eta=\frac{1024d}{\varphi}=\frac{2^{c^{*}/\epsilon^{6}+20}\cdot\log m}{\varphi^{2}}
. It is immediate to verify that
\eta>256d
. Observe also that:

\frac{d^{2}}{2^{c^{*}/\epsilon^{6}}\cdot\log m}=\frac{1024d}{\varphi}\geq\eta.

We start by considering the case where

\frac{1}{\alpha}<\log n
. In this case, we apply the algorithm from
Theorem

9.1
to graph
G
, the set
{M}
of pairs of its vertices, and parameters
d,\eta,\alpha
and
\epsilon
. Assume first that the outcome of the algorithm is a cut
(A,B)
with
|E_{G}(A,B)|\leq\frac{1024d}{\eta}\cdot\min\left\{|E_{G}(A)|,|E_{G}(B)|\right\}=\varphi\cdot\min\left\{|E_{G}(A)|,|E_{G}(B)|\right\}
, and
|T\cap A|,|T\cap B|\geq\frac{\alpha k}{8}
. In this case, we return the cut
(A,B)
as the outcome of the algorithm. Otherwise, the outcome of the algorithm from
Theorem

9.1
is a routing
{\mathcal{P}}
in
G
of a subset
{M}^{\prime}\subseteq{M}
containing at least
k\cdot(1-\alpha)
pairs of vertices, such that every path in
{\mathcal{P}}
has length at most
d\leq\frac{2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\cdot\log n}{\varphi}
, and the total congestion caused by the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}
is at most
\frac{4\eta}{\alpha}\leq\frac{2^{O(1/\epsilon^{5})}\cdot\log n}{\alpha\cdot\varphi^{2}}
. We then return this set of paths as the outcome of the algorithm.

The running time of the algorithm from
Theorem

9.1
is bounded by:

O\left(m^{1+O(\epsilon)}(d^{2}+\eta d)\right)\leq O\left(m^{1+O(\epsilon)}\left(\frac{2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\cdot\log^{2}n}{\varphi^{2}}+\frac{2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\cdot\log^{2}n}{\varphi^{3}}\right)\right)\leq O\left(\frac{m^{1+O(\epsilon)}}{\varphi^{3}}\right).
