be an arbitrary matching that connects every vertex of
A^{\prime}_{q}
to a distinct vertex of
B^{\prime}_{q}
(such a matching must exist since
|A_{q}|\leq|B_{q}|
). As before, we define a set
F_{q}
of fake edges, containing the edges of
{M}^{\prime\prime}_{q}
, and an embedding
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime\prime}_{q}=\left\{P(e)\mid e\in F_{q}\right\}
of the edges in
{M}^{\prime\prime}_{q}
, where each fake edge is embedded into itself. Lastly, we set
{M}_{q}={M}^{\prime}_{q}\cup{M}^{\prime\prime}_{q}
, and we add the edges of
{M}_{q}
to graph
H
.

From now on we assume that the algorithm never terminated with a cut
(X,Y)
with
|X|,|Y|\geq z
and
|E_{G}(X,Y)|\leq\varphi\cdot\min\left\{|X|,|Y|\right\}
. Note that, from
3.2
, the final graph
H
is a
\psi
-expander, for
\psi\geq\frac{\varphi^{*}}{2}\geq\Omega\left(\frac{1}{2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\cdot\log^{5}n}\right)
. Moreover, we are guaranteed that there is an embedding of
H
into
G+F
with congestion
O\left(\frac{\log^{2}n}{\varphi}\right)
, where
F=\bigcup_{i=1}^{r}F_{i}
is a set of
O(z\log n)
fake edges. Notice that, in the embedding that we constructed, every edge of
H
is either embedded into a path consisting of a single fake edge, or it is embedded into a path in the graph
G
; every fake edge in
F
serves as an embedding of exactly one edge of
H
.

We now claim that there is a large enough universal constant
c_{0}
, such that, if we let
\alpha=2^{c_{0}/\epsilon^{6}}\cdot\log^{7}n
and
\alpha^{\prime}=2^{c_{0}/\epsilon^{6}}\cdot\log^{6}n
, then for every cut
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
in
G
with
|E_{G}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})\leq\frac{\varphi}{\alpha}\cdot\min\left\{|X^{\prime}|,|Y^{\prime}|\right\}
,
\min\left\{|X^{\prime}|,|Y^{\prime}|\right\}<\alpha^{\prime}\cdot z
holds.

Indeed, consider any cut
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
in
G
with
|X^{\prime}|,|Y^{\prime}|\geq\alpha^{\prime}\cdot z
. We assume w.l.o.g. that
|X^{\prime}|\leq|Y^{\prime}|
. It is enough to show that
|E_{G}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})|>\frac{\varphi\cdot|X^{\prime}|}{\alpha}
.

Notice that
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
also defines a cut in graph
H
, and, since
H
is a
\psi
-expander,
|E_{H}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})|\geq\psi\cdot|X^{\prime}|\geq\psi\cdot\alpha^{\prime}\cdot z\geq\psi\cdot z\cdot 2^{c_{0}/\epsilon^{6}}\cdot\log^{6}n
.
Since
\psi\geq\frac{1}{2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\cdot\log^{5}n}
, assuming that
c_{0}
is a large enough constant, we get that
|E_{H}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})|\geq c_{0}z\log n
.

We partition the set
E_{H}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
of edges into two subsets. The first subset,
E_{1}
, is the set of edges corresponding to the fake edges (so each edge
e\in E_{1}
is embedded into a path
P(e)=\left\{e\right\}
in
G+F
), and
E_{2}
contains all remaining edges (each of which is embedded into a path of
G
). Recall that the total number of the fake edges,
|F|\leq O(z\log n)
, while
|E_{H}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})|\geq c_{0}z\log n
.
Therefore, by letting
c_{0}
be a large enough constant, we can ensure that
|E_{1}|\leq|E_{H}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})|/2
.

The embedding of
H
into
G+F
defines, for every edge
e\in E_{2}
a corresponding path
P(e)
in
G
, that must contribute at least one edge to the cut
E_{G}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
. Since the embedding causes congestion
O\left(\frac{\log^{2}n}{\varphi}\right)
, we get that:

\begin{split}|E_{G}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})|&\geq\Omega\left(\frac{|E_{H}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})|\cdot\varphi}{\log^{2}n}\right)\\
&\geq\Omega\left(\frac{\varphi\cdot\psi\cdot|X^{\prime}|}{\log^{2}n}\right)\\
&\geq\Omega\left(\frac{\varphi\cdot|X^{\prime}|}{2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\cdot\log^{7}n}\right)\\
&>\frac{\varphi\cdot|X^{\prime}|}{2^{c_{0}/\epsilon^{6}}\cdot\log^{7}n}\\
&=\frac{\varphi|X^{\prime}|}{\alpha},\end{split}

(we have used the fact that
c_{0}
is a large enough constant). We note that we have ignored the extra vertex
v_{0}
that we have added to
G
if
|V(G)|
is odd, but the removal of this vertex can only change the cut sparsity and the cardinalities of
X^{\prime}
and
Y^{\prime}
by a small constant factor that can be absorbed in
c_{0}
.

Lastly, we bound the running time of the algorithm. The algorithm consists of
O(\log n)
iterations. Every iteration employs the algorithm from

Theorem

2.6
, whose running time is
O\left(|E(H)|^{1+O(\epsilon)}\cdot\Delta_{H}^{7}\right)\leq O\left(n^{1+O(\epsilon)}\right)
, since
\Delta_{H}\leq O(\log n)
, and
\log^{8}n<n^{4\epsilon}
(the latter follows from the assumption that
\epsilon>\frac{2}{(\log n)^{1/25}}
, and since, from the inequality
\frac{2}{(\log n)^{1/25}}<\epsilon<\frac{1}{400}
,
n
must be large enough).
Additionally, in every iteration we use Algorithm
MatchOrCut
from
Theorem

11.2
, whose running time is
O\left(m^{1+o(1)}\right)
. Therefore, the total running time is
O\left(m^{1+O(\epsilon)+o(1)}\right)
.

We also immediately obtain the following analogue of Lemma 7.4 from
[
CGL+20
]
.

Theorem 11.4
.

There is a constant
c_{0}
and a deterministic algorithm, that, given an
n
-vertex and
m
-edge graph
G=(V,E)
and parameters
0<\varphi\leq 1
and
\frac{4}{(\log n)^{1/25}}<\epsilon<\frac{1}{400}
:

•

either returns a cut
(X,Y)
in
G
with
|E_{G}(X,Y)|\leq\varphi\cdot\min\left\{|X|,|Y|\right\}
;

•

or correctly establishes that
G
is a
\varphi^{\prime}
-expander, for
\varphi^{\prime}=\frac{\varphi}{2^{c_{0}/\epsilon^{6}}\cdot\log^{7}n}
.

The running time of the algorithm is
O\left(m^{1+O(\epsilon)+o(1)}\right)
.

Proof.

The proof is almost identical to the proof of
Theorem

11.3
. The only difference is that we set the parameter
z
that is used in the calls to Algorithm
MatchOrCut
from
11.2
to
1
. This ensures that no fake edges are introduced.

11.3
Sparsest Cut and Lowest-Conductance Cut – Proof of
Theorem

2.7

In this section we prove
Theorem

2.7
.

Theorem

11.4
immediately gives a deterministic
\left(2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\cdot\log^{7}n\right)
-approximation algorithm for the
Sparsest Cut problem with running time
O\left(m^{1+O(\epsilon)+o(1)}\right)
, for all
\epsilon\geq\frac{4}{(\log n)^{1/25}}
. Indeed, let
G
be the input
m
-edge graph. For
1\leq i\leq\left\lceil\log m\right\rceil
, let
\varphi_{i}=1/2^{i}
. For
1\leq i\leq\left\lceil\log m\right\rceil
, we apply the algorithm from
Theorem

11.4
to graph
G
, with the parameter
\varphi_{i}
. Let
i
be the smallest integer, for which the algorithm returned a cut
(X,Y)
with
|E_{G}(X,Y)|<\varphi_{i}\cdot\min\left\{|X|,|Y|\right\}
. Then, when applied to
G
with parameter
\varphi_{i+1}=\varphi_{i}/2
, the algorithm correctly established that
G
is a
\varphi^{\prime}
-expander, for
\varphi^{\prime}=\frac{\varphi_{i}}{2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\cdot\log^{7}n}
. In other words, the sparsity of the sparsest cut in
G
is at least
\varphi^{\prime}
. Therefore,
(X,Y)
is an
O(2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\cdot\log^{7}n)
-approximate sparsest cut. The running time of the algorithm remains
O\left(m^{1+O(\epsilon)+o(1)}\right)
. By setting
\epsilon=(1/c\log\log\log n)^{1/6}
, for a large enough constant
c
, we obtain a factor-
O(\log^{7}n\log\log n)
-approximation, in time
O\left(m^{1+o(1)}\right)
.

We now show that we can obtain an algorithm with similar guarantees for the
Lowest Conductance Cut
problem. The algorithm follows easily from the algorithm for the
Sparsest Cut
problem, and is identical to that of
[
CGL+20
]
. The only difference is that we are using the stronger algorithm for
Sparsest Cut
that we obtained. Let
G=(V,E)
be an input to the
Lowest Conductance Cut
problem, with
|V|=n
and
|E|=m
. Let
\frac{4}{(\log n)^{1/25}}<\epsilon<\frac{1}{400}
be a parameter. We start by obtaining a factor-
\left(2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\cdot\log^{7}n\right)
-approximation algorithm with running time
O\left(m^{1+O(\epsilon)+o(1)}\right)
.

Denote by
\psi
the conductance of the lowest-conductance cut in
G
. We can assume without loss of generality that
\psi<\frac{1}{2^{c/\epsilon^{6}}\cdot\log^{7}n}
for some large enough constant
c
, since otherwise we can let
v
be a lowest-degree vertex in
G
, and return the cut
(\left\{v\right\},V\setminus\left\{v\right\})
, whose conductance is
1
.
We use Algorithm
ReduceDegree
from
Section

11.1.1
, in order to construct, in time
O(m)
, a graph
\hat{G}
, whose maximum vertex degree is bounded by
10
, and
|V(\hat{G})|=2m
.

Note that, if we denote
\varphi
the value of the sparsest cut in
\hat{G}
, then
\varphi\leq\psi
must hold. This is since every cut
(A,B)
in
G
naturally defines a cut
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
in
\hat{G}
, with
|A^{\prime}|=\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(A),|B^{\prime}|=\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(B)
, and
|E_{\hat{G}}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})|=|E_{G}(A,B)|
. We use our approximation algorithm for the Sparsest Cut problem in graph
\hat{G}
, to obtain a cut
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
of
\hat{G}
, whose sparsity is at most
\left(2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\cdot\log^{7}n\right)\cdot\varphi
, in time
O\left(m^{1+O(\epsilon)+o(1)}\right)
.

Using Algorithm
MakeCanonical
from
Lemma

11.1
, we obtain a cut
(X^{\prime\prime},Y^{\prime\prime})
of
\hat{G}
, with
|X^{\prime\prime}|\geq|X^{\prime}|/2
,
|Y^{\prime\prime}|\geq|Y^{\prime}|/2
, and
|E_{\hat{G}}(X^{\prime\prime},Y^{\prime\prime})|\leq O(|E_{\hat{G}}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})|)\leq\left(2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\cdot\log^{7}n\right)\cdot\varphi\cdot\min\left\{|X^{\prime}|,|Y^{\prime}|\right\}\leq\left(2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\cdot\log^{7}n\right)\cdot\psi\cdot\min\left\{|X^{\prime\prime}|,|Y^{\prime\prime}|\right\}
, such that both
X^{\prime\prime}
and
Y^{\prime\prime}
are canonical vertex sets. This cut naturally defines a cut
(X,Y)
in
G
, with
\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(X)=|X^{\prime\prime}|
,
\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(Y)=|Y^{\prime\prime}|
, and
|E_{G}(X,Y)|=|E_{\hat{G}}(X^{\prime\prime},Y^{\prime\prime})|
. Therefore:

\begin{split}|E_{G}(X,Y)|&=|E_{\hat{G}}(X^{\prime\prime},Y^{\prime\prime})|\\
&\leq\left(2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\cdot\log^{7}n\right)\cdot\psi\cdot\min\left\{|X^{\prime\prime}|,|Y^{\prime\prime}|\right\}\\
&\leq\left(2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\cdot\log^{7}n\right)\cdot\psi\cdot\min\left\{\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(X),\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(Y)\right\}.\end{split}

We conclude that cut
(X,Y)
is a factor
(2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\cdot\log^{7}n)
-approximate solution to instance
G
of
Lowest Conductance Cut
.
Since the running time of Algorithm
MakeCanonical
is
O(m)
, the running time of the whole algorithm remains
bounded by
O\left(m^{1+O(\epsilon)+o(1)}\right)
. As before, by setting
\epsilon=(1/c\log\log\log n)^{1/6}
, for a large enough constant
c
, we obtain a factor-
O(\log^{7}n\log\log n)
-approximation for
Lowest Conductance Cut
, in time
O\left(m^{1+o(1)}\right)
.

11.4
Minimum Balanced Cut – Proof of Theorems
2.8
and
2.9

In this section we prove
Theorem

2.8
and
Theorem

2.9
. Our proof is somewhat more involved than that of
[
CGL+20
]
, who iteratively used the algorithm for
Most Balanced Sparse Cut
from (the weaker version of)
Theorem

11.3
. The reason is that, while our bounds for the parameters
\alpha
and
\alpha^{\prime}
are better than those obtained by
Theorem

11.3
, they are still super-logarithmic. Therefore, if we follow the framework of
[
CGL+20
]
, who apply the algorithm from
Theorem

11.3
over the course of
O(1/\epsilon)
iterations, we will still accumulate an approximation factor that is at least as high as
(\log n)^{\Theta(1/\epsilon)}
.

The proofs of
Theorem

2.8
and
Theorem

2.9
are very similar to each other, and we start with presenting the part that is common to both proofs.
Recall that we are given as input a graph
G
with
|V(G)|=n
,
|E(G)|=m
and a parameter
0<\psi\leq 1
.
We can assume w.l.o.g. that both
m
and
n
are greater than a large enough constant, since otherwise we can use the algorithm of
[
CGL+20
]
, whose running time can now be bounded by
O(m)
.

We use a parameter
\epsilon=1/(\log\log\log m)^{1/25}
. It is easy to verify that
m^{\epsilon}=2^{O(\log n/(\log\log\log n)^{1/25})}>\log^{4}n
must hold.
We can also assume that
\psi<1/(2^{c/\epsilon^{6}}\log^{8}n)
for a large enough constant
c
, since otherwise we can compute an arbitrary partition
(A,B)
of
V(G)
with
\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(A),\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(A)\geq\operatorname{Vol}(G)/3
(since for every vertex
v\in V(G)
,
\deg_{G}(v)<\operatorname{Vol}(G)/2
, such a partition can be computed by a simple greedy algorithm, that iteratively adds each vertex to a set of
\left\{A,B\right\}
, whose current volume is smaller). Clearly,
|E_{G}(A,B)|\leq m\leq\frac{\operatorname{Vol}(G)}{2}\leq\psi\cdot 2^{c/\epsilon^{6}}\cdot(\log^{8}n)\cdot\operatorname{Vol}(G)\leq\psi\cdot(\log n)^{8+o(1)}\cdot\operatorname{Vol}(G)
. Therefore, from now on we assume that
\psi<1/(2^{c/\epsilon^{6}}\log^{8}n)
for a large enough constant
c
.

As in the algorithm of
[
CGL+20
]
,
we start by applying Algorithm
ReduceDegree
from
Section

11.1.1
to graph
G
, in order to construct, in time
O(m)
, a graph
\hat{G}
whose maximum vertex degree is bounded by
10
, and
|V(\hat{G})|=2m
. Denote
V(G)=\left\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\right\}
. Recall that graph
\hat{G}
is constructed from graph
G
by replacing each vertex
v_{i}
with an
\alpha_{0}
-expander
H(v_{i})
on
\deg_{G}(v_{i})
vertices, where
\alpha_{0}=\Theta(1)
. For convenience, we denote the set of vertices of
H(v_{i})
by
V_{i}
. Therefore,
V(\hat{G})=V_{1}\cup V_{2}\cup\cdots\cup V_{n}
. Denote
|V(\hat{G})|=\hat{n}
. Consider now some subset
S
of vertices of
\hat{G}
. As before, we say that
S
is a
canonical
set of vertices if, for all
1\leq i\leq n
, either
V_{i}\subseteq S
or
V_{i}\cap S=\emptyset
holds.
Our starting point is the following lemma, that is an easy application of the
Cut-Matching Game
, combined with Algorithm
MakeCanonical
from
Lemma

11.1
. The proof is included in Section
B
of Appendix.

Lemma 11.5
.

There is a deterministic algorithm, whose input consists of a canonical set
V^{\prime}\subseteq V(\hat{G})
of vertices of
\hat{G}
, with
|V^{\prime}|\geq 2\hat{n}/3
,
and parameters
0<\varphi<1
and
\rho\geq\log n
. The algorithm
computes one of the following:

•

either a partition
(X,Y)
of
V^{\prime}
, where both
X,Y
are canonical subsets of
V(\hat{G})
,
|X|,|Y|\geq\rho
, and
|E_{\hat{G}}(X,Y)|\leq\varphi\cdot\min\left\{|X|,|Y|\right\}
;

•

or a
\varphi^{*}
-expander graph
H
with
V(H)=V^{\prime}
and maximum vertex degree
O(\log n)
, where
\varphi^{*}\geq\Omega\left(\frac{1}{2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\cdot\log^{5}n}\right)
, together with a set
F
of at most
O(\rho\cdot\log n)
edges of
H
, such that there exists an embedding
{\mathcal{P}}
of
H\setminus F
into
\hat{G}[V^{\prime}]
with congestion at most
O\left(\frac{\log^{2}n}{\varphi}\right)
.

The running time of the algorithm is
O\left(m^{1+O(\epsilon)+o(1)}\right)
.

We emphasize that the algorithm from the above lemma does not compute the embedding
{\mathcal{P}}
explicitly, and instead it only guarantees its existence.
We obtain the following immediate corollary of
Lemma

11.5
.
The corollary uses the parameter
\varphi^{*}\geq\Omega\left(\frac{1}{2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\cdot\log^{5}n}\right)
from
Lemma

11.5
.

Corollary 11.6
.

There is a deterministic algorithm, that, given a parameter
\rho\geq\log n
, and a parameter
c^{\prime}>1
, computes a cut
(X^{*},Y^{*})
in graph
\hat{G}
, such that both
X^{*}
and
Y^{*}
are canonical sets of vertices,
|X^{*}|\geq|Y^{*}|
, and
|E_{\hat{G}}(X^{*},Y^{*})|\leq\frac{c^{\prime}\psi\log^{3}n}{\varphi^{*}}\cdot\hat{n}
. Moreover, if
|Y^{*}|<\hat{n}/3
, then the algorithm also computes a
\varphi^{*}
-expander graph
H
with
V(H)=X^{*}
and maximum vertex degree
O(\log n)
, together with a set
F
of at most
O(\rho\log n)
edges of
H
, such that there exists an embedding
{\mathcal{P}}
of
H\setminus F
into
\hat{G}[X^{*}]
with congestion
\eta\leq O\left(\frac{\varphi^{*}}{c^{\prime}\psi\log n}\right)
.The running time of the algorithm is
O\left(\frac{m^{2+O(\epsilon)+o(1)}\cdot}{\rho}\right)
.

Proof.

The proof easily follows by iteratively applying the algorithm from
Lemma

11.5
.
Let
\varphi=\frac{c^{\prime}\psi\log^{3}n}{\varphi^{*}}
. Our algorithm consists of at most
r=\frac{\hat{n}}{\rho}
iterations. For all
1\leq i\leq r
, at the beginning of iteration
i
, we are given a partition
(X_{i},Y_{i})
of
V(\hat{G})
, such that both
X_{i},Y_{i}
are canonical sets,
|X_{i}|\geq\frac{2\hat{n}}{3}
, and
|E_{\hat{G}}(X_{i},Y_{i})|\leq\varphi\cdot|Y_{i}|
. At the beginning of the algorithm, we use the partition
(X_{1},Y_{1})
of
V(\hat{G})
, with
X_{1}=V(\hat{G})
and
Y_{1}=\emptyset
. We now describe the execution of the
i
th iteration.

In order to execute the
i
th iteration, we apply the algorithm from
Lemma

11.5
to set
V^{\prime}=X_{i}
of vertices of
\hat{G}
, and parameters
\rho
and
\varphi
, that remain unchanged. We now consider two cases.

Assume first, that the algorithm returned a partition
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
of
X_{i}
, with
|X^{\prime}|,|Y^{\prime}|\geq\rho
, and
|E_{\hat{G}}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})|\leq\varphi\cdot\min\left\{|X^{\prime}|,|Y^{\prime}|\right\}
, such that both
X^{\prime}
and
Y^{\prime}
are canonical sets of vertices. Assume w.l.o.g. that
|X^{\prime}|\geq|Y^{\prime}|
. We then construct a new cut
(X_{i+1},Y_{i+1})
in
\hat{G}
, by letting
X_{i+1}=X^{\prime}
and
Y_{i+1}=V(\hat{G})\setminus X^{\prime}=Y^{\prime}\cup Y_{i}
. Note that cut
(X_{i+1},Y_{i+1})
can be obtained from cut
(X_{i},Y_{i})
by moving the vertices of
Y^{\prime}
from
X_{i}
to
Y_{i}
. Therefore,
E_{\hat{G}}(X_{i+1},Y_{i+1})\subseteq E_{\hat{G}}(X_{i},Y_{i})\cup E_{\hat{G}}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
, and:

|E_{\hat{G}}(X_{i+1},Y_{i+1})|\leq|E_{\hat{G}}(X_{i},Y_{i})|+|E_{\hat{G}}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})|\leq\varphi\cdot|Y_{i}|+\varphi\cdot|Y^{\prime}|\leq\varphi\cdot|Y_{i+1}|.

If
|X_{i+1}|\geq 2\hat{n}/3
holds, then we continue to the next iteration. Otherwise, since
|X_{i}|\geq 2\hat{n}/3
, we get that
|X_{i+1}|\geq|X_{i}|/2\geq\hat{n}/3
. Therefore, we obtain a partition
(X_{i+1},Y_{i+1})
of
V(\hat{G})
with
|X_{i+1}|,|Y_{i+1}|\geq\hat{n}/3
, and
|E_{\hat{G}}(X_{i+1},Y_{i+1})|\leq\varphi\cdot|Y_{i+1}|\leq\varphi\cdot\hat{n}=\frac{c^{\prime}\psi\log^{3}n}{\varphi^{*}}\cdot\hat{n}
. We then return cut
(X^{*},Y^{*})=(X_{i+1},Y_{i+1})
and terminate the algorithm.

Next, we assume that the algorithm from
Lemma

11.5
returned
a
\varphi^{*}
-expander graph
H
with
V(H)=X_{i}
and maximum vertex degree
O(\log n)
, together with a set
F
of at most
O(\rho\cdot\log n)
edges of
H
, such that there exists an embedding
{\mathcal{P}}
of
H\setminus F
into
\hat{G}[X_{i}]
with congestion at most
O\left(\frac{\log^{2}n}{\varphi}\right)\leq O\left(\frac{\varphi^{*}}{c^{\prime}\psi\log n}\right)
. In this case, we return the cut
(X^{*},Y^{*})=(X_{i},Y_{i})
, graph
H
, and set
F
of edges.

It now remains to bound the running time of the algorithm. Notice that in every iteration, the cardinality of the set
Y_{i}
of vertices grows by at least
\rho
, and, since
|V(\hat{G})|=2m
, the number of iterations is bounded by
2m/\rho
. In each iteration we use the algorithm from
Lemma

11.5
, whose running time is at most
O\left(m^{1+O(\epsilon)+o(1)}\right)
. Overall, the running time of the algorithm is bounded by
O\left(\frac{m^{2+O(\epsilon)+o(1)}}{\rho}\right)
.

We are now ready to complete the proofs of
Theorem

2.8
and
Theorem

2.9
.

11.4.1
Proof of
Theorem

2.8

We first consider a special case, where
\psi<\frac{\log^{4}m}{m}
. In this case, our algorithm simply repeatedly cuts off low-conductance cuts from graph
G
. Specifically, we perform a number of iterations, and we maintain a vertex-induced subgraph
G^{\prime}\subseteq G
. We also maintain a cut
(A,B)
in
G
, with
A=V(G^{\prime})
and
B=V(G)\setminus V(G^{\prime})
. Initially, we set
G^{\prime}=G
,
A=V(G)
, and
B=\emptyset
. The algorithm continues as long as
\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(A)\geq 2\operatorname{Vol}(G)/3
.
Let
\alpha=O(\log^{7}n\log\log n)
be the approximation factor that the algorithm for the
Lowest Conductance Cut
problem from
Theorem

2.7
achieves.
Throughout the algorithm, we will ensure that
|E_{G}(A,B)|\leq\alpha\cdot\psi\cdot\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(B)
holds.

In order to execute a single iteration, we apply the approximation algorithm for the
Lowest Conductance Cut
problem from
Theorem

2.7
to the current graph
G^{\prime}
. Let
(X,Y)
be the cut that the algorithm returns, and let
\psi^{\prime}=\frac{|E_{G^{\prime}}(X,Y)|}{\min\left\{\operatorname{Vol}_{G^{\prime}}(X),\operatorname{Vol}_{G^{\prime}}(Y)\right\}}
be the conductance of the cut. Assume first that
\psi^{\prime}>\alpha\psi
. Then we are guaranteed that graph
G^{\prime}
has conductance at least
\psi
. We return the cut
(A,B)
and terminate the algorithm. Since
|E_{G}(A,B)|\leq\alpha\cdot\psi\cdot\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(B)\leq\psi\cdot(\log n)^{7+o(1)}\cdot\operatorname{Vol}(G)
, this is a valid output for the algorithm.

Assume now that
\psi^{\prime}\leq\alpha\psi
, and assume w.l.o.g. that
\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(X)\geq\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(Y)
. Then
|E_{G^{\prime}}(X,Y)|\leq\psi^{\prime}\cdot\operatorname{Vol}_{G^{\prime}}(Y)\leq\alpha\psi\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(Y)
. Consider a new cut
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
in
G
, where
A^{\prime}=X
and
B^{\prime}=V(G)\setminus X=B\cup Y
. It is then easy to verify that:

|E_{G}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})|\leq|E_{G}(A,B)|+|E_{G^{\prime}}(X,Y)|\leq\alpha\psi\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(A)+\alpha\psi\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(Y)\leq\alpha\psi\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(B^{\prime}).

If
\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(A^{\prime})=\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(X)\geq 2\operatorname{Vol}(G)/3
, then we replace cut
(A,B)
with cut
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
, and continue to the next iteration. Otherwise,
\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(A^{\prime})\geq\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(A)/2\geq\operatorname{Vol}(G)/3
must hold, and
\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(B^{\prime})\geq\operatorname{Vol}(G)-\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(A^{\prime})\geq\operatorname{Vol}(G)/3
holds as well. We return cut
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
and terminate the algorithm. As before, since
|E_{G}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})|\leq\alpha\cdot\psi\cdot\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(B^{\prime})\leq\psi\cdot(\log n)^{7+o(1)}\cdot\operatorname{Vol}(G)
, this is a valid output of the algorithm.

It now remains to analyze the running time of the algorithm. The number of iterations in the algorithm is bounded by
O(m)
, and the running time of a single iteration is dominated by the running time of the algorithm from

Theorem

2.7
, which is bounded by
O\left(m^{1+o(1)}\right)
. Therefore, the running time of the algorithm is bounded by
O\left(m^{2+o(1)}\right)\leq O\left(m^{1+o(1)}/\psi\right)
, since we have assumed that
\psi<\frac{\log^{4}m}{m}
.

In the remainder of the proof, we assume that
\psi\geq\frac{\log^{4}m}{m}
.
We start by computing a graph
\hat{G}
exactly as described above, and then applying the algorithm from
Corollary

11.6
to it, with parameter
\rho=\frac{\psi m}{\tilde{c}^{2}\log n}
, where
\tilde{c}
is a large constant, whose value we set later, and parameter
c^{\prime}
, that is a large enough constant, whose value we set later. Since
\psi\geq\frac{\log^{4}m}{m}
, and
m
is large enough, we get that
\rho>\log n
. Let
(X^{*},Y^{*})
be the outcome of the algorithm.

Recall that both
X^{*}
and
Y^{*}
are canonical sets of vertices,
|X^{*}|\geq|Y^{*}|
, and
|E_{\hat{G}}(X^{*},Y^{*})|\leq\frac{c^{\prime}\psi\log^{3}n}{\varphi^{*}}\cdot\hat{n}
. Notice that cut
(X^{*},Y^{*})
in
\hat{G}
naturally defines a cut
(A,B)
in
G
: for every vertex
v_{i}\in V(G)
, we add
v_{i}
to
A
if
V_{i}\subseteq X^{*}
, and we add it to
B
otherwise. It is immediate to verify that
\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(A)=|X^{*}|
,
\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(B)=|Y^{*}|
, and
|E_{G}(A,B)|=|E_{\hat{G}}(X^{*},Y^{*})|\leq\frac{c^{\prime}\psi\log^{3}n}{\varphi^{*}}\cdot\hat{n}\leq\psi\cdot 2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\cdot(\log^{8}n)\cdot\operatorname{Vol}(G)\leq\psi\cdot(\log n)^{8+o(1)}\cdot\operatorname{Vol}(G)
, since
\varphi^{*}\geq\Omega\left(\frac{1}{2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\cdot\log^{5}n}\right)
and
\epsilon=1/(\log\log\log m)^{1/25}
.

Consider first the case that
|Y^{*}|\geq\hat{n}/3
. Then, from the above discussion,
\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(A),\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(B)\geq\hat{n}/3=\operatorname{Vol}(G)/3
. In this case,
we return cut
(A,B)
as the outcome of the algorithm.

We assume from now on that
|Y^{*}|<\hat{n}/3
, and so the algorithm from
Corollary

11.6
computed a
\varphi^{*}
-expander graph
H
with
V(H)=X^{*}
and maximum vertex degree
O(\log n)
, together with a set
F
of at most
O(\rho\log n)
edges of
H
, such that there exists an embedding
{\mathcal{P}}
of
H\setminus F
into
\hat{G}[X^{*}]
with congestion at most
\eta
, where
\eta\leq O\left(\frac{\varphi^{*}}{c^{\prime}\psi\log n}\right)
.
Since
\rho=\frac{\psi m}{\tilde{c}^{2}\log n}
, and
\tilde{c}
is a sufficiently large constant, we get that
|F|\leq\frac{\psi m}{\tilde{c}}
.
Let
\hat{G}^{\prime}
be the graph that is obtained from
\hat{G}[X^{*}]
, by adding the edges of
F
to it. We need the following simple observation.

Observation 11.7
.

Graph
\hat{G}^{\prime}
is a
\varphi^{\prime}
-expander, for
\varphi^{\prime}=\Omega(c^{\prime}\psi\log n)
.

Proof.

Consider any partition
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
of
V(\hat{G}^{\prime})
, with
|A^{\prime}|\leq|B^{\prime}|
. It is enough to prove that
|E_{\hat{G}^{\prime}}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})|\geq\varphi^{\prime}\cdot|A^{\prime}|
. Recall that the set
{\mathcal{P}}
of paths defines an embedding of
H\setminus F
into
\hat{G}[X^{*}]
with congestion at most
\eta
. By embedding every edge of
F
into itself, we can augment the set
{\mathcal{P}}
of paths to obtain an embedding of
H
into
\hat{G}^{\prime}=\hat{G}[X^{*}]\cup F
, with congestion at most
\eta
.

Notice that cut
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
in
\hat{G}^{\prime}
also defines a cut in graph
H
. Since graph
H
is a
\varphi^{*}
-expander, if we denote by
E^{\prime}=E_{H}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
, then
|E^{\prime}|\geq\varphi^{*}\cdot|A^{\prime}|
. Consider now the set
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}=\left\{P(e)\mid e\in E^{\prime}\right\}
of paths, where for each edge
e\in E^{\prime}
,
P(e)
is the path of
{\mathcal{P}}
that serves as an embedding of the edge
e
. Then every path in
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
must contain an edge of
E_{\hat{G}^{\prime}}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
, and the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
cause congestion at most
\eta
. Therefore,
|E_{\hat{G}^{\prime}}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})|\geq\frac{|E^{\prime}|}{\eta}\geq\frac{\varphi^{*}\cdot|A^{\prime}|}{\eta}\geq\Omega(c^{\prime}\psi|A^{\prime}|\log n)=\varphi^{\prime}\cdot|A^{\prime}|
, since
\eta\leq O\left(\frac{\varphi^{*}}{c^{\prime}\psi\log n}\right)
.

Recall that we have used the cut
(X^{*},Y^{*})
in
\hat{G}
to define a cut
(A,B)
in
G
, with
\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(A)=|X^{*}|
,
\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(B)=|Y^{*}|
, and
|E_{G}(A,B)|=|E_{\hat{G}}(X^{*},Y^{*})|\leq\psi\cdot(\log n)^{8+o(1)}\cdot\operatorname{Vol}(G)
. Consider now a graph
G^{\prime}
, that, intuitively, is obtained from
G[A]
, by adding the edges of
F
to it. Formally, in order to obtain graph
G^{\prime}
, we start from graph
G[A]
, and the consider the edges
e\in F
one by one. Let
e=(x,y)
be any such edge, and let
v_{i},v_{j}\in A
be the vertices with
x\in V_{i}
and
y\in V_{j}
. If
i\neq j
, then we add edge
e^{\prime}=(v_{i},v_{j})
to graph
G^{\prime}
, and we think of
e^{\prime}
as a
copy of edge
e
. Notice that graph
G^{\prime}
can be equivalently obtained from graph
\hat{G}^{\prime}
by contracting, for every vertex
v_{i}\in A
, all vertices of
V_{i}
into a single node.
Next, we use the following easy observation:

Observation 11.8
.

Graph
G^{\prime}
has conductance at least
24\psi
.

Proof.

Consider any cut
(X,Y)
in
G^{\prime}
. We can naturally define a corresponding cut
(\hat{X},\hat{Y})
in graph
\hat{G}^{\prime}
: for every vertex
v_{i}\in X
, we add all vertices of
V_{i}
to
\hat{X}
, and for every vertex
v_{i}\in Y
, we add all vertices of
V_{i}
to
\hat{Y}
. It is easy to verify that
|\hat{X}|=\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(X)
. Since every vertex of
X^{*}
is incident to at most
c^{*}\log n
edges of
F
, for some constant
c^{*}
(as maximum vertex degree in
H
is
O(\log n)
), it is easy to verify that, for every vertex
v_{i}\in A
,
\deg_{G^{\prime}}(v_{i})\leq(c^{*}\log n)\deg_{G}(v_{i})
. Therefore,
\operatorname{Vol}_{G^{\prime}}(X)\leq(c^{*}\log n)\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(X)
, and so
|\hat{X}|\geq\frac{\operatorname{Vol}_{G^{\prime}}(X)}{c^{*}\log n}
. Using similar reasoning,
|\hat{Y}|\geq\frac{\operatorname{Vol}_{G^{\prime}}(Y)}{c^{*}\log n}
. Lastly, since, from
11.7
,
graph
\hat{G}^{\prime}
is a
\varphi^{\prime}
-expander, for
\varphi^{\prime}=\Omega(c^{\prime}\psi\log n)
, we get that
|E_{G^{\prime}}(X,Y)|=|E_{\hat{G}^{\prime}}(\hat{X},\hat{Y})|\geq\varphi^{\prime}\cdot\min\left\{|\hat{X}|,|\hat{Y}|\right\}\geq\Omega\left(\frac{c^{\prime}\psi}{c^{*}}\right)\cdot\min\left\{\operatorname{Vol}_{G^{\prime}}(X),\operatorname{Vol}_{G^{\prime}}(Y)\right\}
. Since
c^{*}
is a fixed constant, and we can set
c^{\prime}
to be a large enough constant, we get that
|E_{G^{\prime}}(X,Y)|\geq 24\psi\cdot\min\left\{\operatorname{Vol}_{G^{\prime}}(X),\operatorname{Vol}_{G^{\prime}}(Y)\right\}
. We conclude that the conductance of graph
G^{\prime}
is at least
24\psi
.

Next, we use the following pruning theorem of
[
SW19
]
, that works with graph conductance instead of expansion.

Theorem 11.9
(Restatement of Theorem 1.3 from
[
SW19
]
).

There is a deterministic algorithm, that, given access to adjacency list of a graph
G=(V,E)
that has conductance
\psi
, for some
0<\psi\leq 1
, and a collection
E^{\prime}\subseteq E
of
{k\leq\psi|E|/10}
edges of
G
, computes a subgraph
G^{\prime}\subseteq G\setminus E^{\prime}
, that has conductance at least
\psi/6
. Moreover, if we denote
A=V(G^{\prime})
and
B=V(G)\setminus A
, then
|E_{G}(A,B)|\leq 4k
, and
\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(B)\leq 8k/\psi
.
The running time of the algorithm is
{\tilde{O}(k/\psi^{2})}
.

We apply the algorithm from
Theorem

11.9
to graph
G^{\prime}
, conductance parameter
24\psi
, and the set
E^{\prime}=F
of edges.
Since we have assumed that
|Y^{*}|<\hat{n}/3=\operatorname{Vol}(G)/3
, we get that
|X^{*}|\geq 2\hat{n}/3\geq 2\operatorname{Vol}(G)/3
. At the same time,
|E_{G}(X^{*},Y^{*})|<\psi\cdot 2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\cdot(\log^{8}n)\cdot\operatorname{Vol}(G)<\operatorname{Vol}(G)/10
, since we have assumed that
\psi<\frac{1}{2^{c/\epsilon^{6}}\cdot(\log^{8}n)}
for a large enough constant
c
. Therefore,
|E(G^{\prime})|\geq\frac{\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(X^{*})-|E_{G}(X^{*},Y^{*})|}{2}\geq\frac{\operatorname{Vol}(G)}{4}\geq\frac{m}{2}
.
Recall that
|F|\leq\frac{\psi m}{\tilde{c}}
holds, where
\tilde{c}
is a large enough constant. Therefore,
|F|\leq\frac{24\psi|E(G^{\prime})|}{10}
.

The algorithm must then return a partition
(Z,Z^{\prime})
of
V(G^{\prime})=A
, such that graph
G[Z]
has conductance at least
\frac{24\psi}{6}\geq\psi
, and
\operatorname{Vol}_{G^{\prime}}(Z^{\prime})\leq\frac{8|F|}{24\psi}\leq\frac{m}{3\tilde{c}}
, while
|E_{G}(Z,Z^{\prime})|\leq 4|F|\leq\frac{4\psi m}{\tilde{c}}\leq O(\psi)\cdot\operatorname{Vol}(G)
.

We construct a cut
(A^{*},B^{*})
in graph
G
, by letting
A^{*}=Z
and
B^{*}=Z^{\prime}\cup B
.
Notice that
|E_{G}(A^{*},B^{*})|\subseteq|E_{G}(A,B)|+|E_{G}(Z,Z^{\prime})|\leq\psi\cdot(\log n)^{8+o(1)}\cdot\operatorname{Vol}(G)
. Additionally, we get that:

\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(A^{*})=\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(Z)\geq\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(A)-\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(Z^{\prime})\geq\frac{2\operatorname{Vol}(G)}{3}-\frac{m}{3\tilde{c}}\geq\frac{7\operatorname{Vol}(G)}{12}.

If
\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(B^{*})\geq\operatorname{Vol}(G)/3
, then we get that
\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(A^{*}),\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(B^{*})\geq\operatorname{Vol}(G)/3
. Otherwise, we are guaranteed that
\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(A^{*})\geq 2\operatorname{Vol}(G)/3
, and that the conductance of graph
G[A^{*}]
is at least
\psi
.

It now remains to bound the running time of the algorithm. The running time of the algorithm from
Corollary

11.6
is bounded by

O\left(\frac{m^{2+O(\epsilon)+o(1)}}{\rho}\right)\leq O\left(\frac{m^{1+o(1)}}{\psi}\right)
, since
\rho=\frac{\psi m}{\tilde{c}^{2}\log n}
and
\epsilon=1/(\log\log\log m)^{1/25}
. The running time of the algorithm from
Theorem

11.9
is
\tilde{O}\left(\frac{|F|}{\psi^{2}}\right)\leq\tilde{O}\left(\frac{m}{\psi}\right)
. Therefore, the total running time of the algorithm is bounded by
O\left(\frac{m^{1+o(1)}}{\psi}\right)
.

11.4.2
Proof of
Theorem

2.9

The proof is very similar to the proof of
Theorem

2.8
. The main difference is that we set
\rho=m^{1-2\epsilon}
, and that we do not employ

Theorem

11.9
.

We start by computing a graph
\hat{G}
exactly as before, and then applying the algorithm from
Corollary

11.6
to it, with parameter
\rho=m^{1-2\epsilon}
, and
c^{\prime}
a large constant whose value we set later. Let
(X^{*},Y^{*})
be the outcome of the algorithm.
Recall that both
X^{*}
and
Y^{*}
are canonical sets of vertices,
|X^{*}|\geq|Y^{*}|
, and
|E_{\hat{G}}(X^{*},Y^{*})|\leq\frac{c^{\prime}\psi\log^{3}n}{\varphi^{*}}\cdot\hat{n}\leq(\log n)^{8+o(1)}\cdot\operatorname{Vol}(G)
, since
\varphi^{*}\geq\Omega\left(\frac{1}{2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\cdot\log^{5}n}\right)
and
\epsilon=\frac{1}{(\log\log\log n)^{1/25}}
. We use cut
(X^{*},Y^{*})
in
\hat{G}
in order to define a cut
(A,B)
in
G
exactly as before. As before,
\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(A)=|X^{*}|
,
\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(B)=|Y^{*}|
, and
|E_{G}(A,B)|=|E_{\hat{G}}(X^{*},Y^{*})|\leq\psi\cdot(\log n)^{8+o(1)}\cdot\operatorname{Vol}(G)
.
As before, if
|Y^{*}|\geq\hat{n}/3
, then
\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(A),\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(B)\geq\hat{n}/3=\operatorname{Vol}(G)/3
, and
we return cut
(A,B)
as the outcome of the algorithm.

We assume from now on that
|Y^{*}|<\hat{n}/3
, and so the algorithm from
Corollary

11.6
computed a
\varphi^{*}
-expander graph
H
with
V(H)=X^{*}
and maximum vertex degree
O(\log n)
, together with a set
F
of at most
O(\rho\log n)
edges of
H
, such that there exists an embedding
{\mathcal{P}}
of
H\setminus F
into
\hat{G}[X^{*}]
with congestion at most
\eta
, where
\eta\leq O\left(\frac{\varphi^{*}}{c^{\prime}\psi\log n}\right)
.
Notice that in this case,
\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(A)\geq|X^{*}|\geq 2\hat{n}/3=2\operatorname{Vol}(G)/3
holds.

Consider any partition
(Z,Z^{\prime})
of
A
, with
|E_{G}(Z,Z^{\prime})|<\psi\cdot\operatorname{Vol}(G)
, and assume w.l.o.g. that
\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(Z)\leq\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(Z^{\prime})
. We claim that
\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(Z)<\operatorname{Vol}(G)/100
holds. Indeed, assume otherwise. Consider a cut
(\hat{Z},\hat{Z}^{\prime})
in graph
H
, obtained as follows: for every vertex
v_{i}\in Z
, we add all vertices of
V_{i}
to
\hat{Z}
, and for every vertex
v_{j}\in Z^{\prime}
, we add all vertices of
V_{j}
to
\hat{Z}^{\prime}
. Clearly,
|\hat{Z}|=\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(Z)\geq\operatorname{Vol}(G)/100
, and similarly
|\hat{Z}^{\prime}|\geq\operatorname{Vol}(G)/100
. Since graph
H
is a
\varphi^{*}
-expander,
|E_{H}(\hat{Z},\hat{Z}^{\prime})|\geq\varphi^{*}\cdot\min\left\{|\hat{Z}|,|\hat{Z}^{\prime}|\right\}\geq\frac{\varphi^{*}\cdot\operatorname{Vol}(G)}{100}
. Denote
E^{\prime}=E_{H}(\hat{Z},\hat{Z}^{\prime})
.
Recall that
\varphi^{*}\geq\Omega\left(\frac{1}{2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\cdot\log^{5}n}\right)
, and so
|E^{\prime}|\geq\Omega\left(\frac{m}{2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\cdot\log^{5}n}\right)
. On the other hand,
|F|\leq O(\rho\log n)\leq O(m^{1-2\epsilon}\cdot\log n)
. From our choice of
\epsilon=\frac{1}{(\log\log\log n)^{1/25}}
, we get that
|F|<|E^{\prime}|/2
, and so
|E^{\prime}\setminus F|\geq\frac{\varphi^{*}\cdot\operatorname{Vol}(G)}{200}
. Since there exists an
embedding of
H\setminus F
into
\hat{G}[X^{*}]
with congestion at most
\eta
, and
\eta\leq O\left(\frac{\varphi^{*}}{c^{\prime}\psi\log n}\right)
, we get that:

|E_{\hat{G}}(\hat{Z},\hat{Z}^{\prime})|\geq\frac{|E^{\prime}|}{2\eta}\geq\Omega(c^{\prime}\psi\operatorname{Vol}(G)\log n).

Since
|E_{G}(Z,Z^{\prime})|=|E_{\hat{G}}(\hat{Z},\hat{Z}^{\prime})|
, we reach a contradiction to our assumption that
|E_{G}(Z,Z^{\prime})|<\psi\cdot\operatorname{Vol}(G)
. We conclude that for every partition
(Z,Z^{\prime})
of
A
with
\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(Z),\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(Z^{\prime})\geq\operatorname{Vol}(G)/100
,
|E_{G}(Z,Z^{\prime})|\geq\psi\cdot\operatorname{Vol}(G)
must hold.

The running time of the algorithm is asymptotically bounded by the running time of the algorithm from
Corollary

11.6
, which is in turn bounded by

O\left(\frac{m^{2+O(\epsilon)+o(1)}}{\rho}\right)\leq O\left(m^{1+O(\epsilon)+o(1)}\right)\leq O(m^{1+o(1)})
, since
\rho=m^{1-2\epsilon}
and
\epsilon=1/(\log\log\log m)^{1/25}
.

11.5
Expander Decomposition – Proof of
Theorem

2.10

In this section we prove
Theorem

2.10
. The proof is essentially identical to the proof of Corollary 6.1 from
[
CGL+20
]
. The only difference is that we use our algorithm from
Theorem

2.8
instead of the algorithm of
[
CGL+20
]
.

We maintain a collection
{\mathcal{H}}
of disjoint vertex-induced subgraphs of
G
that we call
clusters
, which is partitioned into two subsets, set
{\mathcal{H}}^{A}
of
active clusters
, and set
{\mathcal{H}}^{I}
of
inactive clusters
. We ensure that for every inactive cluster
H\in{\mathcal{H}}^{I}
, the conductance of
H
is at least
\psi
. We also maintain a set
E^{\prime}
of “deleted” edges, that are not contained in any cluster in
{\mathcal{H}}
. At the beginning of the algorithm, we let
{\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{H}}^{A}=\left\{G\right\}
,
{\mathcal{H}}^{I}=\emptyset
, and
E^{\prime}=\emptyset
. The algorithm proceeds as long
{\mathcal{H}}^{A}\neq\emptyset
, and consists of iterations.
For convenience, we denote
\alpha=(\log n)^{8+o(1)}
, so that the algorithm from
Theorem

2.8
, when applied to an
n
-vertex graph
G
and some parameter
\psi^{\prime}
, is guaranteed to return a cut
(A,B)
in
G
with
|E_{G}(A,B)|\leq\psi^{\prime}\cdot\alpha\cdot\operatorname{Vol}(G)
.
We set
\psi=\frac{\delta}{c\alpha\cdot\log n}
, where
c
is the constant from the theorem statement. Clearly,
\psi=\Omega\left(\frac{\delta}{(\log n)^{9+o(1)}}\right)
.

In every iteration, we apply the algorithm from
Theorem

2.8
to every graph
H\in{\mathcal{H}}^{A}
, with the parameter
\psi
. Consider the cut
(A,B)
in
H
that the algorithm returns, with
|E_{H}(A,B)|\leq\alpha\psi\cdot\operatorname{Vol}(H)\leq\frac{\delta\cdot\operatorname{Vol}(H)}{c\log n}
. We add the edges of
E_{H}(A,B)
to set
E^{\prime}
. If
\operatorname{Vol}_{H}(A),\operatorname{Vol}_{H}(B)\geq\operatorname{Vol}(H)/3
, then we replace
H
with
H[A]
and
H[B]
in
{\mathcal{H}}
and in
{\mathcal{H}}^{A}
. Otherwise, we are guaranteed that

\operatorname{Vol}_{H}(A)\geq 2\operatorname{Vol}(H)/3
, and graph
H[A]
has conductance at least
\psi
. Then we remove
H
from
{\mathcal{H}}
and
{\mathcal{H}}^{A}
, add
H[A]
to
{\mathcal{H}}
and
{\mathcal{H}}^{I}
, and add
H[B]
to
{\mathcal{H}}
and
{\mathcal{H}}^{A}
.

When the algorithm terminates,
{\mathcal{H}}^{A}=\emptyset
, and so every graph in
{\mathcal{H}}
has conductance at least
\psi
. Notice that in every iteration, the maximum volume of a graph in
{\mathcal{H}}^{A}
must decrease by a constant factor. Therefore, the number of iterations is bounded by
O(\log m)
. It is easy to verify that the number of edges added to set
E^{\prime}
in every iteration is at most
\alpha\cdot\psi\cdot\operatorname{Vol}(G)\leq\frac{\delta\cdot\operatorname{Vol}(G)}{c\log m}
. Therefore, by letting
c
be a large enough constant, we can ensure that
|E^{\prime}|\leq\delta\cdot\operatorname{Vol}(G)
. The output of the algorithm is the partition
\Pi=\left\{V(H)\mid H\in{\mathcal{H}}\right\}
of
V
. From the above discussion, we obtain a valid
(\delta,\psi)
-expander decomposition, for
\psi=\Omega\left(\frac{\delta}{(\log m)^{9+o(1)}}\right)
.

It remains to analyze the running time of the algorithm. The running time of a single iteration is bounded by
O(m^{1+o(1)}/\psi)
, and, since the number of iterations is
O(\log m)
, the total running time of the algorithm is bounded by
O(m^{1+o(1)}/\psi)\leq O(m^{1+o(1)}/\delta)
.

Appendix A
Proof of
Lemma

4.1

The proof uses a standard ball-growing technique.
Let
H=G\setminus E^{\prime}
.
Let
S
be any set of vertices of
H
, such that not all vertices of
S
are isolated in
H
. We define
L_{0}=S
, and, for integers
i>0
, we define
L_{i}=B_{H}(S,i)
. We refer to the sets
L_{0},L_{1},\ldots
of vertices as
BFS layers
defined with respect to
S
. For an index
1\leq i<\left\lfloor d/2\right\rfloor
, we say that layer
L_{i}
is
acceptable
if
|\delta_{H}(L_{i})|<\frac{\varphi}{2}\cdot|E_{H}(L_{i})|
. We use the following simple claim:

Claim A.1
.

Let
B=B_{H}(S,d)
, and assume that
|E_{H}(B)|<|E(H)|
. Then there is an index
1\leq i<\left\lfloor d/2\right\rfloor-1
, such that layer
L_{i}
is acceptable.

Proof.

Assume otherwise. Then for all
1\leq i<\left\lfloor d/2\right\rfloor-1
, layer
L_{i}
is not acceptable, and so
|\delta_{H}(L_{i})|\geq\frac{\varphi}{2}\cdot|E_{H}(L_{i})|
. Therefore,
|E_{H}(L_{i+1})|\geq\left(1+\frac{\varphi}{2}\right)|E_{H}(L_{i})|
. Since not all vertices of
S
are isolated in
H
, we get that
|E_{H}(L_{1})|\geq 1
. Overall, we get that:

|E_{H}(L_{\left\lfloor d/2\right\rfloor-1})|\geq\left(1+\frac{\varphi}{2}\right)^{\left\lfloor d/2\right\rfloor-2}\geq\left(1+\frac{\varphi}{2}\right)^{(8\log m)/\varphi}\geq e^{2\log m}>m,

(we have used the fact that for all
k>1
,
\left(1+\frac{1}{k}\right)^{k+1}>e
). This is impossible, so there must be an index
1\leq i<\left\lfloor d/2\right\rfloor-1
for which layer
L_{i}
is acceptable.

We are now ready to complete the proof of
Lemma

4.1
.
We denote the BFS layers in graph
H
defined with respect to
X
by
L_{0}^{\prime},L_{1}^{\prime},\ldots
, and BFS layers defined with respect to
Y
by
L_{0}^{\prime\prime},L_{1}^{\prime\prime},\ldots
. We
run two algorithms in parallel. The first algorithm performs a BFS search in
H
starting from
X
to compute layers
L_{0}^{\prime},L_{1}^{\prime},\ldots
one by one. When a layer
L_{i}^{\prime}
is computed, the algorithm checks whether it is acceptable. The second algorithm similarly performs a BFS search starting from
Y
to compute layers
L_{0}^{\prime\prime},L_{1}^{\prime\prime},\ldots
one by one, and, when a layer
L_{i}^{\prime\prime}
is computed, the algorithm checks whether it is acceptable. The two algorithms run in parallel, so that at every time point both algorithms have explored the same number of edges of
H
. The moment one of the two algorithms finds an acceptable layer, we terminate both algorithms.

Assume w.l.o.g. that the first acceptable layer that was computed by either algorithm is
L_{i}^{\prime}
. We then set
X^{\prime}=L_{i}^{\prime}
and
Y^{\prime}=V(G)\setminus X^{\prime}
.
Note that, from
A.1
,
i<d/2
must hold, so
X^{\prime}\cap Y=\emptyset
(as
\mbox{\sf dist}_{H}(X,Y)\geq d
). Clearly,
X\subseteq X^{\prime}
and
Y\subseteq Y^{\prime}
.
From the definition of an acceptable layer, we are guaranteed that
|E_{H}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})|=|\delta_{H}(X^{\prime})|<\frac{\varphi}{2}\cdot|E_{H}(L^{\prime}_{i})|=\frac{\varphi}{2}\cdot|E_{H}(X^{\prime})|
. Since we ensured that the number of edges that the two BFS searches explore at every time point is the same, we are guaranteed that
|E_{H}(X^{\prime})|\leq|E(Y^{\prime})|
. The running time of the algorithm is bounded by
O(|E_{H}(X^{\prime})|+|\delta_{H}(X^{\prime})|+n)\leq O(|E_{H}(X^{\prime})|+n)\leq O(|E_{G}(X^{\prime})|+n)
.

Lastly, observe that
|E_{G}(X^{\prime})|\geq|E_{H}(X^{\prime})|
and
|E_{G}(Y^{\prime})|\geq|E_{H}(Y^{\prime})|
. Furthermore:

|E_{G}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})|\leq|E^{\prime}|+|E_{H}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})|\leq\frac{\varphi}{4}|X|+\frac{\varphi}{2}\cdot\min\left\{|E_{H}(X^{\prime})|,|E_{H}(Y^{\prime})|\right\}\leq\varphi\cdot\min\left\{|E_{G}(X^{\prime})|,|E_{G}(Y^{\prime})|\right\},

since
|X|=|Y|
, and graph
G
is connected, so
|X|\leq 2\min\left\{|E_{G}(X^{\prime})|,|E_{G}(Y^{\prime})|\right\}
.

Appendix B
Proof of
Lemma

11.5

The proof of the lemma is a simple application of the
Cut-Matching Game
.
Let
G^{\prime}=\hat{G}[V^{\prime}]
, and let
n^{\prime}=|V(G^{\prime})|
. If
n^{\prime}
is an odd integer, then we add an extra vertex
v_{0}
to
G^{\prime}
, and connect it with an edge to an arbitrary vertex of
G^{\prime}
.
We let
H
be a graph with
V(H)=V(G^{\prime})
and
E(H)=\emptyset
.

We will execute the
Cut-Matching Game
on graph
H
, while simultaneously computing an embedding of
H
into
G^{\prime}
. Some of the edges of
H
will be designated as fake edges and added to the set
F
of fake edges. These edges do not need to be embedded into
G^{\prime}
. Initially,
F=\emptyset
.

We perform a number of iterations, that correspond to the
Cut-Matching Game
. In every iteration
i
, we will add a matching
{M}_{i}
to graph
H
, and a set
F_{i}\subseteq{M}_{i}
of fake edges to set
F
. We will also implicitly maintain embedding
{\mathcal{P}}_{i}
of the set
{M}_{i}\setminus F_{i}
of edges into
G^{\prime}
(in other words, the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}_{i}
are not computed explicitly, but are only guaranteed to exist). We will ensure that the number of iterations is bounded by
O(\log n^{\prime})\leq O(\log n)
, so the maximum vertex degree in
H
is always bounded by
\Delta_{H}\leq O(\log n)
. At the beginning of the algorithm, graph
H
contains the set
V[G^{\prime}]
of vertices and no edges. We now describe the execution of the
i
th iteration.

In order to execute the
i
th iteration, we apply Algorithm from
Theorem

2.6
to the current graph
H
, with parameter
\epsilon
remaining unchanged.
Notice that, since
\epsilon=\frac{1}{(\log\log\log n)^{1/25}}
, and
m
is large enough,
\epsilon>\frac{8}{(\log m)^{1/25}}
holds. Since
|V(G^{\prime})|\geq\frac{2\hat{n}}{3}\geq m
, we are guaranteed that
\epsilon>\frac{2}{(\log n^{\prime})^{1/25}}
, so that condition of
Theorem

2.6
holds.

Assume first that the output of the algorithm from
Theorem

2.6
is a cut
(A_{i},B_{i})
in
H
with
|A_{i}|,|B_{i}|\geq n^{\prime}/4
and
|E_{H}(A,B)|\leq n^{\prime}/100
. We treat this partition as the move of the Cut Player. Assume w.l.o.g. that
|A_{i}|\leq|B_{i}|
. Next, we compute an arbitrary partition
(A^{\prime}_{i},B^{\prime}_{i})
of
V(G^{\prime})
with
|A^{\prime}_{i}|=|B^{\prime}_{i}|
and
A^{\prime}_{i}\subseteq A_{i}
.
We apply Algorithm
MatchOrCut
from
Theorem

11.2
to graph
G^{\prime}
, the sets
A^{\prime}_{i},B^{\prime}_{i}
of vertices, a sparsity parameter
\varphi^{\prime}=\varphi/c
, where
c
is a large enough constant, and parameter
z=8\rho
. Next, we consider two cases. The first case happens if the algorithm returns a cut
(X,Y)
in
G^{\prime}
, with
|X|,|Y|\geq z/2\geq 4\rho
, and
|E_{G^{\prime}}(X,Y)|\leq\varphi^{\prime}\cdot\min\left\{|X|,|Y|\right\}=\frac{\varphi}{c}\cdot\min\left\{|X|,|Y|\right\}
. Once we delete the extra vertex
v_{0}
(if it exists), we obtain a cut
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
in the original graph
G^{\prime}
, with
|X^{\prime}|,|Y^{\prime}|\geq 2\rho
and
|E_{G^{\prime}}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})|\leq\frac{2\varphi}{c}\cdot\min\left\{|X^{\prime}|,|Y^{\prime}|\right\}
. Next, we apply Algorithm
MakeCanonical
from
Lemma

11.1
, to compute a canonical cut
(X^{\prime\prime},Y^{\prime\prime})
in
G^{\prime}
, such that:

|X^{\prime\prime}|\geq|X^{\prime}|/2\geq\rho
,
|Y^{\prime\prime}|\geq|Y^{\prime}|/2\geq\rho
, and:

\begin{split}|E_{\hat{G}}(X^{\prime\prime},Y^{\prime\prime})|&\leq O(|E_{\hat{G}}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})|)\\
&=O(|E_{G^{\prime}}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})|)\\
&\leq O\left(\frac{\varphi}{c}\cdot\min\left\{|X^{\prime}|,|Y^{\prime}|\right\}\right)\\
&\leq\varphi\cdot\min\left\{|X^{\prime\prime}|,|Y^{\prime\prime}|\right\},\end{split}

if
c
is sufficiently large. We then terminate the algorithm and return the partition
(X^{\prime\prime},Y^{\prime\prime})
of
V^{\prime}
. From the above discussion, it has all required properties.

Consider now the second case, where the algorithm from
Theorem

11.2
computes a matching
{M}^{\prime}_{i}\subseteq A^{\prime}_{i}\times B^{\prime}_{i}
with
|{M}^{\prime}_{i}|\geq|A^{\prime}_{i}|-z=|A^{\prime}_{i}|-8\rho
, such that there exists a set
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}_{i}=\left\{P(a,b)\mid(a,b)\in{M}^{\prime}_{i}\right\}
of paths in
G^{\prime}
, where for each pair
(a,b)\in{M}^{\prime}_{i}
, path
P(a,b)
connects
a
to
b
, and the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}_{i}
cause congestion at most
O\left(\frac{\log n}{\varphi}\right)
.
We let
A^{\prime\prime}_{i}\subseteq A^{\prime}_{i}
,
B^{\prime\prime}_{i}\subseteq B^{\prime}_{i}
be the sets of vertices that do not participate in the matching
{M}^{\prime}_{i}
, and we let
F_{i}
be an arbitrary perfect matching between these vertices. Lastly, we set
{M}_{i}={M}^{\prime}_{i}\cup F_{i}
. We view the matching
{M}_{i}
as the response of the matching player in the
Cut-Matching Game
. We add the edges of
M_{i}
to
H
, and continue to the next iteration.
Notice that
|F_{i}|\leq 8\rho
.

We perform the iterations as described above, until the algorithm from
Theorem

2.6
returns a subset
S\subseteq V(G^{\prime})
of at least
|V(G^{\prime})|/2
vertices, such that graph
H[S]
is
\varphi^{*}
-expander, for
\varphi^{*}\geq\Omega\left(\frac{1}{2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\cdot\Delta_{H}^{3}\cdot\log^{2}n}\right)\geq\Omega\left(\frac{1}{2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\cdot\log^{5}n}\right)
. Recall that
Theorem

3.6
guarantees that this must happen after at most
O(\log n)
iterations. We then perform one last iteration, whose index we denote by
q
.

We let
B_{q}=S
and
A_{q}=V(G)\setminus S
, and apply Algorithm
MatchOrCut
from
Theorem

11.2
to the sets
A_{q},B_{q}
of vertices, a sparsity parameter
\varphi^{\prime}=\varphi/c
and parameter
z=8\rho
. As before, we consider two cases. The first case happens if the algorithm returns a cut
(X,Y)
in
G
, with
|X|,|Y|\geq z/2\geq 4\rho
and
|E_{G^{\prime}}(X,Y)|\leq\varphi^{\prime}\cdot\min\left\{|X|,|Y|\right\}
. In this case, we compute a partition
(X^{\prime\prime},Y^{\prime\prime})
of
V(G^{\prime})\setminus\left\{v_{0}\right\}
exactly as before, so that both
X^{\prime\prime},Y^{\prime\prime}
are canonical sets of vertices of cardinality at least
\rho
each, and
|E_{\hat{G}}(X^{\prime\prime},Y^{\prime\prime})|\leq\varphi\cdot\min\left\{|X^{\prime\prime}|,|Y^{\prime\prime}|\right\}
. We return the cut
(X^{\prime\prime},Y^{\prime\prime})
and terminate the algorithm.
In the second case, the algorithm from
Theorem

11.2
computes a matching
{M}^{\prime}_{q}\subseteq A^{\prime}_{q}\times B^{\prime}_{q}
with
|{M}^{\prime}_{q}|\geq|A_{q}|-z=|A_{q}|-8\rho
, such that there exists a set
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}_{q}=\left\{P(a,b)\mid(a,b)\in{M}^{\prime}_{q}\right\}
of paths in
G^{\prime}
, where for each pair
(a,b)\in{M}^{\prime}_{q}
, path
P(a,b)
connects
a
to
b
, and the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}_{q}
cause congestion at most
O\left(\frac{\log n}{\varphi}\right)
. As before, we let
A^{\prime}_{q}\subseteq A_{q}
,
B^{\prime}_{q}\subseteq B_{q}
be the sets of vertices that do not participate in the matching
{M}^{\prime}_{q}
, and we let
F_{q}
be an arbitrary matching that connects every vertex of
A^{\prime}_{q}
to a distinct vertex of
B^{\prime}_{q}
(such a matching must exist since
|A_{q}|\leq|B_{q}|
). We then set
{M}_{q}={M}^{\prime}_{q}\cup{M}^{\prime\prime}_{q}
, and we add the edges of
{M}_{q}
to graph
H
.

From now on we assume that the algorithm never terminated with a partition
(X^{\prime\prime},Y^{\prime\prime})
of
V(G^{\prime})\setminus\left\{v_{0}\right\}
, where both
X^{\prime\prime},Y^{\prime\prime}
are canonical sets of vertices of cardinality at least
\rho
each, and
|E_{\hat{G}}(X^{\prime\prime},Y^{\prime\prime})|\leq\varphi\cdot\min\left\{|X^{\prime\prime}|,|Y^{\prime\prime}|\right\}
. Note that, from
3.2
, the final graph
H
is a
\varphi^{*}/2
-expander, for
\varphi^{*}\geq\Omega\left(\frac{1}{2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\cdot\log^{5}n}\right)
.
Let
F=\bigcup_{i}F_{i}
. Since, for all
i
,
|F_{i}|\leq 8\rho
, and since, from
Theorem

3.6
, the number of iterations is bounded by
O(\log n)
, we get that
|F|\leq O(\rho\log n)
. Lastly, consider the set
{\mathcal{P}}=\bigcup_{i}{\mathcal{P}}_{i}
of paths in graph
G^{\prime}
. It is immediately to verify that the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}
embed graph
H\setminus F
into
G^{\prime}
. Since every set
{\mathcal{P}}_{i}
of paths causes congestion at most
O\left(\frac{\log n}{\varphi}\right)
, the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}
cause congestion at most
O\left(\frac{\log^{2}n}{\varphi}\right)
in
G^{\prime}
.

One remaining subtlety is that graph
H
, as well as current graph
G^{\prime}
may contain the extra vertex
v_{0}
, that needs to be removed from both graphs. Recall that the degree of
v_{0}
in graph
H
is at most
O(\log n)
. Let
u_{1},\ldots,u_{r}
denote the neighbor vertices of
v_{0}
in
H
. Let
H^{\prime}
be obtained from graph
H
by deleting vertex
v_{0}
from it, and adding, for every pair
u_{j},u_{j^{\prime}}
of neighbor vertices of
v_{0}
, and edge
(u_{j},u_{j^{\prime}})
connecting them. Each such new edge is added to the set
F
of fake edges. It is easy to verify that
H^{\prime}
remains a
\varphi^{*}/2
-expander. Since
\rho\geq\log n
, while the degree of
v_{0}
in
H
is at most
O(\log n)
,
|F|\leq O(\rho\log n)
continues to hold, and all vertex degrees in
H^{\prime}
are at most
O(\log n)
. Since vertex
v_{0}
has degree
1
in
G^{\prime}
, we can assume that it does not lie on any path in
\left\{P(e)\mid e\in E(H^{\prime})\setminus F\right\}
, and so
v_{0}
can be safely deleted from
G^{\prime}
as well. The output of the algorithm in this case is graph
H^{\prime}
and set
F
of its edges.

Lastly, we bound the running time of the algorithm. The algorithm consists of
O(\log n)
iterations. Every iteration employs the algorithm from

Theorem

2.6
, whose running time is
O\left(|E(H)|^{1+O(\epsilon)}\cdot\Delta_{H}^{7}\right)\leq O\left(n^{1+O(\epsilon)}\right)
, since
\Delta_{H}\leq O(\log n)
, and
\log^{8}n<n^{4\epsilon}
(since, as we have observed,
n^{2\epsilon}\geq m^{\epsilon}>\log^{4}n
).
Additionally, in every iteration we use Algorithm
MatchOrCut
from
Theorem

11.2
, whose running time is
O\left(m^{1+o(1)}\right)
, and Algorithm
MakeCanonical
from
Lemma

11.1
, whose running time is
O(m)
. Therefore, the total running time is
O\left(m^{1+O(\epsilon)+o(1)}\right)
.
